On Sárközy’s theorem for shifted primes Thanks: The author is supported by a Simons Investigator grant and is grateful to the Simons Foundation for their continued support.
Ben Green

Abstract
. Suppose that A ⊂ { 1 , … , N } A\subset\{1,\dots,N\} has no two elements differing by p − 1 p-1 , p p prime. Then | A | ≪ N 1 − c |A|\ll N^{1-c} .



Part I
Introduction

1.
Introduction

Let
\delta(N)
denote the relative density of the largest set
A\subset\{1,\dots,N\}
not containing two elements
a,a^{\prime}
differing by
p-1
,
p
a prime (that is to say,
\delta(N)=\frac{|A|}{N}
). A well-known 1978 result of Sárközy
[
31
]
is that
\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\delta(N)=0
, that is to say the set
\{p-1:p\;\mbox{prime}\}
is a
set of recurrence
. In fact, Sárközy’s work establishes a bound

\delta(N)\ll(\log\log N)^{-2-o(1)}.

This was subsequently improved by Lucier
[
16
]
, who proved that

\delta(N)\ll(\log\log N)^{-\omega(N)}

for some function
\omega(N)\rightarrow\infty
.
In 2008 Ruzsa and Sanders
[
30
]
made a substantial advance on the problem by establishing that

\delta(N)\ll e^{-c(\log N)^{1/4}}

for some
c>0
. The exponent of
\frac{1}{4}
here was recently improved to
\frac{1}{3}
by Wang
[
37
]
. This is the best bound currently in the literature.

Ruzsa and Sanders remark in their paper that, even assuming the Generalised Riemann Hypothesis (GRH), the best bound that seems to be available using their methods is
\delta(N)\ll e^{-c(\log N)^{1/2}}
. They repeat a 1982 question of Ruzsa
[
28
]
, asking whether a bound of the shape
\delta(N)\ll N^{-c}
can be proven under such an assumption. Our main result in this paper is a bound of this strength, without any unproven hypothesis.

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
A\subset\{1,\dots,N\}
be a set such that
A-A
contains no number of the form
p-1
,
p
a prime. Then
|A|\ll N^{1-c}
for some
c>0
.

We remark that essentially the same arguments would prove the analogous result with
p+1
replacing
p-1
, and that trivial examples show that no such result is possible for
p+a
with any fixed
a
other than
\pm 1
.

We do not give a value for
c
here, but using their explicit zero-density estimates Thorner and Zaman
[
36
]
have shown that
c=10^{-18}
is admissible.

If one assumes GRH, it is possible to establish Theorem
1.1
with any
c<\frac{1}{12}
. Moreover, the argument simplifies dramatically (it is less than one third the length of the unconditional one). The interested reader may find a writeup of this argument in
[
9
]
.

We will give an outline of our method for proving Theorem
1.1
in the next section. The key new development is that we eschew the density increment strategy common to all of the works cited above,
and instead study the so-called
van der Corput
property, which is strictly stronger than that of being a set of recurrence, directly.

Let us recall what the van der Corput property for the shifted primes is. For each
N
, set

\gamma(N):=\inf_{T\in\mathscr{T}_{N}}a_{0},

where
\mathscr{T}_{N}
denotes the collection of all cosine polynomials

T(x)=a_{0}+\sum_{p\leqslant N}a_{p-1}\cos(2\pi(p-1)x)

where
a_{i}\in\mathbf{R}
and which satisfy
T(0)=1
,
T(x)\geqslant 0
for all
x
. The van der Corput property is that
\gamma(N)\rightarrow 0
as
N\rightarrow\infty
. We note that the van der Corput property may be formulated in different ways, and the above formulation is due to Kamae and Mendès France
[
14
]
and Ruzsa
[
27
]
. Montgomery’s
Ten Lectures

[
19
, Chapter 2]
provides a good discussion.

We will show the following result.

Theorem 1.2
.

We have
\gamma(N)\ll N^{-c}
for some absolute constant
c>0
. That is, there is a cosine polynomial

T(x)=a_{0}+\sum_{p\leqslant N}a_{p-1}\cos(2\pi(p-1)x)

where
a_{i}\in\mathbf{R}
,
T(0)=1
,
T(x)\geqslant 0
for all
x
and
a_{0}\ll N^{-c}
.

One may of course define the quantities
\delta(N)
and
\gamma(N)
with respect to an arbitrary set, rather than just for the shifted primes. In this general setting, roughly speaking we have
\delta\lessapprox\gamma
. More precisely, it follows from
[
19
, Chapter 2, Lemma 1]
that one has
\delta(N)\leqslant(1+\frac{H}{N})\gamma(H)
for any
H
,
1\leqslant H\leqslant N
, so in particular
\delta(N)\leqslant 2\gamma(N)
. We will give a self-contained proof of this latter fact (for shifted primes, but the argument is general) in Section
2
. Bourgain
[
2
]
gave an example showing that inequalities in the opposite direction do not hold in general, that is to say being a van der Corput set is strictly stronger than being a recurrent set. See also
[
17
]
for related material.

We now revert to the specific setting of the shifted primes. Regarding previous results in the direction of Theorem
1.2
, Kamae and Mendès France showed that the shifted primes have the van der Corput property, but gave no explicit bounds. Currently, the best-known upper bound for
\gamma(N)
seems to be that of Slijepčević
[
32
]
, who showed that
\gamma(N)\leqslant(\log N)^{-1+o(1)}
.

Finally, we note that the best-known lower bound for
\delta(N)
is rather small, though not as small as one might naïvely guess. This is a result of Ruzsa
[
29
]
, who showed that

\delta(N)\geqslant\frac{1}{N}e^{(\log 2+o(1))\frac{\log N}{\log\log N}}.

(1.1)

Note that this is asymptotically smaller than
N^{-1+\varepsilon}
for any
\varepsilon>0
, and one would expect this to be the true behaviour of
\delta(N)
. In fact, (
1.1
) may be close to the true bound. I believe (and see
[
32
]
) that no better lower bound is known for
\gamma(N)
.

Proving upper bounds of this strength is surely hopeless. Indeed, if one could show that
\delta(N)<N^{-1/2-c}
for some
c>0
then, taking
A
to be the multiples of
q
up to
q^{2-c^{\prime}}
, one sees that there is a prime
p\leqslant q^{2-c^{\prime}}
congruent to
1(\operatorname{mod}\,q)
. Such a bound is beyond what is currently known, even on GRH.

Acknowledgements.
The initial impetus for thinking about the van der Corput property came after the author became aware that the Lovász
\theta
-function can be used to show that any set
A\subset\mathbf{Z}/q\mathbf{Z}
of size
>\sqrt{q}
contains two elements differing by a square
(\operatorname{mod}\,q)
, where
q
is any product of distinct primes
1(\operatorname{mod}\,4)
. This connection was also observed by Naslund
[
21
]
, who says it was known to Ruzsa (unpublished). I thank Zachary Chase for interesting conversations on this topic and for presenting Lovász’s paper to me in an easily digestible form which made the connection to the van der Corput property clear. I would also like to thank Ofir Gorodetsky, Lasse Grimmelt, Emmanuel Kowalski, Valeriya Kovaleva, James Maynard, Eric Naslund, Joni Teräväinen and Jesse Thorner for various helpful comments and correspondence. Finally, I am very grateful to the two anonymous referees for a careful reading of the paper.

Notation.
In this section we introduce notation which is
global
to the paper, most of which is standard. There will be many further pieces of notation of a more local nature which we will introduce later on.

Our use of asymptotic notation such as
O()
,
o()
,
\ll
and
\gg
is standard in analytic number theory. Sometimes we will add subscripts to this notation; thus, for example,
O_{B}(1)
means a constant depending only on some parameter
B
. We will use the rougher notation
X\lessapprox Y
to mean
X\ll N^{o(1)}Y
, where here
N
is a global parameter throughout the paper (we are interested in subsets of
\{1,\dots,N\}
). We write
X=\tilde{O}(Y)
to mean the same thing. Often, but not always, the
N^{o(1)}
term will be just a fixed power of
\log N
. We will remind the reader of this notation when we first use it.

We will use the following standard arithmetic functions: the Möbius function
\mu
, Euler’s totient function
\phi
, the von Mangoldt function
\Lambda
, and its restriction to primes
\Lambda^{\prime}
(that is, the proper prime powers
p^{i}
,
i\geqslant 2
, are removed from the support). Given positive integers
a,b
we write
(a,b)
for their gcd and
[a,b]
for their lcm.

A slightly unusual notation we will use is to write
\operatorname{denom}(x)
for the denominator of the rational number
x
when written in lowest terms, or in other words the least positive integer
q
such that
qx\in\mathbf{Z}
. The quantity
\operatorname{denom}(x)
is well-defined for
x\in\mathbf{Q}/\mathbf{Z}
, which is the context in which we will use it.

Given
\theta\in\mathbf{R}
, we write
e(\theta)=e^{2\pi i\theta}
and
\|\theta\|
for the distance from
\theta
to the nearest integer. There is no other use for
\|\cdot\|
in the paper, so we do not need a more pedantic notation such as
\|\cdot\|_{\mathbf{R}/\mathbf{Z}}
.

If
q
is a positive integer, we write
c_{q}(n):=\sum_{a\in(\mathbf{Z}/q\mathbf{Z})^{*}}e(\frac{an}{q})
, the Ramanujan sum.

If
p
is a prime and
n
is a non-zero integer, we write
v_{p}(n)
to be the power of
p
dividing
n
, and extend this to a function
v_{p}:\mathbf{Q}\setminus\{0\}\rightarrow\mathbf{Z}
in the usual way, defining
v_{p}(\frac{a}{b}):=v_{p}(a)-v_{p}(b)
. We will, by a slight abuse of notation, define
v_{p}
on
(\mathbf{Z}/p^{n}\mathbf{Z})\setminus\{0\}
by taking
v_{p}(x)=v_{p}(\overline{x})
for any
\overline{x}\in\mathbf{Z}
projecting to
x
under the natural map.

In several later sections of the paper we make considerable use of the notation
F^{+}(n):=F(n+1)
,
F^{-}(n):=F(n-1)
. Whilst I acknowledge that this is a somewhat unattractive and nonstandard piece of notation, it is essential in order to keep expressions manageable later on.

Finally, in a number of sections we will see the constant
M
, whose definition may be found at (
11.3
) and which takes the value
M=7\cdot 3^{6}\cdot 2^{22993}
. The precise value of this constant is not especially important provided that it is large enough, but we choose to fix a value for definiteness.

2.
Non-negative trigonometric polynomials

Rather than work with the van der Corput property as defined in the introduction directly, we instead deduce bounds on
\delta(N)
and
\gamma(N)
from the following proposition, which encodes a property easily seen to be equivalent to the van der Corput property.

Proposition 2.1
.

Suppose that there is a function
\Psi:\mathbf{Z}\rightarrow\mathbf{R}
and
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}>0
with the following properties:

(1)

\Psi(n)
is supported on the set
\{p-1:\mbox{$p$ prime}\}
;

(2)

\sum_{n=1}^{N}\Psi(n)\cos(2\pi n\theta)\geqslant-\delta_{1}N
, for all
\theta\in\mathbf{R}/\mathbf{Z}
;

(3)

\sum_{n=1}^{N}\Psi(n)\geqslant\delta_{2}N
.

Then
\delta(N)\leqslant\frac{2\delta_{1}}{\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}}
and
\gamma(N)\leqslant\frac{\delta_{1}}{\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}}
. Conversely, there are
\delta_{1},\delta_{2}>0
and a function
\Psi
satisfying
(1)
,
(2)
and
(3)
above and such that
\gamma(N)=\frac{\delta_{1}}{\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}}
.

Remarks.
An immediate corollary of this is that
\delta(N)\leqslant 2\gamma(N)
.

We remark that no specific properties of the set
\{p-1:\mbox{$p$ prime}\}
are used in this section; the arguments and conclusions apply to recurrence and van der Corput properties of general sets.

Proof of Proposition
2.1
.

We begin with the first two statements, the bounds on
\delta(N)
and
\gamma(N)
. We will use Fourier analysis
(\operatorname{mod}\,2N)
. Suppose that
A\subset\{1,\dots,N\}
does not contain any elements differing by
p-1
, where
p\leqslant N
is a prime. Then it does not contain any elements differing modulo
2N
by
p-1
,
p\leqslant N
a prime.

Now we have

\displaystyle(\delta_{1}

\displaystyle+\delta_{2})N|A|^{2}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{r\in\mathbf{Z}/2N\mathbf{Z}}\bigg|\sum_{n=1}^{N}1_{A}(n)e\big(\frac{rn}{2N}\big)\bigg|^{2}\bigg(\delta_{1}N+\Re\sum_{x=1}^{N}\Psi(x)e\big(\frac{rx}{2N}\big)\bigg),

(2.1)

since the term with
r=0
is
\geqslant(\delta_{1}+\delta_{2})|A|^{2}N
by (3) and all other terms are non-negative by (2). On the other hand, we have

\sum_{r\in\mathbf{Z}/2N\mathbf{Z}}\bigg|\sum_{n=1}^{N}1_{A}(n)e\big(\frac{rn}{2N}\big)\bigg|^{2}=2N\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{m\equiv n(\mbox{\scriptsize mod}\,2N)}\!\!\!\!\!1_{A}(m)1_{A}(n)=2N|A|,

(2.2)

whilst

\displaystyle\sum_{r\in\mathbf{Z}/2N\mathbf{Z}}

\displaystyle\bigg|\sum_{n=1}^{N}1_{A}(n)e\big(\frac{rn}{2N}\big)\bigg|^{2}\sum_{x=1}^{N}\Psi(x)e\big(\frac{rx}{2N}\big)

\displaystyle=2N\sum_{m=1}^{N}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sum_{x=1}^{N}1_{m-n+x\equiv 0(\mbox{\scriptsize mod}\,2N)}1_{A}(m)1_{A}(n)\Psi(x)=0,

because
\Psi
is supported on
p-1
,
p
prime, and
A
does not contain two elements differing by such a number
(\operatorname{mod}\,2N)
.

Taking real parts and combining this with (
2.1
), (
2.2
) gives
(\delta_{1}+\delta_{2})|A|^{2}N\leqslant 2\delta_{1}N^{2}|A|
, from which we obtain
|A|\leqslant\frac{2\delta_{1}}{\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}}N
, as required.

For the van der Corput property, consider

T(x):=\big(\delta_{1}N+\sum_{n=1}^{N}\Psi(n)\big)^{-1}\big(\delta_{1}N+\sum_{n=1}^{N}\Psi(n)\cos(2\pi nx)\big).

It is clear that
T(0)=1
,
T(x)\geqslant 0
for all
x
and finally

a_{0}=\frac{\delta_{1}N}{\delta_{1}N+\sum_{n=1}^{N}\Psi(n)}\leqslant\frac{\delta_{1}}{\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}},

which shows that indeed
\gamma(N)\leqslant\frac{\delta_{1}}{\delta_{1}+\delta_{2}}
.

Finally we turn to the converse statement. Suppose we have a cosine polynomial
T(x)=a_{0}+\sum_{p\leqslant N}a_{p-1}\cos(2\pi(p-1)x)
where
a_{i}\in\mathbf{R}
,
T(0)=1
,
T(x)\geqslant 0
for all
x
and
a_{0}=\gamma(N)
. Define
\Psi(n)
to be
a_{p-1}
if
n=p-1
,
p\leqslant N
a prime, and
0
otherwise. Then property (1) is satisfied. Since
T(x)=a_{0}+\sum_{n\leqslant N}\Psi(n)\cos(2\pi nx)
, the assumption that
T(x)\geqslant 0
implies (2), with
\delta_{1}:=\frac{a_{0}}{N}
. The assumption that
T(0)=1
implies (3), with
\delta_{2}:=\frac{1-a_{0}}{N}
. The claim follows.
∎

From this, we see that Theorem
1.1
and Theorem
1.2
are both consequences of the following result (which is in fact equivalent to Theorem
1.2
).

Theorem 2.2
.

There are absolute constants
0<\kappa_{2}<\kappa_{1}
and a function
\Psi:\mathbf{Z}\rightarrow\mathbf{R}
with the following properties:

(1)

\Psi(n)
is supported on the set
\{p-1:\mbox{$p$ prime, $p\leqslant N$}\}
;

(2)

\sum_{n=1}^{N}\Psi(n)\cos(2\pi\theta n)\geqslant-N^{1-\kappa_{1}}
, for all
\theta\in\mathbf{R}/\mathbf{Z}
;

(3)

\sum_{n=1}^{N}\Psi(n)\geqslant N^{1-\kappa_{2}}
.

The proof of this result occupies the rest of the paper.

3.
Sketch of the construction and plan of the paper

3.1.
Sketch of the construction

The main business of the paper is the construction of the function
\Psi
in Proposition
2.1
.

The construction is complicated, so we begin by explaining roughly where it comes from. The starting point is to observe that

\Psi_{0}(n):=\Lambda^{\prime}(n+1)\Lambda^{\prime}(n-1)\tau(n)^{2}\big(1-\frac{|n|}{N}\big)_{+}

(3.1)

ought to have something like the desired properties, where
\Lambda^{\prime}
is the von Mangoldt function restricted to primes,
\tau
is the divisor function, and
x_{+}
denotes
\max(x,0)
. To see why, consider how one would expect this function to behave on progressions
n\equiv a(\operatorname{mod}\,p)
, where
p
is a prime of moderate size. The
\Lambda^{\prime}(n+1)
term will be uniformly distributed across
a\not\equiv-1(\operatorname{mod}\,p)
, whilst the
\Lambda^{\prime}(n-1)
term will be uniformly distributed where
a\not\equiv 1(\operatorname{mod}\,p)
. Thus one expects, assuming that
\Lambda^{\prime}(n+1)
and
\Lambda^{\prime}(n-1)
behave suitably independently, that
\Lambda^{\prime}(n-1)\Lambda^{\prime}(n+1)
is uniformly distributed where
a\not\equiv\pm 1(\operatorname{mod}\,p)
. The symmetry about 0 here guarantees that the exponential sum of
\Lambda^{\prime}(n+1)\Lambda^{\prime}(n-1)
at points
\frac{r}{p}
ought to be essentially real-valued. The divisor function
\tau(n)
has roughly twice the weight on
a\equiv 0(\operatorname{mod}\,p)
as it does on each of the other values of
a
(for each divisor
d
of
n/p
, we get both
d
and
pd
as divisors of
n
). Very roughly, this leads one to expect that
\Psi_{0}(n)
has total weight proportional to
\nu(a)
on
n\equiv a(\operatorname{mod}\,p)
, where
\nu(0)=4
,
\nu(\pm 1)=0
and
\nu(a)=1
for all other
a
. This weight has exponential sum at
\frac{r}{p}
which is both real
and
positive, which is a very helpful step towards proving (2). Moreover, by considering the multiplicative behaviour of the functions concerned, one can hope for a similar statement at points
\frac{b}{q}
,
q
squarefree. Finally, the tent function
(1-\frac{|n|}{N})_{+}
has non-negative Fourier transform (the Fejér kernel) and so one might expect
\Psi_{0}
to satisfy something like property (2).

Unfortunately, we cannot make any aspect of the preceding discussion rigorous. Indeed, the function
\Lambda^{\prime}(n+1)\Lambda^{\prime}(n-1)
may, for all we know, be essentially zero (the negation of this is the twin prime conjecture) and so certainly understanding the asymptotics of its exponential sum is hopeless.

Our actual construction of
\Psi
is inspired by (
3.1
) but includes various truncated and modified versions of the arithmetic functions involved as well as some extra terms. Roughly, we will construct it as a product

\Psi(n):=\Lambda^{\prime}(n+1)\Lambda_{Q}(n-1)H_{Q}(n)D_{Q^{\prime}}(n)E(n)w(n).

(3.2)

Here,
Q,Q^{\prime}
are certain parameters to be specified later, with
Q^{\prime}<Q
. Let us give a very brief overview of what each of these terms are and their purpose.

The
\Lambda_{Q}
term is a kind of Fourier-truncation of the von Mangoldt function given by

\Lambda_{Q}(n):=\sum_{q\leqslant Q}\frac{\mu(q)}{\phi(q)}c_{q}(n),

(3.3)

where

c_{q}(n):=\sum_{a\in(\mathbf{Z}/q\mathbf{Z})^{*}}e(\frac{an}{q})
is a Ramanujan sum. This function models the expected distribution of
\Lambda
on progressions but is much easier to analyse than
\Lambda
itself. It was used by Heath-Brown
[
11
]
to give an alternative proof of Vinogradov’s three primes theorem.

The factor
H_{Q}
behaves (somewhat) like a Fourier-truncated truncated version of
\tau^{2}
. It is defined as

H_{Q}(n):=\sum_{q\leqslant Q}\eta(q)c_{q}(n)\quad\mbox{where}\quad\eta(q):=\mu^{2}(q)\prod_{p|q}\frac{3}{p+3}.

(3.4)

Thus one should think of
\Lambda^{\prime}(n+1)\Lambda_{Q}(n-1)H_{Q}(n)
as a kind of Fourier-truncated variant of the function
\Lambda(n+1)\Lambda(n-1)\tau(n)^{2}
discussed at the beginning of the section. We discussed, on a heuristic level, the distribution of this latter function in progressions of small modulus (prime, in the discussion, but we need the general case), and a similar heuristic discussion applies to
\Lambda^{\prime}(n+1)\Lambda_{Q}(n-1)H_{Q}(n)
. This still assumes that
\Lambda
is well-distributed in progression of small modulus, where “small” means up to a power of
N
. Unfortunately, nothing like this is known to be true: possible zeros near to
\Re s=1
of Dirichlet
L
-functions
L(s,\chi)
can significantly skew the distribution.

To account for these irregularities we introduce the term
D_{Q^{\prime}}
, which we call a
damping term
. This term has the form

D_{Q^{\prime}}(n)=\sum_{r\leqslant Q^{\prime}}\sum_{b\in\mathbf{Z}/r^{3}\mathbf{Z}}\alpha_{b,r}r^{\frac{5}{6}}1_{r|n}e\big(\frac{bn}{r^{3}}\big),

where the
\alpha_{b,r}
are non-negative real numbers summing to
1
. (There is some scope for varying the choice of the numbers
\frac{5}{6}
and
3
, but it is crucial that
\frac{5}{6}<1
.) Its role is essentially to ensure that the irregularities in distribution just discussed still lead to non-negative Fourier coefficients.

The construction of
D_{Q^{\prime}}(n)
is rather complicated. It is defined in Section
13
as an infinite series (
13.7
), but this definition depends on material developed in several earlier sections. Roughly, the main contributions to the
\alpha_{b,r}
come from characters
\chi
which have zeros particularly close to
\Re s=1
. The need for the twists by
e(\frac{bn}{r^{3}})
arises because of the properties of Dirichlet characters to prime power moduli with large exponent, in particular the fact that these can behave like additive characters on large subgroups.

The function
E(n)
is either the constant function
1
or an indicator
1_{q_{1}|n}
. The latter case arises, essentially, when there is an exceptional character
\chi_{1}
of conductor
q_{1}
, that is to say a character such that
L(s,\chi)
has a zero very close to
s=1
.

Finally,
w(n)
is an “archimedean” factor. The tent function
w(n)=(1-\frac{|n|}{N})_{+}
turns out not to work, essentially because one needs the exponential sum of
n^{\rho-1}w(n)
to be pointwise dominated by that of
w(n)
, for
\rho
a possible zero of some
L(s,\chi)
. This is not the case for the tent function, since the exponential sum of
w(n)
in that case (the Fejér kernel) has zeros. We are instead forced to make a much more elaborate construction using properties of the function
W(x)=x^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-x}
, whose Fourier transform has a relatively slow and controlled decay.

3.2.
Plan of the paper

The main business of the paper is to properly define
\Psi
as given by (
3.2
) and to show that it satisfies the conclusions of Theorem
2.2
. This is a lengthy task.

Part
II
of the paper may be read independently of the rest of the paper and may plausibly be useful elsewhere. In this section we construct another approximant
\Lambda_{\sharp,Q,\sigma_{0}}
to
\Lambda
. Unlike
\Lambda_{Q}
, this approximant sees possible nontrivial zeros with
\Re\rho\geqslant 1-\sigma_{0}
up to height
Q
of all Dirichlet
L
-functions
L(s,\chi)
with
\chi
of conductor
\leqslant Q
. Including this information about zeros makes
\Lambda_{\sharp,Q,\sigma_{0}}
a more complicated object than
\Lambda_{Q}
, but it allows us to rigorously show that exponential sums
\sum_{n\leqslant N}(\Lambda(n)-\Lambda_{\sharp,Q,\sigma_{0}}(n))e(n\theta)
enjoy a
power saving

\lessapprox NQ^{-\frac{1}{6}}
over the trivial bound of
N
, uniformly in
\theta
. This allows us to reduce matters to the analysis of, instead of
\Psi
, the modification
\Psi^{\prime}
in which
\Lambda(n+1)
is replaced by
\Lambda_{\sharp,Q,\sigma_{0}}(n+1)
, for suitable
Q,\sigma_{0}
.

Part
III
of the paper assembles various preliminaries for the main argument. In Section
7
, we describe some zero-density estimates and their consequences. The material here is similar to what one would find in a proof of Linnik’s theorem on the least prime in a progression, which is not surprising since Theorem
1.1
is easily seen to imply that the progression
1(\operatorname{mod}\,q)
contains a prime of size
q^{O(1)}
. What we need here is a little stronger than what is required for Linnik’s theorem, where the modulus is fixed. Our main reference is Bombieri’s book
[
1
]
.

Section
8
discusses periodic functions on
\mathbf{Z}
, their rational Fourier expansions, and the notion of positivity. It also introduces some of the key examples and functions in the paper such as
H_{Q}
as described above.

Section
9
and the related Section
10
are rather long and technical, though crucial to the paper. They are concerned with passing between truncated series
\sum_{q\leqslant Q}\alpha(q)c_{q}(n)
, which are good for Fourier analysis, and untruncated series such as
\sum_{q|Q!}\alpha(q)c_{q}(n)
, which are much easier to understand combinatorially. Section 9 concerns triple products of such series and their shifts, and in Section 10 characters are introduced as well. Despite the technical nature of these sections, the statement of the main result of Section 9, Proposition
9.1
, is self-contained and potentially applicable in other contexts.

Section
11
develops material necessary for the construction of the damping term
D_{Q^{\prime}}
. A crucial input here is the Postnikov character formula which, due to the lack of a reference suitable for our purposes, is developed from first principles in Appendix
A
.

Section
12
constructs the archimedean weight
w(n)
, modelled on
x^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-x}
. The main result here, Proposition
12.1
, is self-contained and is also potentially of some use elsewhere.

Finally, we come to Part
IV
, where the function
\Psi
is rigorously constructed and shown to have the desired properties. The construction of the damping function
D_{Q^{\prime}}
is probably the most conceptually interesting part of this analysis. The fully detailed definition of
\Psi
is given in Definition
15.1
, and finally in the rest of that section it is shown that
\Psi
satisfies Theorem
2.2
.

Some comments for the reader.
I have endeavoured to make key intermediate results self-contained where possible, so that it is possible to understand the basic structure of the argument without reading all the proofs. There are a very large number of hyperlinks in the paper to facilitate recall of key definitions and intermediate results. Consequently, I recommend that anyone interested in reading the paper use a suitable PDF viewer rather than the printed version.

Part II
An approximant for the primes

The objective of this part of the paper is define certain approximants
\Lambda_{\sharp}
to the von Mangoldt function
\Lambda
which are very close to it in Fourier space. The definition (which requires a little setting up) may be found in Definition
4.4
, and the key result is Proposition
4.6
. Recall that
N
is a large integer, a global parameter in the paper, and that
X\lessapprox Y
means that
X\ll N^{o(1)}Y
.

4.
Definition of the approximant

We adopt the following fairly standard convention in analytic number theory:
\rho
denotes a nontrivial zero of some
L
-function, and we write
\rho=\beta+i\gamma
; somewhat less standardly, we always write
\sigma:=1-\beta
, thus
\Re\rho=1-\sigma
.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
Q
be a positive real parameter, and let
\sigma_{0}>0
. Write
\Xi_{Q}(\sigma_{0})\subset\mathbf{C}
for the multiset consisting of all
\rho=\beta+i\gamma=1-\sigma+i\gamma
with
\sigma\leqslant\sigma_{0}
and
|\gamma|\leqslant Q
which are zeros of some Dirichlet
L
-function
L(s,\chi)
with conductor at most
Q
.

Remarks.
Later on we will fix
\sigma_{0}=\frac{1}{48}
, a convenient but not optimal choice which works for all our arguments.

When we say that
\Xi_{Q}(\sigma_{0})
is a multiset, we mean the following. If
\rho
occurs as a zero of multiplicity
r_{i}
for primitive Dirichlet characters
\chi_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,m
, then
\rho
should appear in
\Xi_{Q}(\sigma_{0})
with multiplicity
r_{1}+\dots+r_{m}
. Conjecturally, every
\rho
is a simple zero of a unique
L(s,\chi)
(and so
\Xi_{Q}(\sigma_{0})
is simply a set) but this is not known. In fact
\Xi_{Q}(\sigma_{0})
is conjecturally
empty
if
\sigma_{0}<\frac{1}{2}
. If
\rho\in\Xi_{Q}(\sigma_{0})
, we write
\chi_{\rho}
for the primitive Dirichlet character associated to it.

Suppose
\psi
is a Dirichlet character (not necessarily primitive) to modulus
r
. Recall that the
Gauss sum

\tau(\psi)
is defined by

\tau(\psi):=\sum_{b\in(\mathbf{Z}/r\mathbf{Z})^{*}}\psi(b)e\big(\frac{b}{r}\big).

The following three definitions are important ones in the paper.

Definition 4.2
.

Let
\chi
be a primitive Dirichlet character to modulus
q
. Let
r
be a positive integer and let
b\in(\mathbf{Z}/r\mathbf{Z})^{*}
. Then if
q\mid r
we define

c_{\chi}(b,r):=\frac{1}{\phi(r)}\chi(b)\mu\big(\frac{r}{q}\big)\overline{\chi\big(\frac{r}{q}\big)}\overline{\tau(\chi)},

(4.1)

and set
c_{\chi}(b,r)=0
if
q\nmid r
.

Definition 4.3
.

Let
\chi
be a primitive Dirichlet character to modulus
q
. Then we define

F_{\chi,Q}(n):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q|r\\
\frac{r}{q}\leqslant Q\end{subarray}}\sum_{b\in(\mathbf{Z}/r\mathbf{Z})^{*}}c_{\chi}(b,r)e\big(\frac{bn}{r}\big),

(4.2)

where
c_{\chi}
is as given in (
4.1
).

Finally, we can define the approximants
\Lambda_{\sharp}
themselves.

Definition 4.4
.

If
n
is a positive integer, define

\Lambda_{\sharp,Q,\sigma_{0}}(n):=\Lambda_{Q}(n)-\sum_{\rho\in\Xi_{Q}(\sigma_{0})}n^{\rho-1}F_{\chi_{\rho},Q}(n).

(4.3)

Remarks.
As we progress with our analysis we will understand more about why these are relevant definitions. For instance, we will see in Lemma
6.2
that
F_{\chi,Q}
can be thought of as a kind of “Fourier-truncated” version of
\overline{\chi}
restricted to numbers with no prime factors
\leqslant Q
.

When
\chi=\chi_{0}
, the (primitive) principal character, we have
c_{\chi}(b,r)=\frac{\mu(r)}{\phi(r)}
and so

F_{\chi_{0},Q}(n)=\Lambda_{Q}(n),

(4.4)

where
\Lambda_{Q}
is the approximant (
3.3
).
Given this, it is possible and often convenient to write

\Lambda_{\sharp,Q,\sigma_{0}}(n)=\sum_{\rho\in\Xi_{Q}^{*}(\sigma_{0})}\varepsilon_{\rho}n^{\rho-1}F_{\chi_{\rho},Q}(n).

(4.5)

Here, we set
\Xi_{Q}^{*}(\sigma_{0})=\Xi_{Q}(\sigma_{0})\cup\{1\}
and we adopt the convention that
\chi_{1}
is the principal character (of which
1
is of course a pole, not a zero), and
\varepsilon_{\rho}=-1
or
1
according as
\rho
is a zero or a pole (i.e.
\rho=1
).

I am not aware of
\Lambda_{\sharp,Q,\sigma_{0}}
having appeared previously in the literature. Teräväinen
[
35
, Definition 5.2]
makes a vaguely related definition (but only using the zeros of
\zeta
), and there is some similarity with the work of Pintz
[
23
,
24
]
on the exceptional set for the Goldbach problem.

As a final remark, we note that on the assumption of GRH we have
\Lambda_{\sharp,Q,\sigma_{0}}=\Lambda_{Q}
for all
\sigma_{0}<\frac{1}{2}
, since
\Xi_{Q}(\sigma_{0})
is empty.

Lemma 4.5
.

\Lambda_{\sharp,Q,\sigma_{0}}
is real-valued.

Proof.

One may check using
\tau(\overline{\chi})=\chi(-1)\overline{\tau(\chi)}
that
c_{\overline{\chi}}(b,r)=\overline{c_{\chi}(-b,r)}
and thus that

F_{\overline{\chi},Q}=\overline{F_{\chi,Q}}.

(4.6)

Suppose that
\rho\in\Xi_{Q}(\sigma_{0})
, thus
L(\rho,\chi_{\rho})=0
. Then
L(\overline{\rho},\overline{\chi_{\rho}})=0
(note that
L(s,\chi)=\overline{L(\overline{s},\overline{\chi})}
, since both sides are meromorphic and they agree for
\Re(s)>1
). That is,
\overline{\rho}\in\Xi_{Q}(\sigma_{0})
and
\chi_{\overline{\rho}}=\overline{\chi_{\rho}}
.
It follows from this and (
4.6
) that
n^{\rho-1}F_{\chi_{\rho},Q}(n)=\overline{n^{\overline{\rho}-1}F_{\chi_{\overline{\rho}},Q}(n)}
, thus the summands in (
4.3
) come in conjugate pairs.
∎

Finally we state the key Fourier approximation property.

Proposition 4.6
.

Suppose that
\sigma_{0}\leqslant\frac{1}{9}
and that
Q\leqslant N^{\sigma_{0}}
. Then we have

\big|\sum_{n\leqslant N}(\Lambda(n)-\Lambda_{\sharp,Q,\sigma_{0}}(n))e(n\theta)\big|\lessapprox NQ^{-\frac{1}{6}}

and

\big|\sum_{n\leqslant N}(\Lambda^{\prime}(n)-\Lambda_{\sharp,Q,\sigma_{0}}(n))e(n\theta)\big|\lessapprox NQ^{-\frac{1}{6}}

uniformly for
\theta\in\mathbf{R}/\mathbf{Z}
.

Here, note that the second statement (which is the one we will need later on) follows easily from the first, since
\sum_{n\leqslant N}|\Lambda(n)-\Lambda^{\prime}(n)|\lessapprox N^{\frac{1}{2}}
.

5.
Proof of the Fourier approximation property

The business of this section is to prove Proposition
4.6
. The basic idea will be to consider the cases of
\theta
close to a rational with small denominator (the “major arcs”) and
\theta
not close to a rational with small denominator (the “minor arcs”) separately. In other words, we use the circle method. The heart of the argument begins in Subsection
5.3
below.

We will need various ingredients in the proof, which we assemble now before moving on to the main argument.

5.1.
Zero-density estimates

We have the following result, sometimes known as a grand log-free zero-density estimate.

Proposition 5.1
.

We have, uniformly for
\alpha\geqslant\frac{4}{5}
,

\#\{\rho\in\Xi_{Q}(\textstyle\frac{1}{5}\displaystyle):\Re\rho\geqslant\alpha\}\ll Q^{8(1-\alpha)}.

(5.1)

Here, zeros are counted with multiplicity.

This follows from
[
13
, Theorem 1]
(with any constant larger than
6
in place of
8
). That zeros are counted with multiplicity is not explicitly stated there (or indeed in many results of this type) but the proofs do give this. Any result of this general type (that is, a “log-free” zero-density estimate over all moduli) would be suitable for our purposes. Jutila (
ibid.
) describes some previous results of this type and remarks that the first such estimates were due to work of Fogels
[
5
]
and Gallagher
[
6
]
, and can be found explicitly in the latter paper.

Remark.
In Section
7
, we will need more refined estimates coming from the phenomenon of “exceptional zero repulsion”; see Proposition
7.5
.

A consequence of Proposition
5.1
is the following convergence result.

Lemma 5.2
.

Suppose that
Q\leqslant N^{\frac{1}{9}}
. Then
\sum_{\rho\in\Xi_{Q}(\frac{1}{5})}N^{\Re\rho-1}\ll 1
.

Proof.

For
k\geqslant 1
, write
\Xi^{(k)}_{Q}
for the multiset of all
\rho\in\Xi_{Q}(\frac{1}{5})
for which
k-1<(1-\Re\rho)\log Q\leqslant k
. By Proposition
5.1
,
|\Xi_{Q}^{(k)}|\ll e^{8k}
for all
k
. Summing over
k
, we obtain

\sum_{\rho\in\Xi_{Q}}N^{\Re\rho-1}\ll\sum_{k\geqslant 1}N^{-\frac{k-1}{\log Q}}e^{8k}\leqslant\sum_{k\geqslant 1}e^{-9k}e^{8k}\ll 1.

∎

5.2.
Exponential sum estimates

We will need the following estimate for exponential sums twisted by
n^{\rho-1}=n^{\beta}e^{i\gamma\log n}
. This is material of a standard type, but it seems hard to locate a usable reference in the literature.

Proposition 5.3
.

Suppose that
\rho=\beta+i\gamma
, where
\frac{1}{2}\leqslant\beta\leqslant 1
and
|\gamma|\leqslant N^{1/2}
. Then we have

\sum_{n\leqslant N}n^{\rho-1}e(\theta n)\ll N^{\beta}\min\big(1,(1+|\gamma|)N^{-1}\|\theta\|^{-1},|\gamma|^{-1/2}\big).

(5.2)

The proof will require the following two well-known estimates for exponential sums.

Lemma 5.4

(Kuzmin–Landau)
.

Let
I\subset\mathbf{R}
be a bounded interval. Let
f:I\rightarrow\mathbf{R}
be continuously differentiable such that
f^{\prime}
is monotonic and
\|f^{\prime}(t)\|\geqslant\lambda
on
I
. Then

\sum_{n\in I\cap\mathbf{Z}}e(f(n))\ll\lambda^{-1}.

Proof.

See
[
34
, Part I, Theorem 6.7]
or
[
8
, Theorem 2.1]
. Recall that here
\|\theta\|
is the distance to the nearest integer.∎

Lemma 5.5

(van der Corput)
.

Let
I\subset\mathbf{R}
be a bounded interval. Let
f:I\rightarrow\mathbf{R}
be twice continuously differentiable with
\lambda\leqslant|f^{\prime\prime}(x)|\leqslant\alpha\lambda
. Then

\sum_{n\in I\cap\mathbf{Z}}e(f(n))\ll\alpha|I|\lambda^{1/2}+\lambda^{-1/2}.

Proof.

See the comments after the proof of Theorem 6.7 in
[
34
, Part I]
or
[
8
, Theorem 2.2]
for the exact version we state here, or
[
12
, Corollary 8.13]
. ∎

Proof of Proposition
5.3
.

Since
e(\theta n)
is periodic, we may assume
0\leqslant|\theta|\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
throughout, and hence that
\|\theta\|=|\theta|
.
Set

S(\gamma,\theta,N):=\sum_{n\leqslant N}n^{i\gamma}e(\theta n)=\sum_{n\leqslant N}e\big(\frac{\gamma}{2\pi}\log n+\theta n\big).

From the identity

n^{\beta-1}=N^{\beta-1}-(\beta-1)\int^{N}_{n}x^{\beta-2}dx

we see that

\sum_{n\leqslant N}n^{\rho-1}e(\theta n)=N^{\beta-1}S(\gamma,\theta,N)-(\beta-1)\int^{N}_{1}x^{\beta-2}S(\gamma,\theta,x)dx.

(5.3)

Now (
5.2
) consists of three bounds. The first one, that the sum is
\ll N^{\beta}
, is essentially trivial:

\big|\sum_{n\leqslant N}n^{\rho-1}\big|\leqslant\sum_{n\leqslant N}|n^{\rho-1}|=\sum_{n\leqslant N}n^{\beta-1}\ll N^{\beta}.

(Note that the implied constant
is
uniform in
\beta
in the range
\frac{1}{2}\leqslant\beta\leqslant 1
.)

For the second estimate in (
5.2
), we apply the Kuzmin-Landau bound (Lemma
5.4
) with
f(t)=\frac{\gamma}{2\pi}\log t+\theta t
, we see that if
M:=\frac{|\gamma|}{|\theta|}
then

\sum_{M\leqslant n\leqslant x}n^{i\gamma}e(\theta n)\ll|\theta|^{-1}.

Estimating the contribution from
M\leqslant\frac{|\gamma|}{|\theta|}
trivially using
|n^{i\gamma}e(\theta n)|\leqslant 1
gives
S(\gamma,\theta,x)\ll(1+|\gamma|)|\theta|^{-1}
. Substituting into (
5.3
) gives the second estimate in (
5.2
).

For the third estimate in (
5.2
), suppose that
I\subset[2^{j},2^{j+1}]
is some interval. Apply the van der Corput bound (Lemma
5.5
) on this interval with
f(t)=\frac{\gamma}{2\pi}\log t+\theta t
. Since
f^{\prime\prime}(t)=-\frac{\gamma}{2\pi t^{2}}
we can take
\lambda=2^{-2j-2}|\frac{\gamma}{2\pi}|
,
\alpha=4
and obtain the estimate

\sum_{n\in I}n^{i\gamma}e(n\theta)\ll|\gamma|^{1/2}+2^{j}|\gamma|^{-1/2}.

Summing over
j
gives

S(\gamma,\theta,x)\ll|\gamma|^{1/2}\log N+N|\gamma|^{-1/2},

uniformly for
x\leqslant N
.
Substituting this into (
5.3
) gives

\sum_{n\leqslant N}n^{\rho-1}e(\theta n)\ll N^{\beta}|\gamma|^{-1/2}+|\gamma|^{1/2}N^{\beta-1}\log N,

which certainly implies the stated bound if
|\gamma|\leqslant N^{1/2}
(in fact the weaker condition
|\gamma|\leqslant\frac{N}{\log N}
would suffice).∎

5.3.
Minor arcs estimates

We now come to the heart of the proof of Proposition
4.6
. We look first at the minor arcs, obtaining estimates for those
\theta
not close to a rational with small denominator. Recall that the notation
X\lessapprox Y
means
|X|\leqslant N^{o(1)}Y
; in this section, the
N^{o(1)}
terms are just fixed powers of
\log N
, but this is not important for our purposes in this paper.

The following is our key minor arc estimate, which we phrase in the contrapositive.

Lemma 5.6
.

Suppose that
\sigma_{0}\leqslant\frac{1}{5}
and that
\max(Q,\frac{1}{\delta})\leqslant N^{\frac{1}{8}}
. Suppose that, for some
\theta\in\mathbf{R}/\mathbf{Z}
,

\big|\sum_{n\leqslant N}\Lambda_{\sharp,Q,\sigma_{0}}(n)e(n\theta)\big|\geqslant\delta N.

Then there is some integer
r
,
0<|r|\lessapprox\delta^{-3}
, such that
\|\theta r\|\lessapprox\delta^{-3}N^{-1}
.

Proof.

From the definition (
4.3
) of
\Lambda_{\sharp,Q,\sigma_{0}}
(or, more precisely, from (
4.5
)) it follows that

\sum_{\rho\in\Xi_{Q}^{*}(\sigma_{0})}\big|\sum_{n\leqslant N}n^{\rho-1}F_{\chi_{\rho},Q}(n)e(n\theta)\big|\geqslant\delta N.

Recall that we set
\Xi_{Q}^{*}(\sigma_{0})=\Xi_{Q}(\sigma_{0})\cup\{1\}
and we adopt the convention that
\chi_{1}
is the principal character. By Lemma
5.2
it follows that there is some
\rho\in\Xi_{Q}^{*}(\sigma_{0})
such that

\big|\sum_{n\leqslant N}n^{\rho-1}F_{\chi_{\rho},Q}(n)e(\theta n)\big|\gg\delta N^{\beta},

where
\beta=\Re\rho
. Fix this
\rho
and, for notational brevity, write
\chi=\chi_{\rho}
. As usual, write
\rho=\beta+i\gamma
, and let
q
be the conductor of
\chi
. Note that
q\leqslant Q
(or else
\rho
would not appear in
\Xi^{*}_{Q}(\sigma_{0})
) and also that
|\gamma|\leqslant Q
. Substituting in the definition (
4.2
) of
F_{\chi,Q}(n)
and using the triangle inequality, we obtain

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q|r\\
r/q\leqslant Q\end{subarray}}\sum_{b\in(\mathbf{Z}/r\mathbf{Z})^{*}}|c_{\chi}(b,r)|\bigg|\sum_{n\leqslant N}n^{\rho-1}e\big((\theta+\frac{b}{r})n\big)\bigg|\gg\delta N^{\beta}.

(5.4)

From the definition (
4.1
) of
c_{\chi}(b,r)
and the basic bound

|\tau(\chi)|^{2}=q.

(5.5)

for the Gauss sum of a primitive character
\chi
we have

|c_{\chi}(b,r)|\leqslant\frac{q^{1/2}}{\phi(r)}.

(5.6)

Also,
c_{\chi}(b,r)
is supported where
(q,r/q)=1
(and where
r/q
is squarefree). Therefore from (
5.4
) we have

q^{1/2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q|r\\
r/q\leqslant Q\\
(q,r/q)=1\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\phi(r)}\sum_{b\in(\mathbf{Z}/r\mathbf{Z})^{*}}\bigg|\sum_{n\leqslant N}n^{\rho-1}e\big((\theta+\frac{b}{r})n\big)\bigg|\gg\delta N^{\beta}.

(5.7)

Now we use Proposition
5.3
to get an upper bound on

S:=\sum_{b\in(\mathbf{Z}/r\mathbf{Z})^{*}}\bigg|\sum_{n\leqslant N}n^{\rho-1}e\big((\theta+\frac{b}{r})n\big)\bigg|.

For the (at most two) values of
b
with
\|\theta+\frac{b}{r}\|\leqslant\frac{1}{r}
, we use the first and third bounds in (
5.2
), to get a contribution of
\ll N^{\beta}\min(1,|\gamma|)^{-\frac{1}{2}}
. For all other values of
b
we instead use the second bound in (
5.2
), obtaining a contribution of

\ll N^{\beta-1}(1+|\gamma|)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in(\mathbf{Z}/r\mathbf{Z})^{*}\\
\|\theta+\frac{b}{r}\|\geqslant\frac{1}{r}\end{subarray}}\|\theta+\frac{b}{r}\|^{-1}\ll N^{\beta-1}(1+|\gamma|)r\log r,

uniformly in
\theta
. Now since
|\gamma|\leqslant Q
,
r\leqslant Q^{2}
and
Q\leqslant N^{\frac{1}{8}}
, this is dominated by the bound
N^{\beta}\min(1,|\gamma|^{-\frac{1}{2}})
, and so

S\ll N^{\beta}\min(1,|\gamma|^{-\frac{1}{2}}).

(5.8)

Since

q^{1/2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q|r\\
r/q\leqslant Q\\
(q,r/q)=1\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{\phi(r)}\leqslant\frac{q^{1/2}}{\phi(q)}\sum_{k\leqslant Q}\frac{1}{\phi(k)}\ll\frac{q^{1/2}}{\phi(q)}\log Q,

comparing with (
5.8
) and (
5.7
) gives

\frac{q^{1/2}\log Q}{\phi(q)}\min(1,|\gamma|^{-\frac{1}{2}})\gg\delta.

Using
\phi(q)\gtrapprox q
and
q\geqslant 1
, and considering the cases
|\gamma|\leqslant 1
and
|\gamma|\geqslant 1
separately, this gives

q^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1+|\gamma|)^{-1}\gtrapprox\delta^{2},

(5.9)

an inequality we will use shortly.

Returning to (
5.7
), and dropping the conditions
q|r
and
(q,r/q)=1
, we have

\sum_{r\leqslant Q^{2}}\frac{1}{\phi(r)}\sum_{b\in(\mathbf{Z}/r\mathbf{Z})^{*}}\bigg|\sum_{n\leqslant N}n^{\rho-1}e\big((\theta+\frac{b}{r})n\big)\bigg|\gg\delta N^{\beta}q^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

By the first and second estimates in (
5.2
), this implies

\displaystyle\sum_{r\leqslant Q^{2}}\frac{1}{\phi(r)}\sum_{b\in(\mathbf{Z}/r\mathbf{Z})^{*}}\min\big(N,\|\theta+\frac{b}{r}\|^{-1}\big)

\displaystyle\gg\delta Nq^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1+|\gamma|)^{-1}

\displaystyle\gtrapprox\delta^{3}N,

(5.10)

where the second step follows from (
5.9
).

By Dirichlet’s theorem, we may write

\theta=\frac{a}{d}+\eta,\quad\mbox{where}\quad d\leqslant 2Q^{2},\quad|\eta|\leqslant\frac{1}{2dQ^{2}},

with
\frac{a}{d}
in lowest terms.
If
\frac{a}{d}\neq-\frac{b}{r}
, this means that

\|\theta+\frac{b}{r}\|\geqslant\|\frac{a}{d}+\frac{b}{r}\|-\frac{1}{2dQ^{2}}\geqslant\frac{1}{dr}-\frac{1}{2dQ^{2}}\geqslant\frac{1}{2rd}.

Hence, if
r\neq d
then we we have

\sum_{b\in(\mathbf{Z}/r\mathbf{Z})^{*}}\|\theta+\frac{b}{r}\|^{-1}\ll dr+r\log r\ll Q^{4},

and so the contribution to the left-hand side of (
5.10
) from
r\neq d
will be bounded above by
Q^{4}\sum_{r\leqslant Q^{2}}\frac{1}{\phi(r)}\lessapprox Q^{4}
. By our choice of parameters, this is negligible compared to
\delta^{3}N
.
Thus the bulk of the contribution to the left-hand side of (
5.10
) comes from
r=d
, that is to say

\frac{1}{\phi(d)}\sum_{b\in(\mathbf{Z}/d\mathbf{Z})^{*}}\min\big(N,\|\theta+\frac{b}{d}\|^{-1}\big)\gtrapprox\delta^{3}N.

(5.11)

Now

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}b\in(\mathbf{Z}/d\mathbf{Z})^{*}\\
b\neq-a\end{subarray}}\|\theta+\frac{b}{d}\|^{-1}\ll d\log d\lessapprox Q^{2}.

Therefore the bulk of the contribution to the left-hand side of (
5.11
) comes from
b=-a
, and so

\frac{1}{\phi(d)}\min\big(N,\|\theta-\frac{a}{d}\|^{-1}\big)\gtrapprox\delta^{3}N.

This implies both
d\lessapprox\delta^{-3}
and
\|\theta d\|\lessapprox\delta^{-3}N^{-1}
, that is to say the two statements claimed.
This completes the proof.
∎

Combining Lemma
5.6
with standard minor arc estimates for the von Mangoldt function confirms Proposition
4.6
unless
\theta
is close to a rational with small denominator (that is, in the minor arc case), as the following corollary shows.

Corollary 5.7
.

Suppose that
\sigma_{0}<\frac{1}{5}
and that
\max(Q,\frac{1}{\delta})\leqslant N^{\frac{1}{8}}
. Suppose that, for some
\theta\in\mathbf{R}/\mathbf{Z}
,

\big|\sum_{n\leqslant N}(\Lambda(n)-\Lambda_{\sharp,Q,\sigma_{0}}(n))e(n\theta)\big|\geqslant\delta N.

Then there is some integer
r
,
0<|r|\lessapprox\delta^{-3}
, such that
\|\theta r\|\lessapprox\delta^{-3}N^{-1}
.

Proof.

One of
|\sum_{n\leqslant N}\Lambda(n)e(\theta n)|
and
|\sum_{n\leqslant N}\Lambda_{\sharp,Q,\sigma_{0}}(n)e(\theta n)|
is at least
\frac{\delta}{2}N
, by the triangle inequality. In the latter case, the conclusion follows immediately from Lemma
5.6
. Suppose, then, that we are in the former case, so

\big|\sum_{n\leqslant N}\Lambda(n)e(\theta n)\big|\geqslant\frac{\delta}{2}N.

(5.12)

In
[
4
, Chapter 25]
it is shown that if
|\theta-\frac{a}{q}|\leqslant q^{-2}
then

\sum_{n\leqslant N}\Lambda(n)e(\theta n)\ll\big(\frac{N}{q^{1/2}}+N^{4/5}+N^{1/2}q^{1/2}\big)\log^{4}N.

(5.13)

Let
X=c\delta^{2}N\log^{-8}N
for some
c>0
which we will specify shortly. By Dirichlet’s theorem, there is some
q\leqslant X
such that
|\theta-\frac{a}{q}|\leqslant\frac{1}{qX}\leqslant\frac{1}{q^{2}}
. By the assumption that
\delta\geqslant N^{-\frac{1}{8}}
(which is a comfortable one, but stronger than necessary) and that
N
is sufficiently large, the middle term in (
5.13
) is
\leqslant\delta N/6
. By the choice of
X
(if
c
is small enough) the last term in (
5.13
) is also
\leqslant\delta N/6
. It follows from these observations that if (
5.12
) holds then the first term in (
5.13
) is
\geqslant\delta N/6
, which implies that
q\lessapprox\delta^{-2}
.
Note also that
\|\theta q\|\leqslant X^{-1}\lessapprox\delta^{-2}N^{-1}
. Thus the conclusion of Corollary
5.7
holds in this case also (in fact with slightly superior exponents).
∎

5.4.
Major arc estimates

In this section, we handle the cases of Proposition
4.6
not covered by Corollary
5.7
. The main result is the following.

Lemma 5.8

(Major arcs)
.

Suppose that
\sigma_{0}\leqslant\frac{1}{9}
and that
Q\leqslant N^{\sigma_{0}}
.
Suppose that there is some positive integer
d\leqslant Q^{\frac{1}{2}}
such that
\|\theta d\|\leqslant Q^{\frac{1}{2}}N^{-1}
. Then

\sum_{n\leqslant N}(\Lambda(n)-\Lambda_{\sharp,Q,\sigma_{0}}(n))e(\theta n)\lessapprox NQ^{-\frac{1}{4}}.

(5.14)

Before proceeding to the proof, let us see how Corollary
5.7
and Lemma
5.8
together imply Proposition
4.6
.

Proof of Proposition
4.6
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
be arbitrary. Let
\delta=Q^{-\frac{1}{6}}N^{\varepsilon}
, and suppose that for some
\theta
we have

\big|\sum_{n\leqslant N}(\Lambda(n)-\Lambda_{\sharp,Q,\sigma_{0}}(n))e(n\theta)\big|\geqslant\delta N.

Then, by Corollary
5.7
, there is some integer
r
,
0<|r|\lessapprox\delta^{-3}
, such that
\|\theta r\|\lessapprox\delta^{-3}N^{-1}
. If
N
is big enough in terms of
\varepsilon
,
|r|\leqslant Q^{\frac{1}{2}}
and
\|\theta r\|\leqslant Q^{\frac{1}{2}}N^{-1}
, so we are in a position to apply Lemma
5.8
, and this tells us that
NQ^{-\frac{1}{4}}\gtrapprox\delta N
, which is a contradiction for
N
sufficiently large in terms of
\varepsilon
. Since
\varepsilon
was arbitrary, Proposition
4.6
follows.∎

To prove Lemma
5.8
, we will need some preliminaries on Dirichlet characters and on the coefficients
c_{\chi}
as defined in (
4.1
). In particular, we will see why these coefficients play a key role in the paper. Here and in what follows,
\operatorname{cond}(\chi)
means the conductor of the primitive Dirichlet character
\chi
.

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
r
be an integer and
b\in(\mathbf{Z}/r\mathbf{Z})^{*}
. Then

e\big(-\frac{bn}{r}\big)=\sum_{\chi:\operatorname{cond}(\chi)|r}c_{\chi}(b,r)1_{(n,r)=1}\chi(n),

(5.15)

where the sum is over all primitive Dirichlet characters
\chi
.

Proof.

For any character
\psi
to modulus
r
we have

\sum_{t\in(\mathbf{Z}/r\mathbf{Z})^{*}}\psi(t)e\big(\frac{bt}{r}\big)=\overline{\psi(b)}\tau(\psi).

(5.16)

By
[
12
, Lemma 3.1]
, if
\psi
is induced by a primitive character
\chi
with conductor
q
then

\tau(\psi)=\mu\big(\frac{r}{q}\big)\chi\big(\frac{r}{q}\big)\tau(\chi).

Therefore by orthogonality of characters, and writing
\sum_{\psi(\mbox{\scriptsize mod}\,r)}
for the sum over all
\phi(r)
Dirichlet characters to modulus
r
, we have

\displaystyle e\big(-\frac{bn}{r}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\phi(r)}\sum_{\psi(\mbox{\scriptsize mod}\,r)}\big(\overline{\sum_{t\in(\mathbf{Z}/r\mathbf{Z})^{*}}\psi(t)e\big(\frac{bt}{r}\big)}\big)\psi(n)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\phi(r)}\sum_{\psi(\mbox{\scriptsize mod}\,r)}\psi(b)\overline{\tau(\psi)}\psi(n)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\phi(r)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\chi:\operatorname{cond}(\chi)|r\\
\mbox{\scriptsize$\chi$ primitive}\end{subarray}}\chi(b)\mu\big(\frac{r}{q}\big)\overline{\chi\big(\frac{r}{q}\big)}\overline{\tau(\chi)}1_{(n,r)=1}\chi(n).

To get the last line, for each
\psi
we consider the primitive character
\chi
which induces it, noting that the induced character
\psi
to modulus
r
is then
1_{(n,r)=1}\chi(n)
. The result follows immediately by the definition of
c
, namely (
4.1
).∎

We will also need a version of the explicit formula. If
\chi
is a primitive Dirichlet character, denote by
Z(\chi)
the multiset of nontrivial zeros of
L(s,\chi)
in the strip
0<\Re s<1
, together with the pole at
1
if
\chi
is principal. Note that
\Xi^{*}_{Q}(\sigma_{0})
is then the disjoint union of the (multi-)sets
Z(\chi)\cap\Xi^{*}_{Q}(\sigma_{0})
, as
\chi
ranges over all primitive
\chi
with
\operatorname{cond}(\chi)\leqslant Q
; this follows from the definition of
\Xi_{Q}(\sigma_{0})
, Definition
4.1
.

Lemma 5.10
.

Suppose
\sigma_{0}\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
. Let
\psi
be a Dirichlet character
(
not necessarily primitive
)
to some modulus
r
,
r\leqslant Q\leqslant x
, and let
\chi
be the primitive character inducing
\psi
. Then

\sum_{n\leqslant x}\Lambda(n)\psi(n)=\sum_{\rho\in Z(\chi)\cap\Xi^{*}_{Q}(\sigma_{0})}\varepsilon_{\rho}\frac{x^{\rho}}{\rho}+\tilde{O}\big(x^{1-\sigma_{0}}+\frac{x}{Q}\big).

(5.17)

Here,
\varepsilon_{1}=1
and
\varepsilon_{\rho}=-1
when
\rho
is a zero.

Recall that
\tilde{O}(Y)
means a quantity that is bounded in magnitude by
N^{o(1)}Y
.

Proof.

This is a standard result (for instance, it appears as
[
33
, Exercise 45 (iv)]
), but it is not straightforward to find a completely quotable reference. First, we observe that one may quickly reduce to the primitive case
\psi=\chi
. Indeed, the sums over
\rho
are the same since
L(s,\chi)
and
L(s,\psi)
have the same nontrivial zeros (as they differ only by a finite product of terms
1-p^{-s}
), whilst on the other hand
\sum_{n\leqslant x}\Lambda(n)\psi(n)
and
\sum_{n\leqslant x}\Lambda(n)\chi(n)
differ by
\ll\log^{2}x
, as explained in the displayed equation following
[
4
, Chapter 19, equation (12)]
.

Suppose, then, that
\psi=\chi
is primitive. Suppose first that
\chi
is not the principal character
1
. Then
[
4
, Chapter 19, equations (13) to (15)]
state that

\sum_{n\leqslant x}\Lambda(n)\chi(n)=-\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{|\Im\rho|\leqslant Q}\frac{x^{\rho}}{\rho}+O\big(\frac{x(\log rx)^{2}}{Q}+x^{1/4}\log x\big).

(5.18)

Here the sum is over all zeros of
L(s,\chi)
in the critical strip
0<\Re s<1
, and the asterisk denotes that if
\chi
is real has a simple real zero
\beta_{1}
with
\beta_{1}\geqslant 1-\frac{c}{\log Q}
, then
\rho=1-\beta_{1}
is omitted from the sum. To derive (
5.17
), we must remove the contribution of the zeros
\rho
with
0<\Re\rho<1-\sigma_{0}
, which do not lie in
\Xi_{Q}
. To estimate this, divide the set of such
\rho
into sets
\Omega_{k}
,
k=0,1,\dots
, where
2^{k}\leqslant|\Im\rho|\leqslant 2^{k+1}
, and the set
\Omega_{*}
where
|\Im\rho|\leqslant 1
. By standard estimates for the number of zeros (see, for example,
[
4
, Chapter 16]
) we have
|\Omega_{k}|\lessapprox 2^{k}
, and
|\Omega_{*}|\lessapprox 1
. If
\rho\in\Omega_{k}
,
|\frac{x^{\rho}}{\rho}|\ll 2^{-k}x^{1-\sigma_{0}}
, so
\sum_{\rho\in\Omega_{k}}|\frac{x^{\rho}}{\rho}|\lessapprox x^{1-\sigma_{0}}
. If
\rho\in\Omega_{*}
, we have
|\frac{x^{\rho}}{\rho}|\ll x^{1-\sigma_{0}}\log Q
(since
\frac{1}{|\rho|}\ll\log Q
for all the zeros under consideration). Summing the contributions from
\Omega_{*}
and all the
\Omega_{k}
with
k\leqslant\log_{2}X
, we obtain the stated result.

Suppose now that
\chi
is the principal character. Here, (
5.18
) still holds provided we remember to include a main term of
x
coming from the simple pole of
\zeta
, as follows from
[
4
, Chapter 18, equations (9) and (10)]
.
∎

We turn now to the proof of Lemma
5.8
itself.

Proof of Lemma
5.8
.

Recall the definition of
\Lambda_{\sharp,Q,\sigma_{0}}
in the form (
4.5
), viz

\Lambda_{\sharp,Q,\sigma_{0}}(n)=\sum_{\rho\in\Xi_{Q}^{*}(\sigma_{0})}\varepsilon_{\rho}n^{\rho-1}F_{\chi_{\rho},Q}(n),

(5.19)

where (Definition
4.3
)

F_{\chi,Q}(n):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\operatorname{cond}(\chi)|r\\
r\leqslant Q\operatorname{cond}(\chi)\end{subarray}}\sum_{b\in(\mathbf{Z}/r\mathbf{Z})^{*}}c_{\chi}(b,r)e\big(\frac{bn}{r}\big),

with
c_{\chi}
defined in (
4.1
), and
\operatorname{cond}(\chi)
denotes the conductor of
\chi
.

Write
\theta=-\frac{a}{d}+\eta
with
d
as in the statement of Lemma
5.8
. Replacing
d
by a divisor of itself if necessary, we may assume that
\frac{a}{d}
is in lowest terms. By the assumptions of Lemma
5.8
,

|\eta|\leqslant\frac{Q^{\frac{1}{2}}}{dN}<\frac{1}{2Q^{3}}.

(5.20)

Multiplying (
5.19
) through by
e(n\theta)
, summing over
N
and interchanging the order of summation, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{n\leqslant N}

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\sharp,Q,\sigma_{0}}(n)e(\theta n)

\displaystyle=\sum_{r\leqslant Q^{2}}\sum_{b\in(\mathbf{Z}/r\mathbf{Z})^{*}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho\in\Xi_{Q}^{*}(\sigma_{0})\\
q_{\rho}|r\\
r/q_{\rho}\leqslant Q\end{subarray}}\varepsilon_{\rho}c_{\chi_{\rho}}(b,r)\sum_{n\leqslant N}n^{\rho-1}e\big((\frac{b}{r}-\frac{a}{d}+\eta)n\big).

(5.21)

Here, we have written
q_{\rho}:=\operatorname{cond}(\chi_{\rho})
for brevity.

We claim that the contribution from
\frac{b}{r}\neq\frac{a}{d}
is
\lessapprox Q^{5}
, which can be subsumed into the right hand side of the desired bound (
5.14
) because of the assumption that
Q\leqslant N^{\frac{1}{9}}
. To prove this claim, we use the second bound in (
5.2
), the fact that
|\Im\rho|\leqslant Q
, and the trivial bound
|c_{\chi_{\rho}}(b,r)|\leqslant 1
to see that this contribution is bounded by

Q\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r\leqslant Q^{2}\\
b\in(\mathbf{Z}/r\mathbf{Z})^{*}\\
\frac{b}{r}\neq-\frac{a}{d}\end{subarray}}\|\frac{b}{r}-\frac{a}{d}+\eta\|^{-1}\sum_{\rho\in\Xi_{Q}^{*}(\sigma_{0})}N^{\Re\rho-1}\ll Q\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r\leqslant Q^{2}\\
b\in(\mathbf{Z}/r\mathbf{Z})^{*}\\
\frac{b}{r}\neq-\frac{a}{d}\end{subarray}}\|\frac{b}{r}-\frac{a}{d}+\eta\|^{-1},

where the inequality follows from Lemma
5.2
. Now if
\frac{b}{r}\neq-\frac{a}{d}
then
\|\frac{b}{r}-\frac{a}{d}+\eta\|\geqslant\frac{1}{rd}-|\eta|>\frac{1}{2}Q^{-3}
, since
r\leqslant Q^{2}
,
d<Q
and
|\eta|\leqslant(2Q^{3})^{-1}
by (
5.20
). Since the different
\frac{b}{r}
are
Q^{-4}
-separated,

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r\leqslant Q^{2}\\
b\in(\mathbf{Z}/r\mathbf{Z})^{*}\\
\frac{b}{r}\neq-\frac{a}{d}\end{subarray}}\|\frac{b}{r}-\frac{a}{d}+\eta\|^{-1}\ll Q^{4}\log Q.

Thus the contribution of
\frac{b}{r}\neq-\frac{a}{d}
to (
5.21
) is
\ll Q^{5}\log Q\lessapprox Q^{5}
, as claimed. It therefore follows from (
5.21
) that

\sum_{n\leqslant N}\Lambda_{\sharp,Q,\sigma_{0}}(n)e(\theta n)=\sum_{\rho\in\Xi_{Q}^{*}(\sigma_{0})}\varepsilon_{\rho}c_{\chi_{\rho}}(a,d)\sum_{n\leqslant N}n^{\rho-1}e(\eta n)+\tilde{O}(Q^{5}).

(5.22)

(Note that the conditions
q_{\rho}\mid d
and
d/q_{\rho}\leqslant Q
are redundant, since
c_{\chi_{\rho}}(a,d)=0
unless the first is satisfied, and
d\leqslant Q^{\frac{1}{2}}
implies the second is automatically satisfied.)

Now we look at the Fourier transform of the von Mangoldt function
\Lambda
itself. The key input is the explicit formula, Lemma
5.10
.
We want to twist this by
e(\eta n)
for small
\eta
, which can be done by partial summation. Specifically, observe that

e(\eta n)=e(\eta N)-2\pi i\eta\int^{N}_{n}e(\eta t)dt.

Multiplying by
\Lambda(n)\psi(n)
, summing over
n\leqslant N
, interchanging the order of integration and summation and substituting
t=Nu
gives

\displaystyle\sum_{n\leqslant N}\Lambda(n)\psi(n)e(\eta n)=e(\eta N)

\displaystyle\sum_{n\leqslant N}\Lambda(n)\psi(n)-

\displaystyle-2\pi i\eta N\int^{1}_{0}e(\eta Nu)\big(\sum_{n\leqslant Nu}\Lambda(n)\psi(n)\big)du.

Applying the explicit formula (
5.17
) and using the identity

\int^{1}_{0}x^{\rho-1}e(\eta Nx)dx=\frac{1}{\rho}e(\eta N)-2\pi i\eta N\int^{1}_{0}\frac{x^{\rho}}{\rho}e(\eta Nx)dx

(which may be established by integration by parts)
we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{n\leqslant N}\Lambda(n)\psi(n)e(\eta n)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\rho\in Z(\psi)\cap\Xi^{*}_{Q}(\sigma_{0})}\varepsilon_{\rho}N^{\rho}\int^{1}_{0}x^{\rho-1}e(\eta Nx)dx+\tilde{O}\big((1+|\eta N|)(N^{1-\sigma_{0}}+\frac{N}{Q})\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\rho\in Z(\psi)\cap\Xi^{*}_{Q}(\sigma_{0})}\varepsilon_{\rho}N^{\rho}\int^{1}_{0}x^{\rho-1}e(\eta Nx)dx+\tilde{O}(NQ^{-\frac{1}{2}})

(5.23)

by the assumption that
Q\leqslant N^{\sigma_{0}}
, and since
|\eta|\leqslant Q^{\frac{1}{2}}N^{-1}
.

We want to approximate the integral here by a sum, to which end we use the crude approximation

\int^{b}_{a}w(x)dx=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{aN\leqslant n<bN}w\big(\frac{n}{N}\big)+O\big(\frac{1}{N}(\|w\|_{L^{\infty}(a,b)}+\|w^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}(a,b)})\big),

valid for any interval
[a,b]
of length at most
1
and for any differentiable function
w:(a,b)\rightarrow\mathbf{C}
with bounded derivative. We take
w(x):=x^{\rho-1}e(\eta Nx)
and
[a,b]=[N^{-\frac{1}{2}},1]
. Then
\|w\|_{L^{\infty}(a,b)}\leqslant N^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\beta}
(where
\rho=\beta+i\gamma
) and
\|w^{\prime}\|_{L^{\infty}(a,b)}\ll N^{1-\frac{1}{2}\beta}(1+|\gamma|)+|\eta|N\cdot N^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\beta}\ll N^{1-\frac{1}{2}\beta}Q+Q^{\frac{1}{2}}N^{\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\beta}\ll N^{1-\frac{1}{2}\beta}Q
, so we get

\int^{1}_{N^{-\frac{1}{2}}}x^{\rho-1}e(\eta Nx)dx=N^{-\rho}\sum_{N^{\frac{1}{2}}\leqslant n<N}n^{\rho-1}e(\eta n)+O(N^{-\frac{1}{2}\beta}Q).

Since also

\big|\int^{N^{-\frac{1}{2}}}_{0}x^{\rho-1}e(\eta Nx)dx\big|\leqslant\int^{N^{-\frac{1}{2}}}_{0}|x|^{\beta-1}dx\ll N^{-\frac{1}{2}\beta}

and

\big|N^{-\rho}\sum_{1\leqslant n<N^{\frac{1}{2}}}n^{\rho-1}e(\eta n)\big|\leqslant N^{-\beta}\sum_{1\leqslant n<N^{\frac{1}{2}}}|n|^{\beta-1}\ll N^{-\frac{1}{2}\beta},

we have

\int^{1}_{0}x^{\rho-1}e(\eta Nx)dx=N^{-\rho}\sum_{n=1}^{N}n^{\rho-1}e(\eta n)+O(N^{-\frac{1}{2}\beta}Q).

Now (
5.23
) implies that

\displaystyle\sum_{n\leqslant N}\Lambda(n)

\displaystyle\psi(n)e(\eta n)=\sum_{\rho\in Z(\psi)\cap\Xi^{*}_{Q}(\sigma_{0})}\varepsilon_{\rho}\sum_{n=1}^{N}n^{\rho-1}e(\eta N)+

\displaystyle+O\big(Q\sum_{\rho\in Z(\psi)\cap\Xi^{*}_{Q}(\sigma_{0})}N^{\frac{1}{2}\beta}\big)+\tilde{O}(NQ^{-\frac{1}{2}}).

(5.24)

To estimate the first error term, we use the zero-density bound
|Z(\psi)\cap\Xi^{*}_{Q}(\sigma_{0})|\leqslant 1+|\Xi_{Q}(\sigma_{0})|\ll Q
(which follows from Proposition
5.1
, since
\sigma_{0}\leqslant\frac{1}{8}
). This shows that the total error term in (
5.24
) is
\tilde{O}(N^{\frac{1}{2}}Q^{2}+NQ^{-\frac{1}{2}})=\tilde{O}(NQ^{-\frac{1}{2}})
since
Q<N^{\frac{1}{5}}
.

Now (
5.15
) gives

e\big(-\frac{an}{d}\big)=\sum_{\chi:\operatorname{cond}(\chi)|d}c_{\chi}(a,d)1_{(n,d)=1}\chi(n),

where the sum is over primitive
\chi
. Recalling that
\theta=-\frac{a}{d}+\eta
, it follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{n\leqslant N}\Lambda(n)e(n\theta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\chi:\operatorname{cond}(\chi)|d}c_{\chi}(a,d)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\leqslant N\\
(n,d)=1\end{subarray}}\Lambda(n)\chi(n)e(\eta n)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\chi:\operatorname{cond}(\chi)|d}c_{\chi}(a,d)\sum_{n\leqslant N}\Lambda(n)\chi(n)e(\eta n)+\tilde{O}(Q^{\frac{1}{4}}),

where for the second bound we have used
\sum_{n\leqslant N:(n,d)\neq 1}\Lambda(n)\lessapprox 1
and the bound

\sum_{\chi:\operatorname{cond}(\chi)|d}|c_{\chi}(a,d)|\leqslant\phi(d)\frac{d^{\frac{1}{2}}}{\phi(d)}\leqslant Q^{\frac{1}{4}},

(5.25)

which follows from (
5.6
) and the assumption that
d\leqslant Q^{\frac{1}{2}}
. Substituting into (
5.24
) (with
\psi=\chi
, and using the fact that the total error term in (
5.24
) is
\tilde{O}(NQ^{-\frac{1}{2}})
as explained after (
5.24
)) gives

\displaystyle\sum_{n\leqslant N}\Lambda(n)e(\theta n)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho\in\Xi_{Q}^{*}(\sigma_{0})\\
\operatorname{cond}(\chi_{\rho})|d\end{subarray}}\varepsilon_{\rho}

\displaystyle c_{\chi_{\rho}}(a,d)\sum_{n=1}^{N}n^{\rho-1}e(\eta n)

\displaystyle+\tilde{O}\big(Q^{\frac{1}{4}}+NQ^{-\frac{1}{2}}\sum_{\chi:\operatorname{cond}(\chi)|d}|c_{\chi}(a,d)|\big).

Using (
5.25
) again, we see that the error term is
\tilde{O}(NQ^{-\frac{1}{4}})
. Note also that, in the sum over
\rho
, the condition that
\operatorname{cond}(\chi_{\rho})\mid d
can be omitted since
c_{\chi}(a,d)=0
when
\operatorname{cond}(\chi)\nmid d
.

Comparing with (
5.22
), and since
Q<N^{\frac{1}{6}}
, the proof of Lemma
5.8
is complete.
∎

6.
Completed approximants

Recall (Definition
4.3
) the definition

F_{\chi,Q}(n)=\sum_{r\leqslant qQ}\sum_{b\in(\mathbf{Z}/r\mathbf{Z})^{*}}c_{\chi}(b,r)e\big(\frac{bn}{r}\big),

(6.1)

where here
q:=\operatorname{cond}(\chi)
.

If one relaxes the constraint on
r
here, say to
r\mid R!
for some
R>Q
, we get an object which we call a “completed” approximant
\tilde{F}_{\chi,R}
. These completed approximants are much easier to understand combinatorially, and in fact provide the motivation for studying the truncated objects such as
F_{\chi,Q}
. The interplay between completed and truncated approximants is a key theme in this paper, and this section is devoted to the basic properties of completed approximants.

Definition 6.1
.

Suppose that
\chi
is a primitive Dirichlet character to modulus
q
. Let
R
be some integer parameter. Then we define

\tilde{F}_{\chi,R}(n):=\sum_{r|R!}\sum_{b\in(\mathbf{Z}/r\mathbf{Z})^{*}}c_{\chi}(b,r)e\big(\frac{bn}{r}\big).

(6.2)

At this point we introduce the functions
\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}
defined, for prime
p
, by

\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}(n):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&\mbox{if $p|n$}\\
(1-\frac{1}{p})^{-1}&\mbox{if $(p,n)=1$}\end{array}\right.

(6.3)

These functions will feature quite heavily in the paper. The notation
\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}
is (somewhat) standard, and reflects the fact that
\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}
is the most natural
(\operatorname{mod}\,p)
analogue of the von Mangoldt function.
Observe that, for
p
prime,

1-\frac{1}{p-1}c_{p}(n)=1+\frac{\mu(p)}{\phi(p)}c_{p}(n)=\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}(n),

(6.4)

where here (recall) the definition of the Ramanujan sum
c_{r}(n):=\sum_{b\in(\mathbf{Z}/r\mathbf{Z})^{*}}e(\frac{bn}{r})
.

Lemma 6.2
.

Let
Q,R\geqslant 1
be parameters. Let
\chi
be a primitive Dirichlet character with conductor
q
. Then

\tilde{F}_{\chi,R}(n)=\big(\prod_{p\leqslant R}(1-\frac{1}{p})^{-1}\big)\overline{\chi}(n)1_{(n,R!)=1}=\frac{q}{\phi(q)}\overline{\chi}(n)\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\nmid q\\
p\leqslant R\end{subarray}}\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}(n)

(6.5)

and

F_{\chi,Q}(n)=\frac{q}{\phi(q)}\overline{\chi}(n)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r\leqslant Q\\
(r,q)=1\end{subarray}}\frac{\mu(r)}{\phi(r)}c_{r}(n).

(6.6)

Proof.

Recall the definition of
c_{\chi}
, given in Definition
4.2
. In particular
c_{\chi}(b,r)
is supported where
q\mid r
and where
r/q
is squarefree and coprime to
q
. Consider the definition (
6.2
). To begin with, fix
r
(with
q\mid r
and
r/q
squarefree) and look at the inner sum over
b
. We have
r=qr^{\prime}
, with
r^{\prime}
squarefree and coprime to
q
. We may write (uniquely)
\frac{b}{r}=\frac{a}{q}+\frac{b^{\prime}}{r^{\prime}}
, where
a\in(\mathbf{Z}/q\mathbf{Z})^{*}
and
b^{\prime}\in(\mathbf{Z}/r^{\prime}\mathbf{Z})^{*}
. Note that
b\equiv ar^{\prime}(\operatorname{mod}\,q)
and therefore
\chi(b)=\chi(a)\chi(r^{\prime})
, and so

c_{\chi}(b,r)=\frac{1}{\phi(q)}\chi(a)\overline{\tau(\chi)}\frac{\mu(r^{\prime})}{\phi(r^{\prime})}.

Therefore we may factor

\sum_{b\in(\mathbf{Z}/r\mathbf{Z})^{*}}c_{\chi}(b,r)e\big(\frac{bn}{r}\big)=E_{1}E_{2}

where

E_{1}:=\frac{\overline{\tau(\chi)}}{\phi(q)}\sum_{a\in(\mathbf{Z}/q\mathbf{Z})^{*}}\chi(a)e\big(\frac{an}{q}\big),\quad E_{2}:=\frac{\mu(r^{\prime})}{\phi(r^{\prime})}\sum_{b^{\prime}\in(\mathbf{Z}/r^{\prime}\mathbf{Z})^{*}}e\big(\frac{b^{\prime}n}{r^{\prime}}\big).

(6.7)

Now it is easy to see (and we have already used in this in (
5.16
)) that
\sum_{a\in(\mathbf{Z}/q\mathbf{Z})^{*}}\chi(a)e(\frac{an}{q})=\overline{\chi(n)}\tau(\chi)
if
(n,q)=1
. If
\chi
is
primitive
, as here, the condition
(n,q)=1
can be dropped since both sides of the expression vanish if
(n,q)>1
, as explained in
[
12
, Section 3.4]
. Therefore, using (
5.5
),
E_{1}=\frac{q}{\phi(q)}\overline{\chi(n)}
. We may write
E_{2}
more succinctly as
\frac{\mu(r^{\prime})}{\phi(r^{\prime})}c_{r^{\prime}}(n)
.
Summarising, we have

\sum_{b\in(\mathbf{Z}/r\mathbf{Z})^{*}}c_{\chi}(b,r)e\big(\frac{bn}{r}\big)=\frac{q}{\phi(q)}\overline{\chi(n)}\frac{\mu(r^{\prime})}{\phi(r^{\prime})}c_{r^{\prime}}(n)

(6.8)

(where
r=qr^{\prime}
with
(r^{\prime},q)=1
).

The expression (
6.6
) follows immediately from the definition (
6.1
) of
F_{\chi,Q}
, summing (
6.8
) over
r^{\prime}\leqslant Q
(and coprime to
q
).

To obtain (
6.5
), we recall the definition (
6.2
) of
\tilde{F}_{\chi,R}
, and instead sum (
6.8
) over all
r\mid R!
. We obtain

\tilde{F}_{\chi,R}(n)=\frac{q}{\phi(q)}\overline{\chi(n)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r^{\prime}|R!\\
(r^{\prime},q)=1\end{subarray}}\frac{\mu(r^{\prime})}{\phi(r^{\prime})}c_{r^{\prime}}(n).

(6.9)

(We will use this equation in its own right in Section
10
.)

Using the multiplicativity property
c_{ab}(n)=c_{a}(n)c_{b}(n)
when
(a,b)=1
, as well as (
6.4
), we see that sum over
r^{\prime}
factors as

\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\leqslant R\\
p\nmid q\end{subarray}}\big(1+\frac{\mu(p)}{\phi(p)}c_{p}(n)\big)=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\leqslant R\\
p\nmid q\end{subarray}}\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}(n),

from which (
6.5
) follows.
∎

To conclude this section, let us record explicitly what happens in the case
\chi=\chi_{0}
, the principal character. Recall from (
4.4
) that in this case,
F_{\chi_{0},Q}
is in fact equal to
\Lambda_{Q}
, the classical approximant defined in (
3.3
). It is therefore natural to write
\tilde{\Lambda}_{R}
instead of
\tilde{F}_{\chi_{0},R}
for the corresponding completed approximant, thus

\tilde{\Lambda}_{R}(n):=\sum_{q|R!}\frac{\mu(q)}{\phi(q)}c_{q}(n).

(6.10)

The special case
\chi=\chi_{0}
of Lemma
6.2
is then the statement that

\tilde{\Lambda}_{R}(n)=\prod_{p\leqslant R}\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}(n),

(6.11)

which is a normalised characteristic function of the almost primes at level
R
(that is, numbers all of whose prime factors are
>R
). This statement also follows rather directly from (
6.4
) and multiplicativity.

6.1.
Pointwise bounds

An application of the above expressions are pointwise bounds for the
F_{\chi,Q}
. We first prove a general fact about what we will later call Ramanujan series.

Lemma 6.3
.

Suppose that

f(n)=\sum_{r\leqslant Q}v(r)c_{r}(n).

(6.12)

Then

f(n)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d|n\\
d\leqslant Q\end{subarray}}\lambda_{d}\quad\mbox{where}\quad\frac{\lambda_{d}}{d}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d|r\\
r\leqslant Q\end{subarray}}\mu\big(\frac{r}{d}\big)v(r).

(6.13)

Proof.

With
\lambda_{d}
as defined in (
6.13
) we have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d|n\\
d\leqslant Q\end{subarray}}\lambda_{d}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d|n\\
d\leqslant Q\end{subarray}}d\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d|r\\
r\leqslant Q\end{subarray}}\mu\big(\frac{r}{d}\big)v(r)=\sum_{r\leqslant Q}v(r)\sum_{d|(r,n)}d\mu\big(\frac{r}{d}\big).

(6.14)

On the other hand,

1_{d|n}=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{a\in\mathbf{Z}/d\mathbf{Z}}e\big(\frac{an}{d}\big)=\frac{1}{d}\sum_{r|d}c_{r}(n),

and so by Möbius inversion we have

\sum_{d|(r,n)}d\mu\big(\frac{r}{d}\big)=c_{r}(n).

Substituting into (
6.14
) gives the claimed result.
∎

Remark.
The sum
\sum_{d|n:d\leqslant Q}\lambda_{d}
is a kind of sieve weight. Conversely, any such weight can be expressed in the form (
6.12
), with
v(r)=\sum_{d\leqslant Q:r|d}\frac{\lambda_{d}}{d}
, a straightforward exercise we leave to the reader.

Corollary 6.4
.

We have
|F_{\chi,Q}(n)|\ll\tau(n)\log^{3}Q
pointwise. Consequently,

|\Lambda_{\sharp,Q,\sigma_{0}}(n)|\ll\tau(n)\log^{3}Q\big(\sum_{\rho\in\Xi_{Q}^{*}(\sigma_{0})}n^{\Re\rho-1}\big).

(6.15)

Proof.

Set
f(n):=\sum_{r\leqslant Q:(r,q)=1}\frac{\mu(r)}{\phi(r)}c_{r}(n)
. Applying Lemma
6.3
with
v(r)=\frac{\mu(r)}{\phi(r)}1_{(r,q)=1}
, we obtain
f(n)=\sum_{d|n:d\leqslant Q}\lambda_{d}
with

\frac{\lambda_{d}}{d}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d|r\\
r\leqslant Q\\
(r,q)=1\end{subarray}}\frac{\mu(\frac{r}{d})\mu(r)}{\phi(r)}=\frac{\mu(d)}{\phi(d)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\leqslant Q/d\\
(dk,q)=1\\
(d,k)=1\end{subarray}}\frac{\mu^{2}(k)}{\phi(k)}.

(Here, of course, we wrote
r=dk
, noting further that the sum is supported where
r
is squarefree, which means that
d
and
k
are coprime.)
It follows that

|\lambda_{d}|\leqslant\frac{d}{\phi(d)}\sum_{k\leqslant Q}\frac{1}{\phi(k)}\ll\log^{2}Q.

Thus
|f(n)|\leqslant\tau(n)\log^{2}Q
, from which the stated bound follows immediately using (
6.6
). (We could, of course, easily save almost two powers of log by using less crude bounds on
\frac{m}{\phi(m)}
if we wanted to.)

The bound (
6.15
) is immediate from this and the definition (Definition
4.4
) of
\Lambda_{\sharp,Q,\sigma_{0}}
.
∎

Part III
Preliminaries to the main construction

7.
zeros and density estimates

In this section we assemble some more sophisticated zero-density estimates needed for the main construction. Recall the definition (Definition
4.1
) of the multiset
\Xi_{Q}(\sigma_{0})
. One main aim of this section will be to show the existence of a “good scale”
T
at which any exceptional zero in
\Xi_{T}(\sigma_{0})
(that is, a real zero of a real
L(s,\chi)
very close to
s=1
) has fairly small modulus. The key result of this section is Proposition
7.7
.

The manipulations in this section are rather similar to those in the standard proof of Linnik’s theorem as presented, for example, in
[
12
, Section 18.4]
. The main difference here is that we need to consider characters to all moduli
q\leqslant Q
, rather than just a single
q
, which means we must appeal to more general zero-density estimates.

Let us recall some facts about zeros of
L
-functions. In this section
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3},\lambda_{4}
are fixed absolute positive constants. Once again we adopt the standard convention in analytic number theory of writing, for a nontrivial zero
\rho
of some
L
-function,
\rho=\beta+i\gamma
. Somewhat less standardly, we always write
\sigma:=1-\beta
, thus
\Re\rho=1-\sigma
.

The first fact we recall is Page’s theorem, in the form stated at the beginning of
[
1
, Section 6]
. See
[
20
, Corollary 11.10]
for another reference.

Proposition 7.1
.

There is an absolute, computable constant
\lambda_{1}\in(0,1]
such that the following is true for any parameter
Q\geqslant 10
: with possibly one exception, all
\rho\in\Xi_{Q}(\frac{1}{2})
satisfy
\sigma\geqslant\frac{\lambda_{1}}{\log Q}
. The exception, if it exists, is a simple real zero of
L(s,\chi_{*})
for some real Dirichlet character
\chi_{*}
of conductor
q_{*}\leqslant Q
. This zero, if it exists, is called the
exceptional zero
at scale
Q
.

The second is a result of Landau. For the proof, see for instance
[
20
, Theorem 11.7]
.

Proposition 7.2
.

Suppose that
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}
are real characters modulo
q_{1},q_{2}
, with
\chi_{1}\chi_{2}
not principal and with real zeros at
1-\sigma_{1},1-\sigma_{2}
respectively. Then

\max(\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2})\geqslant\frac{\lambda_{1}}{\log q_{1}q_{2}}.

Using these facts together, we can locate scales
Q
at which (essentially) any exceptional zero has relatively small modulus.

Proposition 7.3
.

Let
\kappa\in(0,1)
be given. Then for any
X\geqslant 10^{1/\kappa}
, and for either
Q=X
or
Q=X^{\kappa}
, at least one of the following holds:

(1)

(first case)
all zeros in
\Xi_{Q}(\frac{1}{2})
satisfy
\sigma\geqslant\frac{\lambda_{1}\kappa}{2\log Q}
;

(2)

(second case)
there is an exceptional zero at scale
Q
, but its modulus
q_{*}
satisfies
q_{*}<Q^{\kappa}
.

Remarks.
To avoid confusion, for the time being we call these the first and second cases rather than the unexceptional and exceptional cases since the first case does not strictly preclude an exceptional zero at scale
Q
in the sense of Proposition
7.1
; there could be a zero with
\frac{\lambda_{1}\kappa}{2\log Q}\leqslant\sigma<\frac{\lambda_{1}}{\log Q}
.

Proposition
7.3
is very closely related to, for example,
[
20
, Corollary 11.9]
.

Proof of Proposition
7.3
.

Consider first of all the scale
Q=X
. If there is no exceptional zero at this scale (in the sense of Proposition
7.1
) then we are in case (1) and indeed we have the stronger bound
\sigma\geqslant\frac{\lambda_{1}}{\log Q}
. Suppose then that there is an exceptional zero
\rho_{*}=1-\sigma_{*}
at this scale. If
\sigma_{*}\geqslant\frac{\lambda_{1}\kappa}{2\log Q}
then we are again in case (1). If
q_{*}\leqslant Q^{\kappa}
then we are in case (2). Suppose, then, that
X^{\kappa}<q_{*}\leqslant X
and
\sigma_{*}<\frac{\lambda_{1}\kappa}{2\log X}
.

Now consider the scale
Q=X^{\kappa}
. By analogous reasoning, we are done unless there is an exceptional zero
\rho^{\prime}_{*}
at this scale, with
X^{\kappa^{2}}<q^{\prime}_{*}\leqslant X^{\kappa}
and
\sigma^{\prime}_{*}<\frac{\lambda_{1}\kappa}{2\log X^{\kappa}}
.

Since
q^{\prime}_{*}\leqslant X^{\kappa}<q_{*}
, the zeros
\rho_{*},\rho^{\prime}_{*}
correspond to distinct real characters. By Landau’s result we obtain

\frac{\lambda_{1}\kappa}{2\log X^{\kappa}}\geqslant\max(\sigma_{*},\sigma^{\prime}_{*})\geqslant\frac{\lambda_{1}}{\log q_{*}q^{\prime}_{*}}>\frac{\lambda_{1}}{\log X^{2}},

a contradiction.
∎

We quote the following two results from
[
1
]
. Here,
\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}
are computable absolute constants.

Proposition 7.4
.

Let
Q\geqslant 10
. We have, uniformly in
\alpha\leqslant 1
,

\#\{\rho\in\Xi_{Q}(\textstyle\frac{1}{2}\displaystyle):\Re\rho\geqslant\alpha\}\leqslant\lambda_{2}Q^{\lambda_{3}(1-\alpha)}.

(7.1)

Proof.

This is the first statement in
[
1
, Théorème 14]
, where it is noted that the argument leads to explicit constants if desired. ∎

Proposition 7.5
.

Let
Q\geqslant 10
. If there is an exceptional zero
(
\rho_{*}
)
at scale
Q
then we have the stronger bound

\#\{\rho\in\Xi_{Q}(\textstyle\frac{1}{2}\displaystyle):\rho\neq\rho_{*},\Re\rho\geqslant\alpha\}\leqslant\lambda_{2}(\sigma_{*}\log Q)Q^{\lambda_{3}(1-\alpha)}.

(7.2)

Proof.

This is the second statement in
[
1
, Théorème 14]
.
∎

Remark.
Note that Proposition
7.4
is a grand log-free zero-density estimate and as such is the same as Proposition
5.1
, except that in the latter we gave an explicit exponent of
8
in place of
\lambda_{3}
. In this section, the implied constant
\lambda_{2}
is also important, and moreover we must pair Proposition
7.4
with Proposition
7.5
, which is usually called
exceptional zero repulsion
.

Explicit (large) values for
\lambda_{2},\lambda_{3}
are obtained in the work of Thorner and Zaman
[
36
]
, and using these they were able to show that the exponent
c
in Theorem
1.1
can be taken to be
10^{-18}
.

Let
M
be the explicit absolute constant specified in (
11.3
), that is to say
M=7\cdot 3^{6}\cdot 2^{22993}
.

Proposition 7.6
.

There is an absolute constant
B
such that the following holds for any parameter
Q\geqslant 10
.

•

If there is no exceptional zero at scale
Q
,

4M\sum_{\rho\in\Xi_{Q}(\frac{1}{2})}Q^{-B\sigma}\leqslant 1;

(7.3)

•

If there is an exceptional zero
\rho_{*}
at scale
Q
,

Q^{-B\sigma_{*}}+2M\sum_{\rho\in\Xi_{Q}(\frac{1}{2})\setminus\{\rho_{*}\}}Q^{-B\sigma}\leqslant 1.

(7.4)

Proof.

Let
\lambda_{4}\in(0,1)
be a constant such that

e^{-x}+\lambda_{4}\lambda_{2}x<1

(7.5)

for all
x
with
0<x<\lambda_{1}
(a choice which works is
\lambda_{4}:=\min(\frac{1}{2\lambda_{2}},\frac{1}{2\lambda_{1}\lambda_{2}})
).

Unexceptional case.

Choose
k_{0}
such that

\lambda_{2}\sum_{k\geqslant k_{0}}e^{-2^{k-1}}\leqslant\frac{\lambda_{4}}{4M}.

Set

S_{k}:=\{\rho\in\Xi_{Q}(\textstyle\frac{1}{2}\displaystyle):2^{k}\leqslant B\sigma\log Q<2^{k+1}\}.

For all
\rho
, the fact that we are in the unexceptional case means that
\sigma\log Q\geqslant\lambda_{1}
, so with
B
chosen suitably (that is
B\lambda_{1}>2^{k_{0}}
),
S_{k}
is only nonempty for
k\geqslant k_{0}
. From (
7.1
) we have

|S_{k}|\leqslant\lambda_{2}Q^{\frac{\lambda_{3}2^{k+1}}{B\log Q}}=\lambda_{2}e^{\frac{\lambda_{3}2^{k+1}}{B}}.

Therefore

\displaystyle\sum_{\rho\in\Xi_{Q}(\frac{1}{2})}Q^{-B\sigma}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\geqslant k_{0}}\sum_{\rho\in S_{k}}Q^{-B\sigma}\leqslant\sum_{k\geqslant k_{0}}|S_{k}|e^{-2^{k}}

\displaystyle\leqslant\lambda_{2}\sum_{k\geqslant k_{0}}e^{\frac{\lambda_{3}}{B}2^{k+1}-2^{k}}\leqslant\lambda_{2}\sum_{k\geqslant k_{0}}e^{-\frac{2^{k}}{2}},

which is at most
\frac{1}{4M}
by the choice of
k_{0}
. Here, in the penultimate step, we also assumed that
B\geqslant 4\lambda_{3}
.

Exceptional case.
We proceed essentially as before, but now defining

S_{k}:=\{\rho\neq\rho_{*}:2^{k}\leqslant B\sigma\log Q<2^{k+1}\}.

Once again, if
B
is chosen suitably,
S_{k}
is only nonempty for
k\geqslant k_{0}
. From (
7.2
), we have

|S_{k}|\leqslant\lambda_{2}(\sigma_{*}\log Q)Q^{\frac{\lambda_{3}2^{k+1}}{B\log Q}}=\lambda_{2}(\sigma_{*}\log Q)e^{\frac{\lambda_{3}}{B}2^{k+1}}.

Much as before,

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\rho\in\Xi_{Q}(\frac{1}{2})\\
\rho\neq\rho_{*}\end{subarray}}Q^{-B\sigma}\leqslant\lambda_{2}(\sigma_{*}\log Q)\sum_{k\geqslant k_{0}}e^{-\frac{2^{k}}{2}}\leqslant\frac{1}{4M}\lambda_{2}\lambda_{4}(\sigma_{*}\log Q).

However, by the choice of
\lambda_{4}
, and with
x:=\sigma_{*}\log Q\leqslant\lambda_{1}
in (
7.5
),

\lambda_{2}\lambda_{4}(\sigma_{*}\log Q)<1-Q^{-\sigma_{*}}.

This confirms the desired inequality.∎

Let us finish by stating the consequence of all this that we will need in what follows. In the following, the exponents
\frac{1}{120}
and
10^{-6}
are what turn out to be convenient later; it would be possible to specify any positive values for them, at the expense of making
c
smaller.

Proposition 7.7
.

Let
M:=7\cdot 3^{6}\cdot 2^{22993}
. There is an absolute constant
c>0
with the following property. Suppose that
\sigma_{0}\leqslant\frac{1}{8}
. Let
N
be a large integer. Then there is
T
,
N^{c}\leqslant T\leqslant N^{\frac{1}{120}}
, such that if we list the zeros of
\Xi_{T}(\sigma_{0})

(
with multiplicity
)
in order of decreasing real part as
\rho_{1},\dots,\rho_{J}
,
(
at least
)
one of the following holds, where
\sigma_{j}:=1-\Re\rho_{j}
:

(1)

We have

\sum_{j=1}^{J}N^{-\frac{1}{16}\sigma_{j}}\leqslant\frac{1}{2M};

(7.6)

(2)

\rho_{1}
is a simple, real zero of a real character
\chi_{1}
of conductor
q_{1}\leqslant T^{10^{-6}}
, and

N^{-\frac{1}{16}\sigma_{1}}+2M\sum_{j=2}^{J}N^{-\frac{1}{16}\sigma_{j}}\leqslant 1.

(7.7)

Moreover,

J\ll T.

(7.8)

Proof.

Set
b:=\min(\frac{1}{16B},\frac{\lambda_{1}\kappa}{32\log(4M)})
. Apply Proposition
7.3
with
X:=N^{b}
and
\kappa:=10^{-6}
and let
T=Q=X
or
X^{\kappa}
as in the conclusion of that proposition. Note that

T\leqslant X=N^{b}\leqslant N^{1/16B}.

(7.9)

Then either (first case) we have

\sigma_{1}\geqslant\frac{\lambda_{1}\kappa}{2\log T}\geqslant\frac{\lambda_{1}\kappa}{2\log X}

(7.10)

or (second case) there is an exceptional zero
\rho_{1}
at scale
T
, with modulus
q_{1}\leqslant T^{\kappa}
.

Now apply Proposition
7.6
with
Q=T
. Suppose we are in the first case. If there is no exceptional zero at scale
T
we apply (
7.3
), which gives (using (
7.9
))

\sum_{j=1}^{J}N^{-\frac{1}{16}\sigma_{j}}\leqslant\sum_{j=1}^{J}T^{-B\sigma_{j}}\leqslant\frac{1}{4M}.

If there is an exceptional zero at scale
T
(which must be
\rho_{1}
) then we must instead apply (
7.4
). Ignoring the term with the exceptional zero, as above this gives

\sum_{j=2}^{J}N^{-\frac{1}{16}\sigma_{j}}\leqslant\frac{1}{4M}.

(7.11)

Moreover, by the lower bound (
7.10
) on
\sigma_{1}
we have

N^{-\frac{1}{16}\sigma_{1}}\leqslant N^{-\frac{\lambda_{1}\kappa}{32\log X}}=e^{-\frac{\lambda_{1}\kappa}{32b}}\leqslant\frac{1}{4M}

by the choice of
b
. Adding this to (
7.11
) gives (
7.6
) in this case as well.

Now suppose we are in the second case (of Proposition
7.3
, thus
\rho_{1}
is exceptional with modulus
q_{1}\leqslant T^{\kappa}
). Applying (
7.4
) gives, using (
7.9
) again,

N^{-\frac{1}{16}\sigma_{j}}+2M\sum_{j=2}^{J}N^{-\frac{1}{16}\sigma_{j}}\leqslant T^{-B\sigma_{1}}+2M\sum_{j=2}^{J}T^{-B\sigma_{j}}\leqslant 1.

This is (
7.7
), so we have indeed shown that one of (1), (2) holds.

Finally, (
7.8
) follows immediately from (
5.1
) and the assumption that
\sigma_{0}\leqslant\frac{1}{8}
.
∎

Finally, we will require the following well-known bound.

Lemma 7.8

(Page’s bound)
.

We have
\sigma_{1}\gg q_{1}^{-1/2}(\log q_{1})^{-2}\gg q_{1}^{-1}
.

Proof.

See
[
20
, Corollary 11.12]
