. Note that the implied constant here can be made explicit if desired; one has Siegel’s much stronger bound
\sigma_{1}\gg_{\varepsilon}q_{1}^{-\varepsilon}
for any
\varepsilon>0
(see
[
20
, Theorem 11.14]
), but here the implied constant is not explicit when
\varepsilon<\frac{1}{2}
.∎

8.
Periodic functions and Ramanujan series

In this section we set out some basic nomenclature for periodic functions
f:\mathbf{Z}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}
and for certain special periodic functions which we shall call Ramanujan series (following, for example,
[
26
]
, and inspired by
[
25
]
), and we will prove some basic lemmas that will be needed later.

8.1.
Basic nomenclature and definitions

Periodic functions. Rational Fourier series.
The first key observation is that any periodic function
f:\mathbf{Z}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}
has a (unique)
rational Fourier series
, that is to say we may write

f(n)=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbf{Q}/\mathbf{Z}}f^{\wedge}(\lambda)e(\lambda n)

(8.1)

for certain
f^{\wedge}(\lambda)\in\mathbf{C}
, which we call the rational Fourier coefficients of
f
, and with this sum being supported on just finitely many
\lambda
. To see the existence of such a series, simply observe that

1_{n\equiv b(\mbox{\scriptsize mod}\,r)}=\frac{1}{r}\sum_{x\in\mathbf{Z}/r\mathbf{Z}}e\big(-\frac{xb}{r}\big)e\big(\frac{xn}{r}\big).

(8.2)

Any periodic function may be written as a finite linear combination of basic functions like this.

Norms, support and averages.
If
f
is a periodic function with rational Fourier coefficients
f^{\wedge}(\lambda)
then we write

\|f\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}:=\max_{\lambda}|f^{\wedge}(\lambda)|\quad\mbox{and}\quad\|f\|^{\wedge}_{1}:=\sum_{\lambda}|f^{\wedge}(\lambda)|.

We also define the support
\operatorname{s}(f)
to be the largest denominator of any
\lambda
for which
f^{\wedge}(\lambda)\neq 0
(when
\lambda
is written in lowest terms).

If
f:\mathbf{Z}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}
is a periodic function then we write
\operatorname{Av}f
for the average of
f
(which is well-defined, either as the average of
f
over any period, or as
\lim_{X\rightarrow\infty}X^{-1}\sum_{x=1}^{X}f(x)
).

We have the orthogonality relation that
\operatorname{Av}e(\lambda n)=1_{\lambda=0}
, and so from (
8.1
) we see that
f^{\wedge}(\lambda)=\operatorname{Av}f(n)e(-\lambda n)
. In particular the rational Fourier expansion of a function is unique, and also

\|f\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}\leqslant\operatorname{Av}|f|.

(8.3)

Positivity.
We will say that a periodic function
f
is
Fourier-positive
if
f^{\wedge}
is real and non-negative. If
f,g:\mathbf{Z}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}
are two periodic functions then we write
g\prec f
to mean that
|g^{\wedge}(\lambda)|\leqslant f^{\wedge}(\lambda)
for all
\lambda\in\mathbf{Q}/\mathbf{Z}
. In particular,
f
must be Fourier-positive for this notation to apply. We will use this notation fairly extensively throughout the paper.

A simple example of a Fourier-positive function is, for any fixed
r
, the function
f(n):=1_{r|n}
, which is Fourier-positive by (
8.2
) (with
b=0
). Note that for this function we have

\|1_{r|n}\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}=\frac{1}{r},\quad\|1_{r|n}\|^{\wedge}_{1}=1,\quad\operatorname{s}(1_{r|n})=r.

(8.4)

Ramanujan series.
We say that a periodic function
f
in the form (
8.1
) is a
Ramanujan series
if
f^{\wedge}(\lambda)
is supported where
\operatorname{denom}(\lambda)
is squarefree, and if it depends only on
\operatorname{denom}(\lambda)
. Equivalently, we have an expansion

f(n)=\sum_{q=1}^{\infty}\alpha(q)\sum_{a\in(\mathbf{Z}/q\mathbf{Z})^{*}}e\big(\frac{an}{q}\big)=\sum_{q=1}^{\infty}\alpha(q)c_{q}(n),

(8.5)

supported on finitely many squarefree
q
.

8.2.
Simple properties

Here are some simple properties of positivity which we will use several times.

Proposition 8.1
.

We have the following statements concerning periodic functions from
\mathbf{Z}
to
\mathbf{C}
.

(1)

If
F,G
are Fourier-positive then so are
F+G
,
FG
and
tF
for
t\in\mathbf{R}_{\geqslant 0}
;

(2)

If
(F_{i})_{i\in I}
is a finite collection of Fourier-positive functions then any non-negative linear combination
\sum_{i\in I}t_{i}F_{i}
,
t_{i}\in\mathbf{R}_{\geqslant 0}
, is Fourier-positive;

(3)

If
F\prec G
then
\overline{F}\prec\overline{G}
and
tF\prec tG
for
t\in\mathbf{R}_{\geqslant 0}
;

(4)

If
F^{\prime}_{1}\prec F_{1}
and
F^{\prime}_{2}\prec F_{2}
then
F^{\prime}_{1}+F^{\prime}_{2}\prec F_{1}+F_{2}
and
F^{\prime}_{1}F^{\prime}_{2}\prec F_{1}F_{2}
;

(5)

If
F^{\prime}\prec F
and if
G
is Fourier-positive then
F^{\prime}G\prec FG
. In particular, this holds if
G(n)=e(\lambda n)
.

(6)

If
F
has some integer
m
as a period,
F^{\wedge}
is supported on rationals
\lambda
with
\operatorname{denom}(\lambda)|m
;

(7)

If
F^{\prime},F
have
m
as a period, then
F^{\prime}\prec F
if and only if

\bigg|\sum_{n\in\mathbf{Z}/m\mathbf{Z}}F^{\prime}(n)e\big(-\frac{an}{m}\big)\bigg|\leqslant\sum_{n\in\mathbf{Z}/m\mathbf{Z}}F(n)e\big(-\frac{an}{m}\big)

for all
a\in\mathbf{Z}/m\mathbf{Z}
.

Proof.

(1) and (2) are straightforward.
For the first statement in (3), note that
\overline{F}^{\wedge}(\lambda)=\overline{F^{\wedge}(-\lambda)}
, so

|\overline{F}^{\wedge}(\lambda)|=|F^{\wedge}(-\lambda)|\leqslant G^{\wedge}(-\lambda)=\overline{G^{\wedge}(-\lambda)}=\overline{G}^{\wedge}(\lambda),

where the last step holds since
G^{\wedge}
is real-valued (since
F\prec G
). The second statement in (3) is clear. For (4), we use the formula

(b_{1}b_{2})^{\wedge}(\lambda)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in\mathbf{Q}/\mathbf{Z}\\
\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}=\lambda(\mbox{\scriptsize mod}\,1)\end{subarray}}b_{1}^{\wedge}(\lambda_{1})b_{2}^{\wedge}(\lambda_{2}),

(8.6)

valid for any pair of periodic functions
b_{1},b_{2}:\mathbf{Z}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}
. Applying this first with
b_{i}=F^{\prime}_{i}
and then with
b_{i}=F_{i}
, we obtain

\displaystyle|(F^{\prime}_{1}F^{\prime}_{2})^{\wedge}(\lambda)|

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in\mathbf{Q}/\mathbf{Z}\\
\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}=\lambda(\mbox{\scriptsize mod}\,1)\end{subarray}}|F_{1}^{\prime\wedge}(\lambda_{1})||F_{2}^{\prime\wedge}(\lambda_{2})|

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in\mathbf{Q}/\mathbf{Z}\\
\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}=\lambda(\mbox{\scriptsize mod}\,1)\end{subarray}}F_{1}^{\wedge}(\lambda_{1})F_{2}^{\wedge}(\lambda_{2})=(F_{1}F_{2})^{\wedge}(\lambda),

which is the multiplicative part of (4). The additive part of (4) is straightforward. For (5), it suffices in view of (3) and (4) and the fact that any Fourier-positive
G
is a non-negative linear combination of functions of the form
e(\lambda n)
to check the case
G(n)=e(\lambda n)
. To see this, observe that
|(F^{\prime}G)^{\wedge}(\xi)|=|(F^{\prime})^{\wedge}(\xi-\lambda)|\leqslant F(\xi-\lambda)=(FG)^{\wedge}(\xi)
, for all
\xi\in\mathbf{Q}/\mathbf{Z}
. Item (6) follows from (
8.2
) and the uniqueness of rational Fourier series. Finally, for (7) we note that
\sum_{n\in\mathbf{Z}/m\mathbf{Z}}F(n)e(-\frac{an}{m})=mF^{\wedge}(\frac{a}{m})
, so under the stated assumption we have
|(F^{\prime})^{\wedge}(\frac{a}{m})|\leqslant F^{\wedge}(\frac{a}{m})
for all
a\in\mathbf{Z}/m\mathbf{Z}
. By item (6),
|(F^{\prime})^{\wedge}(\lambda)|\leqslant F^{\wedge}(\lambda)
for all
\lambda\in\mathbf{Q}/\mathbf{Z}
.
∎

Next, some simple properties concerning norms and the support.

Lemma 8.2
.

Suppose that
f:\mathbf{Z}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}
is a periodic function. Then the norms
\|f\|^{\wedge}_{1}
,
\|f\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}
are invariant under shifts: in particular
\|f^{+}\|^{\wedge}_{\bullet}=\|f\|^{\wedge}_{\bullet}=\|f^{-}\|^{\wedge}_{\bullet}
for both norms, where
f^{+}(n)=f(n+1)
and
f^{-}(n)=f(n-1)
.

Proof.

This follows immediately from
(f^{\pm})^{\wedge}(\lambda)=e(\pm\lambda)f^{\wedge}(\lambda)
, which follows from the definition (
8.1
).
∎

Lemma 8.3
.

Suppose that
b_{1},b_{2}:\mathbf{Z}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}
are periodic functions. Then

\|b_{1}b_{2}\|^{\wedge}_{1}\leqslant\|b_{1}\|^{\wedge}_{1}\|b_{2}\|^{\wedge}_{1}.

(8.7)

\|b_{1}b_{2}\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}\leqslant\|b_{1}\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}\|b_{2}\|^{\wedge}_{1}

(8.8)

If
b_{1},b_{2}
have coprime periods then we have the equality

\|b_{1}b_{2}\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}=\|b_{1}\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}\|b_{2}\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}.

(8.9)

Finally, we have

\operatorname{s}(b_{1}b_{2})\leqslant\operatorname{s}(b_{1})\operatorname{s}(b_{2}).

(8.10)

Proof.

The bounds (
8.7
), (
8.8
) are immediate from (
8.6
).
For (
8.9
), suppose that the periods of
b_{1},b_{2}
are
m_{1},m_{2}
respectively, and that
m_{1},m_{2}
are coprime. By Proposition
8.1
(6),
b_{i}^{\wedge}(\lambda_{i})
is supported where
\operatorname{denom}(\lambda_{i})
divides
m_{i}
. Now if
\frac{a_{1}}{m_{1}}+\frac{a_{2}}{m_{2}}\equiv\frac{a^{\prime}_{1}}{m_{1}}+\frac{a^{\prime}_{2}}{m_{2}}(\operatorname{mod}\,1)
then, clearing denominators and using the coprimality of
m_{1},m_{2}
, we see that
a_{1}\equiv a^{\prime}_{1}(\operatorname{mod}\,m_{1})
and
a_{2}\equiv a^{\prime}_{2}(\operatorname{mod}\,m_{2})
, so
\frac{a_{1}}{m_{1}}\equiv\frac{a^{\prime}_{1}}{m_{1}}(\operatorname{mod}\,1)
and
\frac{a_{2}}{m_{2}}\equiv\frac{a^{\prime}_{2}}{m_{2}}(\operatorname{mod}\,1)
. It follows that, in (
8.6
), all the sums
\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}
with
\lambda_{i}\in\operatorname{Supp}(b_{i}^{\wedge})
are distinct, and so for each
\lambda
there is at most one choice of
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
with
b_{1}^{\wedge}(\lambda_{1}),b_{2}^{\wedge}(\lambda_{2})\neq 0
, and so (
8.9
) follows. Finally, (
8.10
) is clear from (
8.6
). ∎

We remark on the case of Dirichlet characters, which will arise several times in the paper.

Lemma 8.4
.

Suppose that
\chi
is a primitive Dirichlet character to modulus
q
. Then
\|\chi\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}\leqslant q^{-\frac{1}{2}}
and
\|\chi\|^{\wedge}_{1}\leqslant q^{\frac{1}{2}}
.

Proof.

The second statement follows from the first, because
\chi
is
q
-periodic and so its rational Fourier series is supported on rationals with denominator
q
. By Fourier analysis on
\mathbf{Z}/q\mathbf{Z}
we have
\chi(n)=\sum_{a\in\mathbf{Z}/q\mathbf{Z}}\gamma(a)e(an/q)
, where
\gamma(a):=\sum_{x\in\mathbf{Z}/q\mathbf{Z}}\chi(x)e(-ax/q)
. By
[
12
, equation (3.12)]
and the remarks following it,
\gamma(a)=\overline{\chi}(a)\tau(\chi)
, where
\tau(\chi)
is the Gauss sum, which has modulus
\sqrt{q}
by
[
12
, Lemma 3.1]
. The first statement of the lemma follows.
∎

Let us also record basic bounds on the Fourier norms of the functions
F_{\chi,Q}
and
\Lambda_{Q}
, which are crucial in the paper.

Lemma 8.5
.

Let
\chi
be a primitive character to modulus
q
. We have

\|F_{\chi,Q}\|^{\wedge}_{1}\leqslant Qq^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(8.11)

In particular,

\|\Lambda_{Q}\|^{\wedge}_{1}\leqslant Q.

(8.12)

Proof.

From the definition (
4.1
) and the evaluation (
5.5
) for Gauss sums, we have the bound
|c_{\chi}(b,r)|\leqslant q^{\frac{1}{2}}\phi(r)^{-1}
. Recall the definition (
4.2
) of
F_{\chi,Q}
. Summing over the
\phi(r)
values of
b\in(\mathbf{Z}/r\mathbf{Z})^{*}
, and then summing over all
r
such that
q\mid r
and
\frac{r}{q}\leqslant Q
, the result follows.

The second statement (
8.12
) is immediate upon taking
\chi=\chi_{0}
, the prinicipal character, and recalling (
4.4
).
∎

8.3.
\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}},\tau^{2}_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}
and their properties

The following two types of periodic function will play an important role in the paper.
For
p
a prime, write (as in (
6.3
))

\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}(n):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&\mbox{if $p\mid n$}\\
\frac{p}{p-1}&\mbox{if $(p,n)=1$}\end{array}\right.

(8.13)

and

\tau^{2}_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}(n):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{4p}{p+3}&\mbox{if $p\mid n$}\\
\frac{p}{p+3}&\mbox{if $(p,n)=1$}.\end{array}\right.

(8.14)

The definitions are chosen so
\operatorname{Av}\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}=\operatorname{Av}\tau^{2}_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}=1
.

The function
\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}
, which is somewhat standard, was already briefly discussed in Section
6
. The notation
\tau^{2}_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}
is not standard; this is supposed to remind the reader that this function behaves somewhat like a
(\operatorname{mod}\,p)
analogue of the squared divisor function
\tau^{2}
, as discussed in subsection
3.1
, albeit normalised to have average value
1
.

It is easily checked that the rational Fourier series of
\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}},\tau^{2}_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}
are

\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}(n)=1-\frac{1}{p-1}\sum_{r\in(\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z})^{*}}e\big(\frac{rn}{p}\big)=1-\frac{1}{p-1}c_{p}(n)

(8.15)

and

\tau^{2}_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}(n)=1+\frac{3}{p+3}\sum_{r\in(\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z})^{*}}e\big(\frac{rn}{p}\big)=1+\frac{3}{p+3}c_{p}(n),

(8.16)

so in particular both are Ramanujan series. Moreover,
\tau^{2}_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}
is Fourier-positive, though
\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}
is not.

Let us also note the following for future use.

Lemma 8.6
.

We have

\|\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}=1,\qquad\|\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}\|^{\wedge}_{1}=2

(8.17)

and

\|\tau^{2}_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}=1,\qquad\|\tau^{2}_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}\|^{\wedge}_{1}\leqslant 4.

(8.18)

The following simple lemma plays a critical role in our paper. Here, recall, we use the notation
f^{+}(n)=f(n+1)
,
f^{-}(n)=f(n-1)
.

Lemma 8.7
.

For every prime
p
,
\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}^{+}\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}^{-}\tau^{2}_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}
is Fourier-positive. Also, we have the bound

\|\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}^{+}\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}^{-}\tau^{2}_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}\leqslant 1+O(p^{-2}).

(8.19)

Proof.

Set
u_{p}:=(1-\frac{1}{p})^{2}(1+\frac{3}{p})\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}^{+}\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}^{-}\tau^{2}_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}
. Then one may check that

u_{p}(n)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&\mbox{if $n\equiv\pm 1(\operatorname{mod}\,p)$}\\
4&\mbox{if $n\equiv 0(\operatorname{mod}\,p)$}\\
1&\mbox{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

If
p\neq 2
we may compute the discrete Fourier transform
\widehat{u}_{p}(k):=p^{-1}\sum_{n\in\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}u_{p}(n)e(-kn/p)
as follows:

\widehat{u}_{p}(k)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{1}{p}(3-2\cos\frac{2\pi k}{p})&k\neq 0\\
1+\frac{1}{p}&k=0.\end{array}\right.

(8.20)

Note that
\widehat{u}_{p}(k)
is real and positive, indeed
\widehat{u}_{p}(0)=1+\frac{1}{p}
and
\widehat{u}_{p}(k)\geqslant\frac{1}{p}
.

By Fourier inversion in
\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}
,

u_{p}(n)=1+\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p}\sum_{k\neq 0}\big(3-2\cos\frac{2\pi k}{p}\big)e\big(\frac{kn}{p}\big),

and so
u_{p}
is Fourier-positive.
When
p=2
one may check that
\widehat{u}_{2}(0)=\widehat{u}_{2}(1)=2
and so these inequalities also hold.

Multiplying through by
(1-\frac{1}{p})^{-2}(1+\frac{3}{p})^{-1}
, we see that

\|\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}^{+}\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}^{-}\tau^{2}_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}\leqslant\big(1-\frac{1}{p}\big)^{-2}\big(1+\frac{3}{p}\big)^{-1}\max\big(1+\frac{1}{p},\frac{5}{p}\big)=1+O(p^{-2}),

which is (
8.19
).
∎

Products of the
\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}
and of the
\tau^{2}_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}
will be important in the paper. In fact, we have already seen
\prod_{p\leqslant R}\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}(n)
; it is the same thing as
\tilde{\Lambda}_{R}(n)
, the normalised characteristic function of the
R
-almost primes, as defined in (
6.10
). Let us record this observation here explicitly:

\tilde{\Lambda}_{R}(n)=\prod_{p\leqslant R}\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}(n).

(8.21)

The product of the
\tau^{2}_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}
is not something we have seen yet, but it and its truncation are equally important in the paper.

Definition 8.8
.

Let
R
be a parameter. Then we define

\tilde{H}_{R}(n):=\prod_{p\leqslant R}\tau^{2}_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}(n),

where
\tau^{2}_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}
is defined as in (
8.14
).

By (
8.16
) and the multiplicativity of Ramanujan sums,
\tilde{H}_{R}(n)
is a Ramanujan series

\tilde{H}_{R}(n)=\sum_{q|R!}\eta(q)\sum_{a\in(\mathbf{Z}/q\mathbf{Z})^{*}}e\big(\frac{an}{q}\big).

Here, for
q\mid R!
we have

\eta(q):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\prod_{p|q}\frac{3}{p+3}&\mbox{if $q$ is squarefree}\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

We also introduce the truncated version of
\tilde{H}
.

Definition 8.9
.

Let
Q
be a parameter. Then we define

H_{Q}(n):=\sum_{q\leqslant Q}\eta(q)\sum_{a\in(\mathbf{Z}/q\mathbf{Z})^{*}}e\big(\frac{an}{q}\big).

This is an important function in the paper, as described in Subsection
3.1
. We have
\eta(q)\leqslant\frac{3^{\omega(q)}}{q}\leqslant q^{-1+o(1)}
, where
\omega(q)
is the number of distinct prime factors of
q
. Therefore we have the bound

\|H^{\wedge}_{Q}\|_{1}\leqslant Q^{1+o(1)}.

(8.22)

8.4.
A class of periodic functions

It turns out that the functions
\Lambda_{Q}
and
H_{Q}
belong to a certain class of periodic functions on
\mathbf{Z}
which has nice closure properties under shifts and pointwise multiplication. This fact will be important later in the paper.

Definition 8.10
.

Let
X\geqslant 1
be an integer and
B\geqslant 0
a real parameter. Then we denote by
\mathscr{C}_{B}(X)
the class of periodic
f:\mathbf{Z}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}
such that
f^{\wedge}(\lambda)
is supported on
\lambda
with
\operatorname{denom}(\lambda)
squarefree and at most
X
, and such that if
q=\operatorname{denom}(\lambda)
then
|f^{\wedge}(\lambda)|\leqslant\tau(q)^{B}/q
.

Lemma 8.11
.

We have
\Lambda_{Q},H_{Q}\in\mathscr{C}_{2}(Q)
.

Proof.

For
\Lambda_{Q}
(defined in (
3.3
)), this is just a case of checking that
|\frac{\mu(q)}{\phi(q)}|\leqslant\frac{\tau(q)^{2}}{q}
, which is true by some distance since
\frac{1}{\phi(q)}=\prod_{p|q}\frac{1}{p-1}\leqslant\prod_{p|q}\frac{2}{p}=\frac{\tau(q)}{q}
.

For
H_{Q}
, we must show that
\eta(q)\leqslant\tau(q)^{2}/q
. Noting that when
q
is squarefree we have
\prod_{p|q}3=\tau(q)^{\frac{\log 3}{\log 2}}<\tau(q)^{2}
gives this bound immediately.
∎

It is clear from the definition that
\mathscr{C}_{B}(X)
is closed under shifts, that is to say if
f(n)\in\mathscr{C}_{B}(X)
then
f(n+h)\in\mathscr{C}_{B}(X)
. Slightly less obvious is the fact that these classes are (weakly) closed under taking products, which is the content of Lemma
8.12
below. Here, for
\lambda\in\mathbf{R}_{>0}
we write
\lambda\mathscr{C}_{B}(X)
for those series of the form
\lambda f
,
f\in\mathscr{C}_{B}(X)
.

Lemma 8.12
.

Suppose that
f_{1}\in\mathscr{C}_{B_{1}}(X_{1})
and
f_{2}\in\mathscr{C}_{B_{2}}(X_{2})
. Then
f_{1}f_{2}\in(\log^{O_{B_{1},B_{2}}(1)}X_{1})\mathscr{C}_{B_{1}+2B_{2}+3}(X_{1}X_{2})
.

To prove this result we will need a counting result, Lemma
8.13
below, about denominators of sums of fractions (when written in lowest terms).

Suppose that
q,r
are squarefree and that
(a,q)=(b,r)=1
, and set

d:=\operatorname{denom}\big(\frac{a}{q}+\frac{b}{r}\big)=\frac{qr}{(ar+bq,qr)}.

Then
\frac{qr}{(q,r)^{2}}\mid d
and
d\mid[q,r]
(and in particular
d
is squarefree).

Lemma 8.13
.

Suppose that
q,r
are squarefree and that
b\in(\mathbf{Z}/r\mathbf{Z})^{*}
. Then

\#\{a\in(\mathbf{Z}/q\mathbf{Z})^{*}:\operatorname{denom}\big(\frac{a}{q}+\frac{b}{r}\big)=d\}\leqslant\frac{d(q,r)}{r}.

Proof.

If
d\nmid[q,r]
then the left-hand side is empty and the result is trivial. Suppose, then, that
d\mid[q,r]
. The denominator condition is equivalent to
(ar+bq,qr)=qr/d
, and so is contained in the condition
ar+bq\equiv 0(\operatorname{mod}\,qr/d)
. Dividing through by
(q,r)
and setting
r^{\prime}:=r/(q,r)
,
q^{\prime}:=q/(q,r)
(thus
q^{\prime},r^{\prime}
are coprime) and
t:=qr/d(q,r)=[q,r]/d
, this condition is equivalent to

ar^{\prime}+bq^{\prime}\equiv 0(\operatorname{mod}\,t).

(8.23)

If this condition is satisfied,
t
must be coprime to
r^{\prime}
. Indeed if
p\mid(t,r^{\prime})
then
p\mid bq^{\prime}=(ar^{\prime}+bq^{\prime})-ar^{\prime}
. But this is impossible since both
b
and
q^{\prime}
are coprime to
r^{\prime}
. Moreover, since
t\mid[q,r]=qr^{\prime}
, it follows that
t\mid q
.

Returning to (
8.23
), as
a
ranges over
\mathbf{Z}/q\mathbf{Z}
then so does
ar^{\prime}
, and so we simply need an upper bound for the number of solutions
(\operatorname{mod}\,q)
to a fixed congruence
x\equiv c(\operatorname{mod}\,t)
, where
t\mid q
. Such an upper bound is
q/t
, and the lemma follows.
∎

Proof of Lemma
8.12
.

Suppose that
\lambda=\frac{a}{q}\in\mathbf{Q}/\mathbf{Z}
is in lowest terms. Then

\displaystyle(f_{1}f_{2})^{\wedge}\big(\frac{a}{q}\big)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{1}\leqslant X_{1}\\
q_{2}\leqslant X_{2}\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1}\in(\mathbf{Z}/q_{1}\mathbf{Z})^{*}\\
a_{2}\in(\mathbf{Z}/q_{2}\mathbf{Z})^{*}\\
\frac{a_{1}}{q_{1}}+\frac{a_{2}}{q_{2}}\equiv\frac{a}{q}(\mbox{\scriptsize mod}\,1)\end{subarray}}f_{1}^{\wedge}\big(\frac{a_{1}}{q_{1}}\big)f_{2}^{\wedge}\big(\frac{a_{2}}{q_{2}}\big)

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{1}\leqslant X_{1}\\
\mu^{2}(q_{1})=1\end{subarray}}\frac{\tau(q_{1})^{B_{1}}}{q_{1}}\sum_{d|q_{1}q}\frac{\tau(d)^{B_{2}}}{d}\#\{a_{1}\in(\mathbf{Z}/q_{1}\mathbf{Z})^{*}:\operatorname{denom}\big(\frac{a_{1}}{q_{1}}-\frac{a}{q}\big)=d\}.

By Lemma
8.13
, this is bounded above by

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{1}\leqslant X_{1}\\
\mu^{2}(q_{1})=1\end{subarray}}\frac{\tau(q_{1})^{B_{1}}}{q_{1}}\sum_{d|q_{1}q}\tau(d)^{B_{2}}\frac{(q_{1},q)}{q}\leqslant\frac{\tau(q)^{B_{2}+1}}{q}\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{1}\leqslant X_{1}\\
\mu^{2}(q_{1})=1\end{subarray}}\!\!\frac{\tau(q_{1})^{B_{1}+B_{2}+1}(q_{1},q)}{q_{1}}.

Here, we used the rather crude bound

\sum_{d|q_{1}q}\tau(d)^{B_{2}}\leqslant\tau(q_{1}q)\max_{d|q_{1}q}\tau(d)^{B_{2}}\leqslant\tau(q_{1}q)^{B_{2}+1}\leqslant\tau(q_{1})^{B_{2}+1}\tau(q)^{B_{2}+1}.

The result now follows from Lemma
B.2
.
∎

8.5.
The functions
\Phi_{r,b}

We now turn to a different example of a Fourier-positive function, which will feature heavily later on since they are important in constructing the damping function
D
.

Let
r
be any positive integer, and let
b\in\mathbf{Z}/r^{3}\mathbf{Z}
. Then we define

\Phi_{r,b}(x):=r^{\frac{5}{6}}1_{r|x}e\big(\frac{bx}{r^{3}}\big).

(8.24)

Remark.
Here, the constants
\frac{5}{6}
and
3
are convenient choices which work for our later arguments. Any
0<c<1<C
with
c
sufficiently close to
1
and
C
sufficiently large would work, though the exponent in the main theorem would be affected.

The functions
\Phi_{r,b}
are of course periodic. They are also Fourier-positive, since both
1_{r|x}
and
e(\frac{bx}{r^{3}})
are. Indeed,

\Phi_{r,b}(n)=r^{-\frac{1}{6}}\sum_{t=0}^{r-1}e\big(\big(\frac{t}{r}+\frac{b}{r^{3}}\big)n\big).

(8.25)

Note in particular that

\|\Phi_{r,b}\|^{\wedge}_{1}=r^{\frac{5}{6}}

(8.26)

and

\operatorname{s}(\Phi_{r,b})\leqslant r^{3}.

(8.27)

9.
Triple correlations of Ramanujan series

We turn now to a key technical result concerning the relationship between certain products of shifted Ramanujan series (as defined in (
8.5
)) and their truncations. Here is the main result.

Proposition 9.1
.

Let
h_{1},h_{2},h_{3}
be distinct integers with
\max_{i}|h_{i}|\leqslant H
.
Consider three Ramanujan series

f_{i}(n)=\sum_{q}\alpha_{i}(q)c_{q}(n),

i=1,2,3
, such that
\alpha_{i}(q)
is supported on finitely many squarefrees and satisfies the bound
|\alpha_{i}(q)|\leqslant\tau^{B}(q)/q
, where
\tau
is the divisor function and
B
is some parameter. Consider also their truncations

f_{i,X_{i}}(n):=\sum_{q\leqslant X_{i}}\alpha_{i}(q)c_{q}(n),

where
X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}
are some thresholds. Set
X:=\min(X_{1},X_{2},X_{3})
.
Consider the shifted products

F(n):=f_{1}(n+h_{1})f_{2}(n+h_{2})f_{3}(n+h_{3})

and the truncated variants

F_{X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}}(n):=f_{1,X_{1}}(n+h_{1})f_{2,X_{2}}(n+h_{2})f_{3,X_{3}}(n+h_{3}).

Then

\|F-F_{X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}}\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}\ll_{B,\varepsilon}H^{3}X^{\varepsilon-1},

(9.1)

for every
\varepsilon>0
.

Remark.
Most likely, this proposition could be extended to products of an arbitrary number of
f_{i}
(rather than just 3) but we do not need any such extension in this paper.

We need the following lemma in the proof.

Lemma 9.2
.

Let
m_{1},m_{2}
be distinct, nonzero integers. Let
r
be a positive integer and suppose that
b\in(\mathbf{Z}/r\mathbf{Z})^{*}
. Let
q_{1},q_{2}
be squarefree, and suppose that
P
is an integer with
P\mid[q_{1},q_{2}]r
. Then

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1}\in(\mathbf{Z}/q_{1}\mathbf{Z})^{*}\\
a_{2}\in(\mathbf{Z}/q_{2}\mathbf{Z})^{*}\\
a_{1}q_{2}r+a_{2}q_{1}r\equiv bq_{1}q_{2}(\mbox{\scriptsize{mod}}\,P(q_{1},q_{2}))\end{subarray}}e\big(\frac{m_{1}a_{1}}{q_{1}}+\frac{m_{2}a_{2}}{q_{2}}\big)\leqslant|m_{1}m_{2}(m_{1}-m_{2})|\tau(P)^{2}.

Proof.

Set
q^{\prime}_{1}=q_{1}/(q_{1},q_{2})
,
q^{\prime}_{2}=q_{2}/(q_{1},q_{2})
,
d:=P/(P,r)
and
r^{\prime}:=r/(P,r)
. The congruence
a_{1}q_{2}r+a_{2}q_{1}r\equiv bq_{1}q_{2}(\operatorname{mod}\,P(q_{1},q_{2}))
is equivalent to
a_{1}q^{\prime}_{2}r+a_{2}q^{\prime}_{1}r\equiv b[q_{1},q_{2}](\operatorname{mod}\,P)
. Moreover, since
(P,r)
divides both
P
and
r
, if the congruence has a solution at all then
b[q_{1},q_{2}]=v(P,r)
for some
v
, and so the congruence is equivalent to
a_{1}q^{\prime}_{2}r^{\prime}+a_{2}q^{\prime}_{1}r^{\prime}\equiv v(\operatorname{mod}\,d)
. Note that
r^{\prime}
is coprime to
d
, so the congruence is equivalent to
a_{1}q^{\prime}_{2}+a_{2}q^{\prime}_{1}\equiv u(\operatorname{mod}\,d)
, where
u:=v(r^{\prime})^{-1}(\operatorname{mod}\,d)
. Thus our task is to show that

S:=\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1}\in(\mathbf{Z}/q_{1}\mathbf{Z})^{*}\\
a_{2}\in(\mathbf{Z}/q_{2}\mathbf{Z})^{*}\\
a_{1}q^{\prime}_{2}+a_{2}q^{\prime}_{1}\equiv u(\mbox{\scriptsize{mod}}\,d)\end{subarray}}e\big(\frac{m_{1}a_{1}}{q_{1}}+\frac{m_{2}a_{2}}{q_{2}}\big)\leqslant|m_{1}m_{2}(m_{1}-m_{2})|\tau(P)^{2}.

(9.2)

We claim that
d\mid[q_{1},q_{2}]
. To see this, recall that
P\mid[q_{1},q_{2}]r
. Therefore
d=P/(P,r)
divides
[q_{1},q_{2}]r/(P,r)=[q_{1},q_{2}]r^{\prime}
. However,
d
and
r^{\prime}
are coprime, so indeed
d\mid[q_{1},q_{2}]
.

Detecting the congruence
a_{1}q^{\prime}_{2}+a_{2}q^{\prime}_{1}\equiv u(\operatorname{mod}\,d)
using additive characters mod
d
, the sum to be estimated is then

S=\frac{1}{d}\!\sum_{\lambda(\mbox{\scriptsize mod}\,d)}\!e\big(-\!\frac{\lambda u}{d}\big)\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1}\in(\mathbf{Z}/q_{1}\mathbf{Z})^{*}\\
a_{2}\in(\mathbf{Z}/q_{2}\mathbf{Z})^{*}\end{subarray}}e\big(\frac{m_{1}a_{1}}{q_{1}}+\frac{m_{2}a_{2}}{q_{2}}+\frac{\lambda(a_{1}q^{\prime}_{2}+a_{2}q^{\prime}_{1})}{d}\big).

(9.3)

Set
t:=[q_{1},q_{2}]/d
(so
t
is an integer, by the above discussion) and observe that

\frac{q^{\prime}_{2}}{d}=\frac{q_{1}q^{\prime}_{2}}{dq_{1}}=\frac{[q_{1},q_{2}]/d}{q_{1}}=\frac{t}{q_{1}},

and similarly
q^{\prime}_{1}/d=t/q_{2}
. Using these expressions, the sum (
9.3
) can be rewritten as

S=\frac{1}{d}\!\sum_{\lambda(\mbox{\scriptsize mod}\,d)}\!e\big(-\!\frac{\lambda u}{d}\big)\!\!\!\sum_{a_{1}\in(\mathbf{Z}/q_{1}\mathbf{Z})^{*}}\!\!\!\!\!e\big(\frac{a_{1}(m_{1}+\lambda t)}{q_{1}}\big)\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{a_{2}\in(\mathbf{Z}/q_{2}\mathbf{Z})^{*}}\!\!\!\!\!e\big(\frac{a_{2}(m_{2}+\lambda t)}{q_{2}}\big).

(9.4)

Now we use the inequality

\big|\sum_{a\in(\mathbf{Z}/q\mathbf{Z})^{*}}e\big(\frac{an}{q}\big)\big|\leqslant(q,n),

which is a standard bound for Ramanujan sums (see
[
12
, equation (3.5)]
or
[
10
, Section 16.6]
). Substituting into (
9.4
) gives

|S|\leqslant\frac{1}{d}\sum_{\lambda(\mbox{\scriptsize mod}\,d)}(q_{1},m_{1}+\lambda t)(q_{2},m_{2}+\lambda t).

(9.5)

Suppose that
p
is prime and that
p\mid(q_{1},m_{1}+\lambda t)
. Then certainly
p\mid q_{1}
. If
p\mid t
, then we must have
p\mid m_{1}
. It follows that either
p\mid\frac{q_{1}}{(q_{1},t)}
, or else
p\mid m_{1}
, and so (since
q_{1}
is squarefree)

(q_{1},m_{1}+\lambda t)\leqslant|m_{1}|\big(\frac{q_{1}}{(q_{1},t)},m_{1}+\lambda t\big).

(9.6)

Note moreover that since
t\mid[q_{1},q_{2}]=q^{\prime}_{2}q_{1}
we have
t\mid q^{\prime}_{2}(q_{1},t)
, and hence
\frac{q_{1}}{(q_{1},t)}=\frac{[q_{1},q_{2}]}{q^{\prime}_{2}(q_{1},t)}
divides
\frac{[q_{1},q_{2}]}{t}=d
. Therefore (
9.6
) implies

(q_{1},m_{1}+\lambda t)\leqslant|m_{1}|(d,m_{1}+\lambda t).

Similarly,

(q_{2},m_{2}+\lambda t)\leqslant|m_{2}|(d,m_{2}+\lambda t).

Substituting into (
9.5
) gives

|S|\leqslant\frac{|m_{1}m_{2}|}{d}\sum_{\lambda(\mbox{\scriptsize mod}\,d)}(d,m_{1}+\lambda t)(d,m_{2}+\lambda t).

(9.7)

Now since
t=[q_{1},q_{2}]/d
and
[q_{1},q_{2}]
is squarefree,
t
is coprime to
d
. Therefore it follows from (
9.7
) that

|S|\leqslant\frac{|m_{1}m_{2}|}{d}\sum_{s(\mbox{\scriptsize mod}\,d)}(d,s)(d,s+h),

(9.8)

where
h:=m_{2}-m_{1}\neq 0
. Without loss of generality,
h>0
.

Now for any
s
,
(d,s)
and
(d,s+h)
are both divisors of
d
, and any common factor of them must divide
h
. If
(d,s)=d_{1}
and
(d,s+h)=d_{2}
then
s\equiv 0(\operatorname{mod}\,d_{1})
and
s\equiv-h(\operatorname{mod}\,d_{2})
, which is a congruence condition (possibly not satisfiable) mod
[d_{1},d_{2}]
. Note that
[d_{1},d_{2}]=d_{1}d_{2}/(d_{1},d_{2})\geqslant d_{1}d_{2}/h
. Therefore, for a fixed choice of
d_{1},d_{2}
, the number of such
s
is at most
d/[d_{1},d_{2}]\leqslant hd/d_{1}d_{2}
. It follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{s(\mbox{\scriptsize mod}\,d)}(d,s)(d,

\displaystyle s+h)=

\displaystyle=\sum_{d_{1},d_{2}\mid d}d_{1}d_{2}\#\{s\in\mathbf{Z}/d\mathbf{Z}:(d,s)=d_{1},(d,s+h)=d_{2}\}

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{d_{1},d_{2}\mid d}d_{1}d_{2}\frac{hd}{d_{1}d_{2}}=hd\tau(d)^{2}.

Substituting into (
9.8
) gives
|S|\leqslant h|m_{1}m_{2}|\tau(d)^{2}
, which immediately implies the lemma.∎

Corollary 9.3
.

Let
m_{1},m_{2}
be distinct nonzero integers. Let
r
be a positive integer and suppose that
b\in(\mathbf{Z}/r\mathbf{Z})^{*}
. Suppose that
q_{1},q_{2}
are squarefree. Then, uniformly in
q_{3}
,

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1}\in(\mathbf{Z}/q_{1}\mathbf{Z})^{*}\\
a_{2}\in(\mathbf{Z}/q_{2}\mathbf{Z})^{*}\\
\operatorname{denom}(\frac{a_{1}}{q_{1}}+\frac{a_{2}}{q_{2}}-\frac{b}{r})=q_{3}\end{subarray}}e\big(\frac{m_{1}a_{1}}{q_{1}}+\frac{m_{2}a_{2}}{q_{2}}\big)\leqslant|m_{1}m_{2}(m_{1}-m_{2})|\tau(r[q_{1},q_{2}])^{3}.

Proof.

The condition
\operatorname{denom}(\frac{a_{1}}{q_{1}}+\frac{a_{2}}{q_{2}}-\frac{b}{r})=q_{3}
is equivalent to
(a_{1}q_{2}r+a_{2}q_{1}r-bq_{1}q_{2},q_{1}q_{2}r)=\frac{rq_{1}q_{2}}{q_{3}}
(note it can only be satisfied if
q_{3}\mid rq_{1}q_{2}
, which implies
q_{3}\mid r[q_{1},q_{2}]
since
q_{3}
is squarefree).

We may detect this condition by Möbius inversion, specifically the version in Lemma
B.5
. Taking
m=a_{1}q_{2}r+a_{2}q_{1}r-bq_{1}q_{2}
,
n=rq_{1}q_{2}
and
\Delta=rq_{1}q_{2}/q_{3}
in that lemma gives

\displaystyle 1_{\operatorname{denom}(\frac{a_{1}}{q_{1}}+\frac{a_{2}}{q_{2}}-\frac{b}{r})=q_{3}}=1_{(a_{1}q_{2}r+a_{2}q_{1}r-bq_{1}q_{2},rq_{1},q_{2})=\frac{rq_{1}q_{2}}{q_{3}}}

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{\frac{rq_{1}q_{2}}{q_{3}}\mid d\mid rq_{1}q_{2}}\mu\big(\frac{d}{rq_{1}q_{2}/q_{3}}\big)1_{a_{1}q_{2}r+a_{2}q_{1}r\equiv bq_{1}q_{2}(\mbox{\scriptsize mod}\,d)}

\displaystyle\qquad=\sum_{\frac{r[q_{1},q_{2}]}{q_{3}}\mid P\mid r[q_{1},q_{2}]}\mu\big(\frac{P}{r[q_{1},q_{2}]/q_{3}}\big)1_{a_{1}q_{2}r+a_{2}q_{1}r\equiv bq_{1}q_{2}(\mbox{\scriptsize mod}\,P(q_{1},q_{2}))},

where
P:=d/(q_{1},q_{2})
(which is an integer).
Multiplying this by
e(\frac{m_{1}a_{1}}{q_{1}}+\frac{m_{2}a_{2}}{q_{2}})
and applying Lemma
9.2
for each value of
P
(of which there are at most
\tau([q_{1},q_{2}]r)
) gives the result.
∎

Lemma 9.4
.

Let
B\geqslant 0
and
X\geqslant 1
be parameters and let
r\geqslant 1
be an integer. Then

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\max(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3})\geqslant X\\
\mu^{2}(q_{1})=\mu^{2}(q_{2})=\mu^{2}(q_{3})=1\\
q_{1}\mid rq_{2}q_{3}\\
q_{2}\mid rq_{1}q_{3}\\
q_{3}\mid rq_{1}q_{2}\end{subarray}}\frac{\tau(q_{1})^{B}}{q_{1}}\frac{\tau(q_{2})^{B}}{q_{2}}\frac{\tau(q_{3})^{B}}{q_{3}}\ll_{B,\varepsilon}r^{\varepsilon}X^{\varepsilon-1}.

(9.9)

Proof.

By symmetry it suffices to handle the sum over those
q_{1},q_{2},q_{3}
for which
q_{1}
is biggest (and hence
q_{1}\geqslant X
). The condition
q_{1}\mid rq_{2}q_{3}
implies that
\frac{q_{1}}{(q_{1},rq_{2})}\mid q_{3}
, and so in particular
q_{3}\geqslant\frac{q_{1}}{(q_{1},rq_{2})}
.

Given
q_{1},q_{2}
, since
q_{3}\mid rq_{1}q_{2}
it follows that there are at most
\tau(rq_{1}q_{2})\leqslant\tau(r)\tau(q_{1})\tau(q_{2})
choices for
q_{3}
. It also follows that, for each permissible
q_{3}
,
\tau(q_{3})\leqslant\tau(r)\tau(q_{1})\tau(q_{2})
. It follows that the LHS of (
9.9
) is bounded above by

\displaystyle\tau(r)^{B+1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{1}\geqslant X\\
q_{2}\leqslant q_{1}\\
\mu^{2}(q_{1})=\mu^{2}(q_{2})=1\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle\frac{\tau(q_{1})^{2B+1}\tau(q_{2})^{2B+1}}{q_{1}q_{2}\frac{q_{1}}{(q_{1},rq_{2})}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{1}\geqslant X\\
\mu^{2}(q_{1})=1\end{subarray}}\frac{\tau(q_{1})^{2B+1}}{q_{1}^{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{2}\leqslant q_{1}\\
\mu^{2}(q_{2})=1\end{subarray}}\frac{\tau(q_{2})^{2B+1}(q_{1},rq_{2})}{q_{2}}.

By Lemma
B.2
(noting that
(q_{1},rq_{2})=(q_{1},r)(\frac{q_{1}}{(q_{1},r)},q_{2})
, and taking
h=\frac{q_{1}}{(q_{1},r)}
in that lemma), the inner sum is
\ll(q_{1},r)\tau(q_{1})^{2B+2}\log^{O_{B}(1)}q_{1}
.
Therefore the LHS of (
9.9
) is bounded above by

\tau(r)^{B+1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}q_{1}\geqslant X\\
\mu^{2}(q_{1})=1\end{subarray}}\frac{(q_{1},r)\tau(q_{1})^{4B+3}\log^{O_{B}(1)}q_{1}}{q_{1}^{2}}

By Lemma
B.3
(and a further application of the divisor bound
\tau(r)\ll_{\varepsilon}r^{\varepsilon}
) this is indeed bounded above by
\ll_{B,\varepsilon}r^{\varepsilon}X^{\varepsilon-1}
, which is what we wanted to prove.
∎

Proof of Proposition
9.1
.

From the definitions of
F
and
F_{X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}}
in the statement of the proposition one may check that

\displaystyle(F-F_{X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}})^{\wedge}

\displaystyle(\lambda)=\sum_{\max(\frac{q_{1}}{X_{1}},\frac{q_{2}}{X_{2}},\frac{q_{3}}{X_{3}})>1}\alpha(q_{1})\alpha(q_{2})\alpha(q_{3})\times

\displaystyle\times\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{i}\in(\mathbf{Z}/q_{i}\mathbf{Z})^{*}\\
\frac{a_{1}}{q_{1}}+\frac{a_{2}}{q_{2}}+\frac{a_{3}}{q_{3}}\equiv\lambda(\mbox{\scriptsize mod}\,1)\end{subarray}}e\big(\frac{h_{1}a_{1}}{q_{1}}+\frac{h_{2}a_{2}}{q_{2}}+\frac{h_{3}a_{3}}{q_{3}}\big).

(9.10)

Using this, we will show first that

|(F-F_{X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}})^{\wedge}(\lambda)|\ll_{B,\varepsilon}H^{3}\operatorname{denom}(\lambda)^{\varepsilon}X^{\varepsilon-1}.

(9.11)

This statement is substantial progress towards Proposition
9.1
. To handle the case where
\operatorname{denom}(\lambda)
is large, however, we need an additional argument using (
9.11
) and Lemma
8.12
.

We turn to the proof of (
9.11
). Suppose that
\lambda=\frac{b}{r}
with
b,r
coprime. If
\frac{a_{1}}{q_{1}}+\frac{a_{2}}{q_{2}}+\frac{a_{3}}{q_{3}}\equiv\lambda(\operatorname{mod}\,1)
then
q_{1}\mid rq_{2}q_{3}
,
q_{2}\mid rq_{1}q_{3}
and
q_{3}\mid rq_{1}q_{2}
. Therefore the RHS of (
9.10
) may be rewritten as
e(h_{3}b/r)
times

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\max(\frac{q_{1}}{X_{1}},\frac{q_{2}}{X_{2}},\frac{q_{3}}{X_{3}})>1\\
q_{1}|rq_{2}q_{3}\\
q_{2}|rq_{1}q_{3}\\
q_{3}|rq_{1}q_{2}\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\alpha(q_{1})\alpha(q_{2})\alpha(q_{3})\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}a_{1}\in(\mathbf{Z}/q_{1}\mathbf{Z})^{*}\\
a_{2}\in(\mathbf{Z}/q_{2}\mathbf{Z})^{*}\\
\operatorname{denom}(\frac{a_{1}}{q_{1}}+\frac{a_{2}}{q_{2}}-\frac{b}{r})=q_{3}\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!e\big(\frac{(h_{1}-h_{3})a_{1}}{q_{1}}+\frac{(h_{2}-h_{3})a_{2}}{q_{2}}\big).

Note that the summation over
a_{3}
has disappeared since
a_{3}
is uniquely determined by
a_{1},q_{1},a_{2},q_{2}
, and the condition
\operatorname{denom}(\frac{a_{1}}{q_{1}}+\frac{a_{2}}{q_{2}}-\frac{b}{r})=q_{3}
guarantees that this
a_{3}
is coprime to
q_{3}
.
Since
h_{1},h_{2},h_{3}
are distinct, the inner sum is exactly of the type considered in Corollary
9.3
, and by that result it is bounded by
O(H^{3}\tau(r[q_{1},q_{2}])^{3})
. It follows that

|(F-F_{X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}})^{\wedge}(\lambda)|\ll H^{3}\tau(r)^{3}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\max(\frac{q_{1}}{X_{1}},\frac{q_{2}}{X_{2}},\frac{q_{3}}{X_{3}})>1\\
q_{1}|rq_{2}q_{3}\\
q_{2}|rq_{1}q_{3}\\
q_{3}|rq_{1}q_{2}\end{subarray}}\!\!\!|\alpha(q_{1})\alpha(q_{2})\alpha(q_{3})|\tau([q_{1},q_{2}])^{3}.

By assumption,
|\alpha(q)|\leqslant\tau(q)^{B}/q
for all
q
, and
\alpha
is supported on squarefrees, and so this is bounded above by

O(H^{3}\tau(r)^{3})\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\max(q_{1},q_{2},q_{3})\geqslant X\\
\\
\mu^{2}(q_{1})=\mu^{2}(q_{2})=\mu^{2}(q_{3})=1\\
q_{1}|rq_{2}q_{3}\\
q_{2}|rq_{1}q_{3}\\
q_{3}|rq_{1}q_{2}\end{subarray}}\frac{\tau(q_{1})^{B+3}}{q_{1}}\frac{\tau(q_{2})^{B+3}}{q_{2}}\frac{\tau(q_{3})^{B+3}}{q_{3}}.

Applying Lemma
9.4
and the divisor bound gives the claimed bound (
9.11
).

Now we supply the further arguments necessary to handle the case where
\operatorname{denom}(\lambda)
is large. Fix some
\lambda
and write
r:=\operatorname{denom}(\lambda)
. Then (
9.11
) gives

|(F-F_{r,r,r})^{\wedge}(\lambda)|\ll_{B,\varepsilon}H^{3}r^{\varepsilon-1}.

(9.12)

Now
F_{r,r,r}
is the product of three functions in the class
\mathscr{C}_{B}(r)
, as defined in Definition
8.10
. By (two applications of) Lemma
8.12
,
F_{r,r,r}\in(\log^{O_{B}(1)}r)\mathscr{C}_{5B+6}(r^{3})
, which means that

|F_{r,r,r}^{\wedge}(\lambda)|\ll_{B,\varepsilon}r^{\varepsilon-1}.

Together with (
9.12
), this implies that

|F^{\wedge}(\lambda)|\ll_{B,\varepsilon}H^{3}r^{\varepsilon-1}.

The same bound also applies to
F_{X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}}^{\wedge}(\lambda)
, simply by replacing the
\alpha_{i}
in the definition of
F
by
\alpha^{\prime}_{i}(n):=\alpha_{i}(n)1_{n\leqslant X_{i}}
. By the triangle inequality (and recalling that
r=\operatorname{denom}(\lambda)
) it follows that

|(F-F_{X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}})^{\wedge}(\lambda)|\ll_{B,\varepsilon}H^{3}\operatorname{denom}(\lambda)^{\varepsilon-1}.

(9.13)

We now have the desired bound
|(F-F_{X_{1},X_{2},X_{3}})^{\wedge}(\lambda)|\ll_{B,\varepsilon}H^{3}X^{\varepsilon-1}
in all cases: if
\operatorname{denom}(\lambda)\leqslant X
, apply (
9.11
), whilst if
\operatorname{denom}(\lambda)>X
, apply (
9.13
).

This completes the proof of Proposition
9.1
.∎

10.
Triple correlations with characters

In this section we build upon the previous section by introducing characters. The main result of the section (and the only one we will need elsewhere) is the following.

Proposition 10.1
.

Let
Q,R
be parameters and suppose that
1\leqslant Q\leqslant Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3}\leqslant R
. Let
\chi
be a primitive Dirichlet character of conductor
q\leqslant R
. Then
we have

\|F^{+}_{\chi,Q_{1}}\Lambda_{Q_{2}}^{-}H_{Q_{3}}-\tilde{F}^{+}_{\chi,R}\tilde{\Lambda}^{-}_{R}\tilde{H}_{R}\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}\ll R^{o(1)}Q^{-\frac{1}{4}}.

The reader may want to take the opportunity to recall the definitions of the various objects appearing here, which may be located as follows:

•

F_{\chi,Q}
is a key object of the paper whose definition is given in Definition
4.3
. It has an alternative expression given in (
6.6
);

•

\tilde{F}_{\chi,R}
is a “completed” version of
F
, defined in Definition
6.1
and with an alternative expression in (
6.5
);

•

\Lambda_{Q}
is a classical approximant to the von Mangoldt function, defined in (
3.3
);

•

\tilde{\Lambda}_{R}
is the completed version of this, defined in (
6.10
) and with an alternative expression in (
8.21
), which exhibits it as a normalised characteristic function of the
R
-rough numbers;

•

H_{Q}
is another key object in the paper, defined in Definition
8.9
, and to be thought of as a kind of proxy for the square of the divisor function;

•

\tilde{H}_{R}
is a completed version of this, defined in Definition
8.8
.

Finally, recall that
f^{+}(n):=f(n+1)
and
f^{-}(n):=f(n-1)
, and that the basic properties of periodic functions and their Fourier series (and in particular the notation
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}^{\wedge}
) are laid out in Section
8
.

For the rest of the section we regard
\chi
and its conductor
q
as fixed, with
q\leqslant R
. Write (see Appendix
C
)
\chi=\prod_{p|q}\chi_{p}
, where
\chi_{p}
is a Dirichlet character of conductor
p^{\beta_{p}}
, where
q=\prod_{p|q}p^{\beta_{p}}
.

We divide the proof of Proposition
10.1
into two parts, according to whether or not
q\geqslant Q^{\frac{1}{2}}
.

10.1.
The case of large conductor

In this section we give some estimates which imply Proposition
10.1
when
q\geqslant Q^{\frac{1}{2}}
. Recall the definition (
8.13
) of
\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}
. It is also convenient to introduce the local factors
\chi_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}
defined by
\chi_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}=\frac{p}{p-1}\chi_{p}
if
p\mid q
, and
\chi_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}=\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}
if
p\nmid q
. Then (
6.5
), (
6.10
) can be written in the form

\tilde{F}_{\chi,R}=\prod_{p\leqslant R}\overline{\chi}_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}},\qquad\tilde{\Lambda}_{R}=\prod_{p\leqslant R}\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}.

(10.1)

Our first estimate deals with the completed terms in Proposition
10.1
.

Lemma 10.2
.

We have
\|\tilde{F}^{+}_{\chi,R}\tilde{\Lambda}_{R}^{-}\tilde{H}_{R}\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}\ll\tau(q)^{16}q^{-\frac{1}{2}}
.

Proof.

By (
10.1
) and (
8.9
), we have

\|\tilde{F}^{+}_{\chi,R}\tilde{\Lambda}_{R}^{-}\tilde{H}_{R}\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}\leqslant\prod_{p\leqslant R}\|f_{p}\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}.

(10.2)

where
f_{p}:=\overline{\chi}_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}^{+}\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}^{-}\tau^{2}_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}
. When
p\nmid q
we have
\chi_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}=\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}
, and so for these
p
we have
\|f_{p}\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}\leqslant 1+O(p^{-2})
by (
8.19
). The product over
p
of all these contributions is
O(1)
.

When
p\mid q
,
f_{p}=\frac{p}{p-1}\overline{\chi}_{p}^{+}\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}^{-}\tau^{2}_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}
, and so by (
8.8
) we have

\|f_{p}\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}\leqslant\frac{p}{p-1}\|\chi_{p}\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}\|\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}^{-}\tau^{2}_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}\|^{\wedge}_{1}.

(10.3)

By Lemma
8.4
we have

\|\chi_{p}\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}\leqslant p^{-\beta_{p}/2}.

(10.4)

By (
8.7
) and (
8.17
), (
8.18
) we have
\|\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}^{-}\tau^{2}_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}\|^{\wedge}_{1}\leqslant 8
.
Substituting this and (
10.4
) into (
10.3
), and using the crude bound
\frac{p}{p-1}\leqslant 2
, we obtain
\|f_{p}\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}\leqslant 16p^{-\beta_{p}/2}
.

Taking products over
p
, we see that the right-hand side of (
10.2
) is bounded by
\ll 16^{\omega(q)}q^{-1/2}
, where
\omega(q)
denotes the number of distinct prime factors of
q
, which implies the claimed bound.∎

The second result of this section, which is a little harder, deals with the uncompleted terms in Proposition
10.1
.

Lemma 10.3
.

Suppose that
1\leqslant Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3}\leqslant R
. Then

\|F^{+}_{\chi,Q_{1}}\Lambda_{Q_{2}}^{-}H_{Q_{3}}\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}\ll\tau(q)^{O(1)}(\log^{O(1)}R)q^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

(10.5)

Proof.

Introduce

f_{Q}(n)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r\leqslant Q\\
(r,q)=1\end{subarray}}\frac{\mu(r)}{\phi(r)}c_{r}(n).

(10.6)

(Of course, this definition depends on
q
, but we are regarding
q
as fixed throughout the section and so suppress the dependence to keep the notation manageable.) By (
6.6
) we have

F_{\chi,Q}=\frac{q}{\phi(q)}\overline{\chi}f_{Q}.

(10.7)

Consider the definition of
\Lambda_{Q}
, viz

\Lambda_{Q}(n)=\sum_{r\leqslant Q}\frac{\mu(r)}{\phi(r)}c_{r}(n).

(10.8)

Since the sum is supported where
r
is squarefree, each
r
in the sum may be written uniquely as
r=dr^{\prime}
where
d\mid q
and
(r^{\prime},q)=1
. By the multiplicative property
c_{r}(n)=c_{d}(n)c_{r^{\prime}}(n)
the contribution from a particular value of
d
(to the sum over
r
in (
10.8
)) is then

\frac{\mu(d)}{\phi(d)}c_{d}(n)\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r^{\prime}\leqslant Q/d\\
(r^{\prime},q)=1\end{subarray}}\frac{\mu(r^{\prime})}{\phi(r^{\prime})}c_{r^{\prime}}(n).

It follows that

\Lambda_{Q}=\sum_{d|q}\frac{\mu(d)}{\phi(d)}c_{d}f_{Q/d}.

(10.9)

(Note, here and in what follows,
c_{d}
is the Ramanujan sum considered as a function.)

Now we expand
F_{\chi,Q_{1}}^{+}\Lambda_{Q_{2}}^{-}
using (
10.7
) and (
10.9
), obtaining

F^{+}_{\chi,Q_{1}}\Lambda^{-}_{Q_{2}}=\frac{q}{\phi(q)}\sum_{d|q}\frac{\mu(d)}{\phi(d)}\overline{\chi}^{+}c_{d}^{-}f_{Q_{1}}^{+}f_{Q_{2}/d}^{-}.

(10.10)

Recall from Definition
8.9
that
H_{Q_{3}}=\sum_{r\leqslant Q_{3}}\eta(r)c_{r}
where
\eta(r)=\prod_{p|r}\frac{3}{p+3}
and
r
is restricted to squarefrees.
Summing according to
e:=(r,q)
, we see that
H_{Q}=\sum_{e|q}\eta(e)c_{e}h_{Q_{3}/e}
, where

h_{Y}:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r^{\prime}\leqslant Y\\
(r^{\prime},q)=1\end{subarray}}\eta(r^{\prime})c_{r^{\prime}}.

(10.11)

Thus from (
10.10
) we obtain

F^{+}_{\chi,Q_{1}}\Lambda^{-}_{Q_{2}}H_{Q_{3}}=\frac{q}{\phi(q)}\sum_{d,e|q}\frac{\mu(d)}{\phi(d)}\eta(e)\overline{\chi}^{+}c_{d}^{-}c_{e}f_{Q_{1}}^{+}f_{Q_{2}/d}^{-}h_{Q_{3}/e}.

(10.12)

Consider first the term
f^{+}_{Q_{1}}f^{-}_{Q_{2}/d}h_{Q_{3}/e}
. The three functions
f_{Q_{1}}
,
f_{Q_{2}/d}
and
h_{Q_{3}/e}
all lie in the class
\mathscr{C}_{2}(R)
, as defined in Definition
8.10
, by a trivial modification of the proof of Lemma
8.11
. Since this class is invariant under shifts, it follows by two applications of Lemma
8.12
that
f^{+}_{Q_{1}}f^{-}_{Q_{2}/d}h_{Q_{3}/e}\in(\log^{O(1)}R)\mathscr{C}_{16}(R)
, and so in particular

\|f^{+}_{Q_{1}}f^{-}_{Q_{2}/d}h_{Q_{3}/e}\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}\ll\log^{O(1)}R.

(10.13)

Moreover, the rational Fourier expansion of this term has only frequencies which are coprime to
q
.

Now let us examine the other terms in (
10.12
). For fixed
d,e
(squarefree, else they are not relevant in (
10.12
)) we have the factorisation over prime powers

\frac{\mu(d)}{\phi(d)}\eta(e)\overline{\chi}^{+}c_{d}^{-}c_{e}=\prod_{p|q}b^{(d,e)}_{p}

(10.14)

where, for each
p
dividing
q
,
b^{(d,e)}_{p}
is a product of the following periodic functions:

•

\overline{\chi}^{+}_{p}
;

•

-\frac{1}{p-1}c_{p}^{-}
(if
p\mid d
) or
1
(otherwise);

•

\frac{3}{p+3}c_{p}
(if
p\mid e
) or
1
(otherwise).

Recall that
c_{p}(n)=-1
if
p\nmid n
, and
c_{p}(n)=p-1
if
p\mid n
.

Now if
z
is any of the four possible products of the periodic functions (
-\frac{1}{p-1}c^{-}_{p}
or
1
) and (
\frac{3}{p+3}c_{p}
or
1
) other than the trivial product
z=1\cdot 1
then we have
\operatorname{Av}|z|\ll\frac{1}{p}
and so (by (
8.3
))
\|z\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}\ll\frac{1}{p}
. Since these functions
z
all have
p
as a period, their rational Fourier transforms are supported on rationals
(\operatorname{mod}\,1)
with denominator
p
and so
\|z\|^{\wedge}_{1}\ll p\|z\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}\ll 1
. This bound also holds when
z=1
, when in fact
\|z\|^{\wedge}_{1}=1
.

Since, if
p\mid q
,
b^{(d,e)}_{p}=\overline{\chi}_{p}^{+}z
for one of the four possible choices of
z
, it follows from this, the bound
\|\overline{\chi}_{p}\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}\leqslant p^{-\beta_{p}/2}
(cf. (
10.4
)) and (
8.8
) that

\|b_{p}^{(d,e)}\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}\leqslant\|\overline{\chi}_{p}^{+}\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}\|z\|^{\wedge}_{1}\leqslant Cp^{-\beta_{p}/2}.

Taking products over
p|q
and using (
8.9
) gives, by (
10.14
),

\big\|\frac{\mu(d)}{\phi(d)}\eta(e)\overline{\chi}^{+}c_{d}^{-}c_{e}\big\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}\leqslant C^{\omega(q)}q^{-1/2}\ll\tau(q)^{O(1)}q^{-1/2}.

(10.15)

By (
8.9
) (noting that the expression in (
10.13
) has period coprime to
q
, whereas the expression in (
10.15
) has period
q
), we obtain

\big\|\frac{\mu(d)}{\phi(d)}\eta(e)\overline{\chi}^{+}c_{d}^{-}c_{e}f_{Q_{1}}^{+}f_{Q_{2}/d}^{-}h_{Q_{3}/e}\big\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}\ll\tau(q)^{O(1)}(\log^{O(1)}R)q^{-1/2},

uniformly in
d,e|q
. Finally, summing over the
\tau(q)^{2}
choices of
d,e
and using (
10.12
) and the (very) crude bound
\frac{q}{\phi(q)}\leqslant\tau(q)
, we obtain (
10.5
).
∎

To conclude this subsection, we observe that Lemmas
10.2
and
10.3
and the triangle inequality immediately imply Proposition
10.1
in the large conductor case
q\geqslant Q^{\frac{1}{2}}
.

10.2.
The case of small conductor

We turn now to the small conductor case
q\leqslant Q^{\frac{1}{2}}
. Let us look again at the expression (
10.12
) for the product
F_{\chi,Q_{1}}^{+}\Lambda_{Q_{2}}^{-}H_{Q_{3}}
.

We will compare this with a corresponding expression for
\tilde{F}_{\chi,R}^{+}\tilde{\Lambda}^{-}_{R}\tilde{H}_{R}
. To obtain such an expression, we first introduce

\tilde{f}_{R}(n):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r|R!\\
(r,q)=1\end{subarray}}\frac{\mu(r)}{\phi(r)}c_{r}(n)=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\leqslant R\\
p\nmid q\end{subarray}}\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}},

(10.16)

where the equivalence of the second and third expressions follows using multiplicativity and is explained after (
6.9
). By (
6.9
), we have

\tilde{F}_{\chi,R}=\frac{q}{\phi(q)}\overline{\chi}\tilde{f}_{R}.

(10.17)

Write

s:=\prod_{p|q}\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}.

(10.18)

Then, by (
8.21
),

\tilde{\Lambda}_{R}=s\tilde{f}_{R}.

(10.19)

Next, introduce

\tilde{h}(n)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}r|R!\\
(r,q)=1\end{subarray}}\eta(r)c_{r}(n)=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\leqslant R\\
p\nmid q\end{subarray}}\tau^{2}_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}.

(10.20)

The equivalence of the second and third terms here follows from (
8.16
) and the multiplicativity of Ramanujan sums. Finally, set

t:=\prod_{p|q}\tau^{2}_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}.

(10.21)

Then, by Definition
8.8
,

\tilde{H}_{R}=t\tilde{h}.

(10.22)

Taking products of (shifted versions of) (
10.17
), (
10.19
) and (
10.22
) gives

\tilde{F}_{\chi,R}^{+}\tilde{\Lambda}^{-}_{R}\tilde{H}_{R}=\frac{q}{\phi(q)}\overline{\chi}^{+}s^{-}t\tilde{f}_{R}^{+}\tilde{f}_{R}^{-}\tilde{h}.

(10.23)

Now let
d,e
be divisors of
q
(as in (
10.12
)). Observe that (for any
Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3},d,e
) the functions
f_{Q_{1}}
,
f_{Q_{2}/d}
and
h_{Q_{3}/e}
are Fourier-truncated versions, in the sense of Proposition
9.1
, of
\tilde{f}_{R}
,
\tilde{f}_{R}
and
\tilde{h}
respectively. This follows from simply inspecting the relevant definitions (
10.6
), (
10.11
), (
10.16
) and (
10.20
). Thus we may apply Proposition
9.1
with
X_{1}=Q_{1}
,
X_{2}=Q_{2}/d
,
X_{3}=Q_{3}/e
,
B=2
and
(h_{1},h_{2},h_{3})=(1,0,-1)
to get

\big\|f_{Q_{1}}^{+}f_{\frac{Q_{2}}{d}}^{-}h_{\frac{Q_{3}}{e}}-\tilde{f}_{R}^{+}\tilde{f}_{R}^{-}\tilde{h}\big\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}\ll X^{-1+o(1)}\ll Q^{-\frac{1}{2}+o(1)}.

(10.24)

Here
X:=\min(Q_{1},\frac{Q_{2}}{d},\frac{Q_{3}}{e})
, and
X\geqslant Q^{\frac{1}{2}}
since
d,e\leqslant q\leqslant Q^{\frac{1}{2}}
.

Now (if
d,e|q
) the Fourier support of
\frac{\mu(d)}{\phi(d)}\eta(e)\overline{\chi}^{+}c_{d}^{-}c_{e}
is on rationals whose denominators are composed of primes dividing
q
, whereas the Fourier support of
f_{Q_{1}}^{+}f_{Q_{2}/d}^{-}h_{Q_{3}/e}-\tilde{f}_{R}^{+}\tilde{f}_{R}^{-}\tilde{h}
is on rationals whose denominators are coprime to
q
, as follows from the definitions (
10.6
), (
10.11
), (
10.16
), (
10.20
). Therefore by (
8.9
), (
10.15
) and (
10.24
) we have

\displaystyle\big\|\frac{\mu(d)}{\phi(d)}\eta(e)\overline{\chi}^{+}c_{d}^{-}c_{e}(f_{Q_{1}}^{+}f_{\frac{Q_{2}}{d}}^{-}h_{\frac{Q_{3}}{e}}-\tilde{f}_{R}^{+}\tilde{f}_{R}^{-}\tilde{h})\big\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leqslant\big\|\frac{\mu(d)}{\phi(d)}\eta(e)\overline{\chi}^{+}c_{d}^{-}c_{e}\big\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}\big\|f_{Q_{1}}^{+}f_{\frac{Q_{2}}{d}}^{-}h_{\frac{Q_{3}}{e}}-\tilde{f}_{R}^{+}\tilde{f}_{R}^{-}\tilde{h}\big\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}\ll Q^{-\frac{1}{2}+o(1)}.

(10.25)

Now from the facts that
\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}=1+\frac{\mu(p)}{\phi(p)}c_{p}
(see (
8.15
)) and
\tau^{2}_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}=1+\eta(p)c_{p}
(see (
8.16
)), the definitions (
10.18
), (
10.21
) and the multiplicativity of
\mu,\phi,\eta
and of
c_{d}(n)
as a function of
d
, we have

\sum_{d|q}\frac{\mu(d)}{\phi(d)}c_{d}=s\quad\mbox{and}\quad\sum_{e|q}\eta(e)c_{e}=t.

Shifting the first equation by
-1
and then summing (
10.25
) over all divisors
d,e
of
q
, we obtain (by the triangle inequality)

\big\|\sum_{d,e|q}\frac{\mu(d)}{\phi(d)}\eta(e)\overline{\chi}^{+}c_{d}^{-}c_{e}f_{Q_{1}}^{+}f_{\frac{Q_{2}}{d}}^{-}h_{\frac{Q_{3}}{e}}-\overline{\chi}^{+}s^{-}t\tilde{f}_{R}^{+}\tilde{f}_{R}^{-}\tilde{h}\big\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}\ll\tau(q)^{2}Q^{-\frac{1}{2}+o(1)}.

Multiplying through by
\frac{q}{\phi(q)}
and comparing with (
10.12
), (
10.23
) we obtain

\|F^{+}_{\chi,Q_{1}}\Lambda^{-}_{Q_{2}}H_{Q_{3}}-\tilde{F}_{\chi,R}^{+}\tilde{\Lambda}^{-}_{R}\tilde{H}_{R}\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}\ll\tau(q)^{3}Q^{-\frac{1}{2}+o(1)},

which verifies Proposition
10.1
in the small conductor case.

11.
Damping characters

11.1.
Introduction

Before starting this section, the reader may wish to revisit the introductory discussion of Subsection
3.1
.

The main result of this section is Proposition
11.2
below. It is the key input in constructing our damping function
D
. We begin by trying to motivate this result, which is not an easy task.

One of the key issues we face in this paper is that a Dirichlet character
\chi
is not a Fourier-positive function. Indeed, if
\chi
is primitive of conductor
q
, its rational Fourier coefficients will have magnitude
q^{-\frac{1}{2}}
(by Lemma
8.4
) but will not always be positive reals. We do, however, have
\chi(n)\prec q^{\frac{1}{2}}1_{q|n}
, because the function on the right
is
Fourier-positive and its rational Fourier coefficients are
q^{-\frac{1}{2}}
at at rationals
\frac{r}{q}
. The function
q^{\frac{1}{2}}1_{q|n}
, whilst somewhat singular, is fairly small (of size
q^{-\frac{1}{2}}
) on average.

Now in our paper we are faced with a function, namely
\Lambda_{\sharp,T,\sigma_{0}}^{+}\Lambda_{Q}^{-}H_{Q}
, which is a sum of objects involving shifted characters
\chi^{+}(n)=\chi(n+1)
. It is complicated, so for the purposes of this motivating discussion suppose we have a function
f(n)=1-\sum_{j\geqslant 2}j^{-2}\chi^{+}_{j}(n)
, which is a convergent sum of Dirichlet characters of the same general flavour. Suppose
\operatorname{cond}(\chi_{j})=q_{j}
. Now as a first step we could consider
f(n)(1+q_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}1_{q_{1}|n})
, where the multiplying factor here is designed to dominate the Fourier oscillations from
\chi^{+}_{1}
. Unfortunately, though it achieves this aim, it introduces new cross-terms such as
-\frac{1}{4}\chi^{+}_{2}(n)(q_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}1_{q_{1}|n})
, which still have large (but not necessarily positive) Fourier coefficients.

To deal with this issue, we need a family of Fourier-positive functions like the
q^{\frac{1}{2}}1_{q|n}
, but with the property that
\chi^{+}
times any such function is Fourier-dominated by something like another function of the same type (where here
\operatorname{cond}(\chi)
and
q
might be different). Then, we can define
D
as a suitable sum of such functions.

A family of functions with something like this property is the set of convex combinations of the functions
\Phi_{q,a}
, which we introduced in Subsection
8.5
. Recall that
\Phi_{q,a}(n):=q^{\frac{5}{6}}1_{q|n}e(\frac{an}{q^{3}})
. The reader will note that this is somewhat akin to the function
q^{\frac{1}{2}}1_{q|n}
appearing in the heuristic discussion above, only it is even more singular (but still with a small
\ell^{1}
norm, of size
q^{-\frac{1}{6}}
) and now with an additional linear phase term. The need for the linear phase term arises from a resonance phenomenon which occurs when (for example), in the above heuristic discussion we have something like
q_{1}=p
and
\operatorname{cond}(\chi_{2})=p^{2}
. Restricted to
n
for which
p\mid n
,
\chi_{2}^{+}
behaves like a linear phase; this sort of phenomenon ultimately comes from the Postnikov character formula (see Appendix
A
).

Before turning to the precise statement of our main proposition, let us give some definitions.

With
N
the main global parameter in the paper, set

\Pi:=\prod_{p\leqslant N}p^{N}.

(11.1)

Definition 11.1
.

Denote by
\mathscr{F}
the class of all convex combinations
\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\Phi_{q_{i},a_{i}}
, where
q_{i}|\Pi
,
0\leqslant\alpha_{i}\leqslant 1
,
\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\leqslant 1
and
a_{i}\in\mathbf{Z}/q_{i}^{3}\mathbf{Z}
. Now let
\mathcal{P}
be some finite set of primes. Write
\mathscr{F}_{\mathcal{P}}
for the subclass spanned (as convex combinations) by those
\Phi_{q_{i},a_{i}}
where each
q_{i}
is divisible by all the primes in
\mathcal{P}
.

Remarks.
Every element of
\mathscr{F}
is Fourier-positive, since all the
\Phi_{q_{i},a_{i}}
are (by (
8.25
)) and by Proposition
8.1
(1). The role of
\Pi
(which could be replaced by any fixed sufficiently composite large number) is to ensure that there are only finitely many functions
\Phi_{q,a}
under consideration, and so the convex combinations may be assumed to be finite ones, and so every function in
\mathscr{F}
is periodic. This is a very helpful technical convenience later on, specifically in Section
13
, where we want to make sense of a convergent infinite sum of such functions.

Define
\nu(p)
,
p
a prime, by

\nu(p):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}7&\mbox{$p=2$};\\
3&\mbox{$3\leqslant p\leqslant 17$};\\
2&\mbox{$19\leqslant p\leqslant 2^{18}$};\\
1&\mbox{$p>2^{18}$}.\end{array}\right.

(11.2)

(The exact values are unimportant but ones which work are given for definiteness; the key feature is that
\nu(p)=1
for sufficiently large
p
.)
Set

M:=\prod_{p}\nu(p).

(11.3)

Here is the main result of the section.

Proposition 11.2
.

Let
\chi
be a primitive Dirichlet character with conductor
q
, and suppose that
q\mid\Pi
, where
\Pi
is defined in (
11.1
). Let
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}(\chi)
be the set of primes dividing
q
. Let
F\in\mathscr{F}
. Then there is some
\tilde{F}=\tilde{F}(F,\chi)\in\mathscr{F}_{\mathcal{P}}
such that
\chi^{+}1_{(n-1,q)=1}F\prec M\tilde{F}
, where
M
is the absolute constant defined in (
11.3
).

Remark.
The
1_{(n-1,q)=1}
term, which does not influence the analysis very much, is necessary later on. It did not feature in the heuristic discussion above.

The first main idea in the proof of Proposition
11.2
is to work one prime at a time, and then take a product over primes at the end. Most of the section is devoted to the case
q=p^{t}
a prime power.

11.2.
Prime power moduli – setup

Definition 11.3
.

For a given prime
p
and exponent
\beta\geqslant 0
, denote by
\mathscr{F}^{\beta}_{p}
the class of all finite convex combinations
\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\Phi_{p^{\beta_{i}},a_{i}}
, where
0\leqslant\alpha_{i}\leqslant 1
,
\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\leqslant 1
, the
\beta_{i}
are positive integers with
\beta\leqslant\beta_{i}\leqslant N
, and
a_{i}\in\mathbf{Z}/p^{3\beta_{i}}\mathbf{Z}
.

Remark.
The upper bound
\beta_{i}\leqslant N
ensures that
p^{\beta_{i}}\mid\Pi
, where
\Pi
is defined in (
11.1
), and so
\mathscr{F}^{\beta}_{p}\subseteq\mathscr{F}
; once again, the role of this upper bound is to ensure that the spaces under consideration are finite-dimensional.

Note that each
\mathscr{F}_{p}^{\beta}
is closed under convex combinations, and that we have the nesting

\mathscr{F}_{p}^{0}\supseteq\mathscr{F}_{p}^{1}\supseteq\cdots.

(11.4)

Every function in
\mathscr{F}_{p}^{0}
is periodic and Fourier-positive (by Proposition
8.1
(1) and (
8.25
)). Here is the main result for prime-power moduli.

Proposition 11.4
.

Let
p
be a prime, and let
\chi
be a primitive Dirichlet character to modulus
p^{t}
,
1\leqslant t\leqslant N
. Let
F\in\mathscr{F}^{\beta}_{p}
. Then there is some
\tilde{F}=\tilde{F}(F,\chi)\in\mathscr{F}^{\max(\beta,1)}_{p}
such that
\chi^{+}1_{(p,n-1)=1}F\prec\nu(p)\tilde{F}
, where
\nu(p)
is as defined in (
11.2
).

We make some initial reductions. First, it suffices to handle the case
F=\Phi_{p^{\beta},a}
(for arbitrary
\beta
, not necessarily the same
\beta
). Indeed, suppose we have this case and write
F=\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\Phi_{p^{\beta_{i}},a_{i}}
. Suppose that
\chi^{+}1_{(p,n-1)=1}\Phi_{p^{\beta_{i}},a_{i}}\prec\nu(p)\tilde{F}_{i}
where
\tilde{F}_{i}\in\mathscr{F}^{\max(\beta_{i},1)}_{p}
. By the nesting (
11.4
), and the fact that
\beta_{i}\geqslant\beta
, we have
\tilde{F}_{i}\in\mathscr{F}_{p}^{\max(\beta,1)}
. Then (using Proposition
8.1
(3) and (4)) we have
\chi^{+}1_{(p,n-1)=1}F\prec\nu(p)\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\tilde{F}_{i}
. Taking
\tilde{F}=\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\tilde{F}_{i}
completes the proof, noting that
\tilde{F}\in\mathscr{F}_{p}^{\max(\beta,1)}
because of the closure of this class under convex combinations.

We may further reduce to the case
a=0
, that is to say
F(n)=p^{5\beta/6}1_{p^{\beta}|n}
. Indeed, if for this
F
,
\chi^{+}1_{(n-1,p)=1}F\prec\nu(p)\tilde{F}
for some
\tilde{F}\in\mathscr{F}_{p}^{\max(\beta,1)}
then (by Proposition
8.1
(5))

\chi^{+}(n)1_{(n-1,p)=1}F(n)e\big(\frac{an}{p^{3\beta}}\big)\prec\nu(p)\tilde{F}(n)e\big(\frac{an}{p^{3\beta}}\big).

The function
\tilde{F}(n)e(\frac{an}{p^{3\beta}})
can be written as a convex combination of functions of the form
\Phi_{p^{\tilde{\beta}},\tilde{a}}(n)e(\frac{an}{p^{3\beta}})=\Phi_{p^{\tilde{\beta}},b}(n)
where
b:=\tilde{a}+p^{3(\tilde{\beta}-\beta)}a
, where
\tilde{\beta}\geqslant\max(\beta,1)
.

These reductions reduce the task of proving Proposition
11.4
to the following.

Proposition 11.5
.

Let
\chi
be a primitive Dirichlet character modulo
p^{t}
,
t\geqslant 1
. Let
F(n)=p^{5\beta/6}1_{p^{\beta}|n}
, where
\beta\geqslant 0
. Then there is some
\tilde{F}=\tilde{F}(F,\chi)\in\mathscr{F}_{p}^{\max(\beta,1)}
such that
\chi^{+}1_{(n-1,p)=1}F\prec\nu(p)\tilde{F}
.

The proof of this falls into two somewhat distinct parts, according to the size of
\beta
. When
\beta\leqslant t/3
we can use Gauss sum estimates: see subsection
11.3
. When
\beta>t/3
, if
p
is odd we use the fact that the values
\chi(1\pm p^{\beta}k)
are polynomial phases in
k
of degree at most
2
by a special case of a well-known result of Postnikov. The case when
t/3<\beta<t/2
, where the aforementioned phases are quadratic, is the most delicate part of the argument. For the details, see subsection
11.4
.

11.3.
The case of small
\beta

The case
\beta\leqslant t/3
of Proposition
11.5
can be handled with Gauss sum estimates.

Proof of Proposition
11.5
,
\beta\leqslant t/3
.

We use the identities

1_{p^{\beta}|n}=p^{-\beta}\sum_{a\in\mathbf{Z}/p^{\beta}\mathbf{Z}}e\big(\frac{an}{p^{\beta}}\big)\quad\mbox{and}\quad 1_{(p,n-1)=1}=1-\frac{1}{p}\sum_{a\in\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}e\big(\frac{a(n-1)}{p}\big)

and the Gauss sum estimate

\bigg|\sum_{x\in\mathbf{Z}/p^{t}\mathbf{Z}}\chi(x)e\big(-\frac{\lambda x}{p^{t}}\big)\bigg|\leqslant p^{t/2},

(11.5)

which holds for all
\lambda\in\mathbf{Z}/p^{t}\mathbf{Z}
, even those divisible by
p
(when the left-hand side is zero) by
[
12
, equation (3.12)]
and the remarks following it. It follows that for all
\lambda\in\mathbf{Z}/p^{t}\mathbf{Z}
we have

\bigg|\sum_{n\in\mathbf{Z}/p^{t}\mathbf{Z}}\chi(n+1)1_{(p,n-1)=1}F(n)e\big(-\frac{\lambda n}{p^{t}}\big)\bigg|\leqslant 2p^{\frac{1}{2}t+\frac{5}{6}\beta},

(11.6)

where here
F(n)=p^{\frac{5\beta}{6}}1_{p^{\beta}|n}
as in the statement of Proposition
11.5
. Since

\sum_{n\in\mathbf{Z}/p^{t}\mathbf{Z}}\Phi_{p^{t},0}(n)e\big(-\frac{\lambda n}{p^{t}}\big)=p^{\frac{5}{6}t},

(11.7)

it follows from Proposition
8.1
(7) that

\chi(n+1)1_{(p,n-1)=1}F(n)\prec\nu(p)\Phi_{p^{t},0}.

(11.8)

Here we made use of the facts that
\frac{1}{2}t+\frac{5}{6}\beta\leqslant(\frac{5}{6}-\frac{1}{18})t
(which follows from the assumption that
\beta\leqslant t/3
) and
2p^{-t/18}\leqslant\nu(p)
(which is a consequence of the definition (
11.2
) of
\nu(p)
). Thus we may take
\tilde{F}=\Phi_{p^{t},0}
, noting that this lies in
\mathscr{F}_{p}^{t}
, which is contained in
\mathscr{F}_{p}^{\max(\beta,1)}
since
t\geqslant\max(3\beta,1)
.
∎

It is convenient to note that the same analysis also handles the case
p=2
and
t\in\{1,2\}
and any
\beta\leqslant t
, since
\nu(2)>4
. Indeed, from (
11.6
), (
11.7
) and the fact that
2\cdot 2^{\frac{1}{2}t+\frac{5}{6}\beta}\leqslant 4\cdot 2^{\frac{5}{6}t}
when
\beta\leqslant t\leqslant 2
, we still have (
11.8
). This is convenient, as these small cases will be mildly exceptional in the analysis of the next subsection.

11.4.
The case of large
\beta

We turn now to the case
\beta>t/3
of Proposition
11.5
. We assume throughout the section that
p^{t}\neq\{2,4\}
, because this case can be handled as described at the end of the last subsection. Note that since
t\geqslant 1
, in this case we automatically have
\beta\geqslant 1
and so
\max(\beta,1)=\beta
.
Let us first of all note that the case
\beta\geqslant t
is trivial, because then if
p^{\beta}\mid n
then
\chi(n+1)1_{(p,n-1)=1}=1
and so
\chi(n+1)1_{(p,n-1)=1}F(n)=F(n)
for all
n
, so we may simply take
\tilde{F}=F
. Suppose, then, for the remainder of this section that
t/3<\beta<t
, and write, for
\lambda\in\mathbf{Z}/p^{t}\mathbf{Z}
,

\displaystyle S(\lambda)

\displaystyle:=\sum_{n\in\mathbf{Z}/p^{t}\mathbf{Z}}\chi(n+1)1_{(p,n-1)=1}1_{p^{\beta}|n}e\big(-\frac{\lambda n}{p^{t}}\big)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k\in\mathbf{Z}/p^{t-\beta}\mathbf{Z}}\chi(1+p^{\beta}k)e\big(-\frac{\lambda k}{p^{t-\beta}}\big).

We now need a consequence of a result which is usually known as the Postnikov character formula. For a self-contained treatment of this topic, see Appendix
A
.

It follows from Proposition
A.4
and the comments immediately following it that, since
p^{t}\notin\{2,4\}
, there are
a_{1},a_{2}\in\mathbf{Z}
such that

S(\lambda)=\sum_{k\in\mathbf{Z}/p^{t-\beta}\mathbf{Z}}e\bigg(\frac{(a_{1}-\lambda)k+a_{2}k^{2}}{p^{t-\beta}}\bigg).

(11.9)

We now divide into two cases (it is important to note that the division into cases depends only on
\chi,\beta
, and
not
on
\lambda
).

Case 1
(
Purely linear case
)
:
we have
a_{2}\equiv 0(\operatorname{mod}\,p^{t-\beta})
. In this case we have simply

S(\lambda)=\sum_{k\in\mathbf{Z}/p^{t-\beta}\mathbf{Z}}e\bigg(\frac{(a_{1}-\lambda)k}{p^{t-\beta}}\bigg)=\sum_{n\in\mathbf{Z}/p^{t}\mathbf{Z}}1_{p^{\beta}|n}e\big(\frac{a_{1}n}{p^{t}}\big)e\big(-\frac{\lambda n}{p^{t}}\big).

Therefore if
F(n)=p^{\frac{5}{6}\beta}1_{p^{\beta}|n}
then, by Proposition
8.1
(7) we have
\chi^{+}1_{(p,n-1)=1}F\prec\tilde{F}
, where
\tilde{F}(n):=p^{\frac{5}{6}\beta}1_{p^{\beta}|n}e(\frac{a_{1}n}{p^{t}})
. Note that
\tilde{F}(n)
is of the form
\Phi_{p^{\beta},b}
, since we may write
\frac{a_{1}}{p^{t}}=\frac{b}{p^{3\beta}}
since
\beta>t/3
. This confirms Proposition
11.5
in Case 1, the purely linear case.

Case 2
(
Not purely linear case
)
:

a_{2}\not\equiv 0(\operatorname{mod}\,p^{t-\beta})
. Suppose first that
p
is odd; minor modifications, which we will indicate at the end, are required when
p=2
.

Given
\lambda
, define
u(\lambda)
by

u(\lambda):=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&\mbox{if $p^{t-\beta}|a_{1}-\lambda$}\\
t-\beta-v_{p}(a_{1}-\lambda)&\mbox{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

(11.10)

Thus
0\leqslant u(\lambda)\leqslant t-\beta
; note that the definition has been made so that it is well-defined for
\lambda\in\mathbf{Z}/p^{t}\mathbf{Z}
. Lemma
D.1
(and (
11.9
)) tells us that

|S(\lambda)|\leqslant p^{t-\beta-\frac{1}{2}u(\lambda)}.

(11.11)

When
u(\lambda)=0
we can do better: in this case we have
v_{p}(a_{2})\leqslant t-\beta-1
by the assumption that we are in the not purely linear case, and so Lemma
D.1
gives

|S(\lambda)|\leqslant p^{t-\beta-\frac{1}{2}}.

(11.12)

Let
0\leqslant v\leqslant t-\beta
. Define

F_{v}(n):=p^{\frac{5}{6}(v+\beta)}1_{p^{v+\beta}|n}e\big(\frac{a_{1}n}{p^{t}}\big).

We note that
F_{v}
is of the form
\Phi_{p^{\tilde{\beta}},\tilde{a}}
with
\tilde{\beta}\geqslant\beta
, so in particular lies in
\mathscr{F}_{p}^{\beta}
. Indeed, we can take
\tilde{\beta}=\beta+v
and then write
\frac{a_{1}}{p^{t}}=\frac{\tilde{a}}{p^{3\tilde{\beta}}}
since
3\tilde{\beta}=3(\beta+v)\geqslant 3\beta>t
. Now (since
v\leqslant t-\beta
)
F_{v}(n)
is a function on
\mathbf{Z}/p^{t}\mathbf{Z}
. We may compute its
(\operatorname{mod}\,p^{t})
Fourier coefficient at
\lambda
as follows:

\displaystyle\sum_{n\in\mathbf{Z}/p^{t}\mathbf{Z}}F_{v}(n)e\big(-\frac{\lambda n}{p^{t}}\big)

\displaystyle=p^{\frac{5}{6}(v+\beta)}\sum_{k\in\mathbf{Z}/p^{t-v-\beta}\mathbf{Z}}e\bigg(\frac{(a_{1}-\lambda)k}{p^{t-v-\beta}}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}0&\mbox{if $v<u(\lambda)$}\\
p^{\frac{5}{6}(v+\beta)+t-v-\beta}&\mbox{if $v\geqslant u(\lambda)$}.\end{array}\right.

In particular, comparing with (
11.11
) one sees that (writing
u=u(\lambda)
for brevity)

\displaystyle\bigg|\sum_{n\in\mathbf{Z}/p^{t}\mathbf{Z}}

\displaystyle\chi(n+1)1_{(n-1,p)=1}F(n)e\big(-\frac{\lambda n}{p^{t}}\big)\bigg|=p^{\frac{5}{6}\beta}|S(\lambda)|\leqslant p^{\frac{5}{6}\beta+t-\beta-\frac{1}{2}u}

\displaystyle=p^{-\frac{1}{3}u}p^{\frac{5}{6}(u+\beta)+t-u-\beta}=p^{-\frac{1}{3}u}\sum_{n\in\mathbf{Z}/p^{t}\mathbf{Z}}F_{u}(n)e\big(-\frac{\lambda n}{p^{t}}\big).

When
u(\lambda)=0
, we may use (
11.12
) instead of (
11.11
), obtaining the bound

\bigg|\sum_{n\in\mathbf{Z}/p^{t}\mathbf{Z}}\chi(n+1)1_{(n-1,p)=1}F(n)e\big(-\frac{\lambda n}{p^{t}}\big)\bigg|\leqslant p^{-\frac{1}{3}}\sum_{n\in\mathbf{Z}/p^{t}\mathbf{Z}}F_{0}(n)e\big(-\frac{\lambda n}{p^{t}}\big).

Since the Fourier coefficients of the
F_{v}
are always non-negative (by (
11.4
)) it follows that for
all

\lambda
(no matter the value of
u(\lambda)
) we have

\bigg|\sum_{n\in\mathbf{Z}/p^{t}\mathbf{Z}}\!\!\chi(n+1)1_{(n-1,p)=1}F(n)e\big(-\frac{\lambda n}{p^{t}}\big)\bigg|\leqslant\sum_{u=0}^{t-\beta}\alpha_{u}(p)\!\!\!\sum_{n\in\mathbf{Z}/p^{t}\mathbf{Z}}\!\!F_{u}(n)e\big(-\frac{\lambda n}{p^{t}}\big),

where
\alpha_{0}(p)=p^{-\frac{1}{3}}
and
\alpha_{u}(p)=p^{-\frac{1}{3}u}
for
u=1,2,\dots
.

Thus, by another application of Proposition (
8.1
) (7) we have

\chi(n+1)1_{(n-1,p)=1}F(n)\prec\sum_{u=0}^{t-\beta}\alpha_{u}(p)F_{u}(n).

Recalling that each
F_{u}
lies in
\mathscr{F}_{p}^{\beta}
, we obtain the desired conclusion of Proposition
11.5
provided that

\sum_{u=0}^{t-\beta}\alpha_{u}(p)\leqslant\nu(p).

(11.15)

However, we have

\sum_{u=0}^{t-\beta}\alpha_{u}(p)\leqslant p^{-\frac{1}{3}}+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}p^{-\frac{1}{3}i},

and a calculation shows that this is at most
1
for
p\geqslant 19
, and less than
3
for all odd
p
. Therefore (
11.15
) does indeed hold.

When
p=2
, the bounds on
|S(\lambda)|
coming from Lemma
D.1
are weaker by a factor of
2^{\frac{1}{2}}
. This means we must take the
\alpha_{u}
larger by this factor, and now the required numerical check is that
2^{\frac{1}{2}}(2^{-\frac{1}{3}}+\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}2^{-\frac{1}{3}i})<7=\nu(2)
.

This completes the proof of Proposition
11.5
in Case 2, and hence of the whole proposition.

11.5.
General moduli

We turn now to the proof of the main result of the section, Proposition
11.2
. This essentially amounts to taking products of Proposition
11.4
over different primes
p
, but one must be slightly careful.

Lemma 11.6
.

Suppose that
q=q_{1}q_{2}
with
(q_{1},q_{2})=1
. Then every
\Phi_{q_{1},a_{1}}\Phi_{q_{2},a_{2}}
is of the form
\Phi_{q,a}
, and vice versa.

Proof.

This follows fairly immediately from the definition (
8.24
) of the
\Phi_{q,a}
and the Chinese remainder theorem.
∎

Finally, we are ready for the proof of the main result of the section.

Proof of Proposition
11.2
.

It suffices to consider the case
F=\Phi_{r,b}
, from which the general case follows by taking appropriate convex combinations (using Proposition
8.1
). Suppose that
r=r^{\prime}q
where
q=\prod_{p\in\mathcal{P}}p^{\beta_{p}}
, and
r^{\prime}
is not divisible by any prime in
\mathcal{P}
, so that
(r^{\prime},q)=1
. We have
F=\Phi_{r,b}=\Phi_{r^{\prime},b^{\prime}}\Phi_{q,a}
where

\Phi_{q,a}=\prod_{p\in\mathcal{P}}\Phi_{p^{\beta_{p}},a_{p}}
, and here
a\in\mathbf{Z}/q^{3}\mathbf{Z}
,
a_{p}\in\mathbf{Z}/p^{3}\mathbf{Z}
and
b^{\prime}\in\mathbf{Z}/(r^{\prime})^{3}\mathbf{Z}
. We also (see Appendix
C
) have a factorisation
\chi=\prod_{p\in\mathcal{P}}\chi_{p}
, where each
\chi_{p}
is a primitive Dirichlet character to modulus
p^{t_{p}}
, where
\prod_{p}p^{t_{p}}=q
(and so, in particular,
t_{p}\geqslant 1
for every
p\in\mathcal{P}
).

By Proposition
11.4
(taking
\beta=0
), for every
p
there is some
\tilde{F}_{p}\in\mathscr{F}_{p}^{1}
such that

\chi_{p}^{+}1_{(p,n-1)=1}\Phi_{p^{\beta_{p}},a_{p}}\prec\nu(p)\tilde{F}_{p}.

Taking products over
p\in\mathcal{P}
(and using Proposition
8.1
(4)) gives

\chi^{+}1_{(q,n-1)=1}\Phi_{q,a}\prec M\prod_{p}\tilde{F}_{p},

where recall that
M:=\prod_{p}\nu(p)
with
\omega
as defined in (
11.2
).
Finally, we may multiply both sides by
\Phi_{r^{\prime},b^{\prime}}
, which is Fourier-positive, so by Proposition
8.1
(3) we have

\chi^{+}1_{(q,n-1)=1}F\prec M\tilde{F},

where

\tilde{F}:=\Phi_{r^{\prime},b^{\prime}}\prod_{p\in\mathcal{P}}\tilde{F}_{p}
. Finally, we observe using Lemma
11.6
that
\tilde{F}
is a convex sum over functions
\Phi_{q^{\prime},a^{\prime}}
, where
r^{\prime}\prod_{p\in\mathcal{P}}p
divides
q^{\prime}
, the key here being that
r^{\prime}
is coprime to all the
p
. In particular,
\tilde{F}
lies in
\mathscr{F}_{\mathcal{P}}
, which is what we wanted to prove.
∎

12.
An archimedean construction

12.1.
Introduction and statement of results

In this section we describe the function
w
in the definition (
3.2
) of our key function
\Psi
. We need
w
to have a fairly long list of properties, which are detailed in Proposition
12.1
, but the most important is that the exponential sum of
w(n)
twisted by
n^{\rho-1}
can be controlled in terms of the exponential sum of
w
alone (see items (4) and (5) of Proposition
12.1
). The requirement for this property means that the most standard kind of cutoff functions such as the Fejér kernel are not sufficient for our purposes, and we instead make a construction modelled on the function
W(x)=x^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-x}
. Some further remarks on the particular choice of
W
may be found at the end of Lemma
12.5
.

Proposition 12.1
.

There is
w:\mathbf{N}\rightarrow[0,\infty)
such that we have the following, for all
\rho=1-\sigma+i\gamma
with
\sigma\leqslant\frac{1}{48}
and
|\gamma|\leqslant N^{\frac{1}{8}}
:

(1)

\sum_{n=1}^{N}n^{-\sigma}w(n)\ll N^{1-\sigma}
;

(2)

\sum_{n=1}^{N}w(n)\gg\frac{N}{\log N}
;

(3)

uniformly for
\eta\in\mathbf{R}/\mathbf{Z}
with
\|\eta\|\geqslant N^{-\frac{1}{2}}
we have

\sum_{n=1}^{N}n^{\rho-1}w(n)e(\eta n)\ll N^{1-\frac{1}{12}}.

(4)

uniformly for
\eta\in\mathbf{R}/\mathbf{Z}

|\sum_{n=1}^{N}n^{\rho-1}w(n)e(\eta n)|\leqslant 11N^{-\frac{1}{4}\sigma}\sum_{n=1}^{N}w(n)\cos(2\pi\eta n)+O(N^{1-\frac{1}{12}}).

(12.1)

(5)

If
\rho=1-\sigma
is real we have, uniformly for
\eta\in\mathbf{R}/\mathbf{Z}
,

\sum_{n=1}^{N}n^{\rho-1}w(n)\cos(2\pi\eta n)\leqslant N^{-\frac{1}{4}\sigma}\sum_{n=1}^{N}w(n)\cos(2\pi\eta n)+O(N^{1-\frac{1}{12}}).

(6)

For any
X
,
1\leqslant X\leqslant N
, we may write
w=w_{0}+w_{1}
where

\sum_{n}w_{0}(n)\ll NX^{-1}

(12.2)

and
w_{1}(n)=\int_{\mathbf{R}}a(\xi)e(\frac{\xi n}{Y})d\xi
where
Y:=\frac{N}{2\log N}
and

\int_{\mathbf{R}}|a(\xi)|d\xi\ll N^{o(1)}X.

(12.3)

For the rest of the section, take
Y:=\frac{N}{2\log N}
(as in the statement of (6) above). The idea is to set

W(x):=x^{-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-x}

(12.4)

for
x>0
and
W=0
elsewhere, and then define

w(n):=W\big(\frac{n}{Y}\big).

(12.5)

12.2.
Basic bounds for sums

In this section we provide some bounds for
\sum_{n=1}^{N}n^{\rho-1}w(n)e(\eta n)
. In the first lemma, we compare this sum to an integral when
\eta
is not too large.

Here, and throughout the rest of the section, we write
T_{\rho}f(x):=x^{\rho-1}f(x)
for
x>0
.

If
f:\mathbf{R}\rightarrow\mathbf{R}
is a (suitable nice) function, we normalise the Fourier transform by
\widehat{f}(\xi):=\int_{\mathbf{R}}f(x)e(-x\xi)dx
, so that the inversion formula reads
f(x)=\int_{\mathbf{R}}\widehat{f}(\xi)e(\xi x)d\xi
.

Lemma 12.2
.

Suppose that
\frac{4}{5}\leqslant\beta\leqslant 1
and
|\gamma|\leqslant N^{\frac{1}{8}}
. Then, uniformly for
|\eta|\leqslant N^{-\frac{1}{8}}
,

\sum_{n=1}^{N}n^{\rho-1}w(n)e(\eta n)=Y^{\rho-1}\widehat{T_{\rho}W}(-\eta Y)+O(N^{\frac{7}{8}}).

Proof.

Set
F(x):=T_{\rho}W(x)e(\eta Yx)=x^{\rho-1}W(x)e(\eta Yx)
for
x>0
.
Writing
\rho=1-\sigma+i\gamma
and

F(x)=x^{-\sigma}\cdot W(x)\cdot e\big(\frac{\gamma}{2\pi}\log x+\eta Yx\big)

and applying the product rule for derivatives, we see that

|F^{\prime}(x)|\ll x^{-\sigma-1}W(x)+x^{-\sigma}W^{\prime}(x)+x^{-\sigma}W(x)\big(\frac{|\gamma|}{x}+|\eta Y|\big).

For the first term we use the bound
W(x)\ll x^{-\frac{1}{2}}
, and for the second
W^{\prime}(x)\ll x^{-\frac{3}{2}}
(which may be checked by looking at
x<1
and
x\geqslant 1
separately). We split the third term in two. The part with
\frac{|\gamma|}{x}
can be bounded as for the first term, but now with an extra factor of
|\gamma|\leqslant N^{\frac{1}{8}}
. We keep the remaining part as it is. Putting all this together gives

|F^{\prime}(x)|\ll x^{-\sigma-\frac{3}{2}}N^{\frac{1}{8}}+x^{-\sigma-\frac{1}{2}}e^{-x}|\eta|Y.

(12.6)

We have

\sum_{n=1}^{N}n^{\rho-1}w(n)e(\eta n)=Y^{\rho-1}\sum_{n=1}^{N}F\big(\frac{n}{Y}\big)

(12.7)

and

\widehat{T_{\rho}W}(-\eta Y)=\int^{\infty}_{0}F(x)dx.

(12.8)

Now we have

\displaystyle\big|\frac{1}{Y}\sum_{n=1}^{N}

\displaystyle F\big(\frac{n}{Y}\big)-\int^{\infty}_{0}F\big|

\displaystyle\leqslant\int^{\frac{1}{Y}}_{0}|F|+\big|\frac{1}{Y}\sum_{n=1}^{N}F\big(\frac{n}{Y}\big)-\int^{\frac{N+1}{Y}}_{\frac{1}{Y}}F\big|+\int^{\infty}_{\frac{N+1}{Y}}|F|.

(12.9)

To bound the third term, we use the bound
|F(x)|\leqslant|W(x)|\leqslant e^{-x}
, valid for
x\geqslant 1
, to bound this term by
e^{-N/Y}=\frac{1}{N^{2}}
(which is tiny).

For the second term in (
12.9
), we can use the mean value theorem to bound this by

\frac{1}{Y^{2}}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\sup_{x\in[\frac{n}{Y},\frac{n+1}{Y}]}|F^{\prime}(x)|.

To bound the sum appearing here, we use the derivative estimate (
12.6
). The contribution from the first term there is

\ll\frac{1}{Y^{2}}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\big|\frac{n}{Y}\big|^{-\frac{3}{2}-\sigma}N^{\frac{1}{8}}\ll Y^{\sigma-\frac{1}{2}}N^{\frac{1}{8}}.

The contribution from the second is

\ll\frac{1}{Y^{2}}|\eta|Y\sum_{n=1}^{N}\big|\frac{n}{Y}\big|^{-\frac{1}{2}-\sigma}e^{-n/Y}\ll|\eta|,

where here we used the bound
\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}n^{-s}e^{-n/Y}\ll_{s}Y^{1-s}
uniformly for
s\leqslant s_{0}<1
.

Finally, to bound the first term in (
12.9
), we use
F(x)\ll x^{-\frac{1}{2}-\sigma}
, obtaining that this term is
\ll Y^{\sigma-\frac{1}{2}}
.

Putting all these bounds into (
12.9
) gives

\bigg|\frac{1}{Y}\sum_{n=1}^{N}F\big(\frac{n}{Y}\big)-\int^{\infty}_{0}F(x)dx\bigg|\ll Y^{\sigma-\frac{1}{2}}N^{\frac{1}{8}}+|\eta|.

Multiplying by
Y^{\rho}
and combining this with (
12.7
) and (
12.8
) gives the desired result.
∎

Lemma 12.3
.

Let
0<\varepsilon<1
. Then we may split
W=W_{0}+W_{1}
, where
0\leqslant W_{0},W_{1}\leqslant W
pointwise,
\operatorname{Supp}(W_{0})\subset[0,2\varepsilon]
and
\operatorname{Supp}(W_{1})\subset[\varepsilon,\infty)
and we have

W_{1}(x)=\int_{\mathbf{R}}\widehat{W}_{1}(\xi)e(\xi x)d\xi,

where

|\widehat{W}_{1}(\xi)|\ll\min\big(1,\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}}|\xi|^{-1},\varepsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}}|\xi|^{-2}\big).

(12.10)

In particular,

\|\widehat{W}_{1}\|_{1}\ll\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}}\log\big(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\big).

Proof.

Let
\Psi:\mathbf{R}\rightarrow[0,\infty)
be a smooth function with
\Psi(x)=0
for
x\leqslant 1
,
0\leqslant\Psi(x)\leqslant 1
for
x\in[1,2]
,
\Psi(x)=1
for
x\geqslant 2
and
\|\Psi^{(m)}\|_{\infty}\ll_{m}1
for all
m
, where
\Psi^{(m)}
denotes the
m
th derivative. The existence of such functions is standard. Set
\Psi_{\varepsilon}(x):=\Psi(x/\varepsilon)
, and then define
W_{1}:=W\Psi_{\varepsilon}
and
W_{0}:=W(1-\Psi_{\varepsilon})
. The support properties and pointwise bounds in (1) are then clear.

For (2), we use the well-known bound (which follows by integration by parts)

|\widehat{W}_{1}(\xi)|\ll\|W_{1}^{(m)}\|_{1}|\xi|^{-m},

(12.11)

for
m=0,1,2
.
We have

\|W_{1}\|_{1}\leqslant\|W\|_{1}\ll 1.

(12.12)

For the first derivative, note first that

\|W^{\prime}_{1}\|_{1}\leqslant\|W^{\prime}\Psi_{\varepsilon}\|_{1}+\|W\Psi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}\|_{1}.

Now
