\|W^{\prime}\Psi_{\varepsilon}\|_{1}\leqslant\int_{x>\varepsilon}|W^{\prime}(x)|dx\ll\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}}
, whilst

\|W\Psi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}\|_{1}\ll\varepsilon^{-1}\int^{2\varepsilon}_{\varepsilon}W(x)dx\ll\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

Therefore

\|W^{\prime}_{1}\|_{1}\ll\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

(12.13)

For the second derivative, we have

\|W^{\prime\prime}_{1}\|_{1}\leqslant\|W^{\prime\prime}\Psi_{\varepsilon}\|_{1}+2\|W^{\prime}\Psi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}\|_{1}+\|W\Psi^{\prime\prime}_{\varepsilon}\|_{1}.

Now

\|W^{\prime\prime}\Psi_{\varepsilon}\|_{1}\leqslant\int_{x>\varepsilon}|W^{\prime\prime}(x)|dx\ll\varepsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}},

\|W^{\prime}\Psi^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}\|_{1}\ll\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\int^{2\varepsilon}_{\varepsilon}|W^{\prime}(x)|dx\ll\varepsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}},

and

\|W\Psi^{\prime\prime}_{\varepsilon}\|_{1}\ll\frac{1}{\varepsilon^{2}}\int^{2\varepsilon}_{\varepsilon}W(x)dx\ll\varepsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}}.

Thus

\|W^{\prime\prime}_{1}\|_{1}\ll\varepsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}}.

(12.14)

Combining (
12.12
), (
12.13
) and (
12.14
) with (
12.11
) gives the stated bound (
12.10
).

The bound on
\|\widehat{W}_{1}\|_{1}
follows by splitting into the ranges
|\xi|\leqslant\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}}
,
\varepsilon^{-\frac{1}{2}}\leqslant|\xi|\leqslant\varepsilon^{-1}
and
|\xi|\geqslant\varepsilon^{-1}
and using (
12.12
), (
12.13
) and (
12.14
) respectively on these ranges.

Finally, the fact that
\|\widehat{W}_{1}\|_{1}
is finite guarantees that
W_{1}
is represented pointwise by its Fourier integral, by well-known theory.
∎

Proposition 12.4
.

Uniformly for
0\leqslant\sigma\leqslant\frac{1}{4}
,
|\gamma|\leqslant N^{\frac{1}{4}}
and for
N^{-\frac{1}{2}}<\|\eta\|\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
we have

\sum_{n=1}^{N}n^{\rho-1}w(n)e(\eta n)\ll N^{1-\frac{1}{12}}.

Proof.

Set
\alpha:=\frac{1}{6}
and
\varepsilon:=N^{-\alpha}
. (It is helpful, to see the structure of the argument, to work with
\alpha
rather than the explicit fraction
\frac{1}{6}
during the proof.) We use the decomposition of
W
from Lemma
12.3
with this value of
\varepsilon
, and write the sum to be bounded as
S_{0}+S_{1}
, where

S_{j}:=\sum_{n=1}^{N}n^{\rho-1}W_{j}\big(\frac{n}{Y}\big)e(\eta n).

We bound
S_{0}
in a fairly trivial manner, using the pointwise bound
W_{0}(x)\leqslant W(x)\leqslant x^{-\frac{1}{2}}
to conclude that

S_{0}\ll\sum_{n=1}^{2N^{1-\alpha}}n^{-\sigma}\big(\frac{n}{Y}\big)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\ll Y^{\frac{1}{2}}(N^{1-\alpha})^{\frac{1}{2}-\sigma}\ll N^{1-\frac{1}{2}\alpha}=N^{1-\frac{1}{12}}.

For
S_{1}
, we use the Fourier expansion of
W_{1}
as described in Lemma
12.3
, obtaining

S_{1}=\int_{\mathbf{R}}\widehat{W}_{1}(\xi)d\xi\sum_{n=1}^{N}n^{\rho-1}e\big((\frac{\xi}{Y}+\eta)n\big)=S^{\prime}_{1}+S^{\prime\prime}_{1},

where
S^{\prime}_{1}
is the integral over
|\xi|\leqslant N^{\frac{1}{3}}
and
S^{\prime\prime}_{1}
is the remaining integral. Using Lemma
12.3
again, we have

S^{\prime}_{1}\ll N^{\frac{1}{2}\alpha+o(1)}\sup_{|\xi|\leqslant N^{\frac{1}{3}}}\bigg|\sum_{n=1}^{N}n^{\rho-1}e\big((\frac{\xi}{Y}+\eta)n\big)\bigg|.

To estimate this, we use the middle bound of Proposition
5.3
, which implies (since
|\gamma|\leqslant N^{\frac{1}{4}}
and
\beta\leqslant 1
) that

S^{\prime}_{1}\ll N^{\frac{1}{2}\alpha+o(1)+\frac{1}{4}}\sup_{|\xi|\leqslant N^{\frac{1}{3}}}\big\|\frac{\xi}{Y}+\eta\big\|^{-1}\ll N^{\frac{1}{2}\alpha+o(1)+\frac{1}{4}+\frac{1}{2}}\ll N^{1-\frac{1}{12}},

where in the penultimate step we used the assumption that
\|\eta\|\geqslant N^{-\frac{1}{2}}
(and recall
Y=\frac{N}{2\log N}
).

Finally, for
S^{\prime\prime}_{1}
we use the bound
|\widehat{W}_{1}(\xi)|\ll\varepsilon^{-\frac{3}{2}}|\xi|^{-2}
and the first (trivial) bound in Proposition
5.3
, obtaining

S^{\prime\prime}_{1}\ll N\int_{|\xi|\geqslant N^{\frac{1}{3}}}|\widehat{W}_{1}(\xi)|d\xi\ll N^{1+\frac{3}{2}\alpha-\frac{1}{3}}=N^{1-\frac{1}{12}}.

Putting all this together concludes the proof.∎

12.3.
Bounds for integrals

Throughout this section, suppose that
\rho=\beta+i\gamma=1-\sigma+i\gamma
.

Obviously, to make any use of Lemma
12.2
, we need some understanding of the Fourier transform of
T_{\rho}W
. We have

\widehat{T_{\rho}W}(\theta)=\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{\rho-1}W(x)e^{-2\pi ix\theta}dx=\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{-\sigma-\frac{1}{2}+i\gamma}e^{-x(1+2\pi i\theta)}dx.

(12.15)

To handle this, we make use of the fact that the formula

\int^{\infty}_{0}x^{s-1}e^{-ax}dx=\frac{\Gamma(s)}{a^{s}}

(12.16)

holds for all
\Re s,\Re a>0
, where
a^{s}=e^{s\log a}
with
\log a
being the principal branch of the logarithm on
\Re a>0
. This fact is “well-known” but it is somewhat difficult to find a satisfactory reference; it appears as
[
15
, equation (1.5.1)]
, which states (quite correctly, but with no further details) that it follows from the well-known case where
a
is real, by the identity principle for holomorphic functions. It is also possible to give a proof using contour integration.

From (
12.15
), (
12.16
) we have

\widehat{T_{\rho}W}(\theta)=\frac{\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}-\sigma+i\gamma)}{(1+2\pi i\theta)^{\frac{1}{2}-\sigma+i\gamma}}.

(12.17)

Specialising to
\rho=1
(i.e.
\sigma=\gamma=0
) gives a formula for
\widehat{W}
, namely

\widehat{W}(\theta)=\Gamma(\textstyle\frac{1}{2}\displaystyle)(1+2\pi i\theta)^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

The bounds we need can now be read from the existing literature on the
\Gamma
-function, and are summarised in the following lemma.

Lemma 12.5
.

We have

|\widehat{T_{\rho}W}(\theta)|\leqslant 10(1+\theta^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}\sigma}\Re\widehat{W}(\theta),

(12.18)

uniformly for
0\leqslant\sigma\leqslant\frac{1}{4}
and all
\gamma,\theta\in\mathbf{R}
.
If
\rho=1-\sigma
is real with
\sigma
small then, uniformly for
0\leqslant\sigma\leqslant\frac{1}{4}
, we have

\Re\widehat{T_{\rho}W}(\theta)\leqslant(1+O(\sigma))(1+4\pi^{2}\theta^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}\sigma}\Re\widehat{W}(\theta).

(12.19)

Proof.

We first find a lower bound for
\Re\widehat{W}(\theta)
. We have

\Re(1+2\pi i\theta)^{-\frac{1}{2}}=(1+4\pi^{2}\theta^{2})^{-\frac{1}{4}}\cos(\textstyle\frac{1}{2}\displaystyle\tan^{-1}(2\pi\theta)).

Since
|\tan^{-1}(2\pi\theta)|\leqslant\frac{\pi}{2}
, we have
\cos(\frac{1}{2}\tan^{-1}(2\pi\theta))\geqslant\cos(\frac{\pi}{4})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
, and so

\Re\widehat{W}(\theta)\geqslant 2^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1+4\pi^{2}\theta^{2})^{-\frac{1}{4}}.

(12.20)

We turn now to finding an upper bound for
|\widehat{T_{\rho}W}(\theta)|
. We will use the inequality

|\Gamma(x+iy)|\leqslant 7e^{-\pi|y|/2},

(12.21)

which holds uniformly for
\frac{1}{4}\leqslant x\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
and for all
y
. For the proof, see Lemma
E.1
. Also,

|(1+2\pi i\theta)^{x+iy}|=(1+4\pi^{2}\theta^{2})^{x/2}e^{-y\tan^{-1}(2\pi\theta)}\geqslant(1+4\pi^{2}\theta^{2})^{x/2}e^{-\pi|y|/2}.

It follows immediately from this, (
12.21
) and (
12.17
) that

|\widehat{T_{\rho}W}(\theta)|\leqslant 7(1+4\pi^{2}\theta^{2})^{-\frac{1}{2}(\frac{1}{2}-\sigma)}.

(12.22)

Comparing (
12.20
) and (
12.22
), the desired bound (
12.18
) follows immediately since
7\sqrt{2}<10
.

Now we turn to (
12.19
). By (
12.17
) we have

\displaystyle\Re

\displaystyle\widehat{T_{1-\sigma}W}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\Gamma(\textstyle\frac{1}{2}\displaystyle-\sigma)(1+4\pi^{2}\theta^{2})^{-\frac{1}{4}+\frac{1}{2}\sigma}\cos\big((\textstyle\frac{1}{2}\displaystyle-\sigma)\tan^{-1}(2\pi\theta)\big).

(12.23)

We want to compare this with the same expression when
\sigma=0
. Since
\Gamma
is differentiable (and never zero) on
(0,1)
we have
\Gamma(\frac{1}{2}-\sigma)=\Gamma(\frac{1}{2})(1+O(\sigma))
. Since
-\frac{\pi}{2}\leqslant\tan^{-1}(2\pi\theta)\leqslant\frac{\pi}{2}
, and since the derivative of
\cos(tx)
with respect to
x
is uniformly bounded for
(t,x)\in[-\frac{\pi}{2},\frac{\pi}{2}]\ \times\mathbf{R}
, we have

\cos\big((\textstyle\frac{1}{2}\displaystyle-\sigma)\tan^{-1}(2\pi\theta)\big)=(1+O(\sigma))\cos(\textstyle\frac{1}{2}\displaystyle\tan^{-1}(2\pi\theta))

uniformly in
\theta
.

Putting this information together with (
12.23
) leads to (
12.19
).
∎

Remarks.
If we tried to run a similar analysis with
W(x)=e^{-x}
(or any
x^{-t}e^{-x}
with
t<\frac{1}{2}
) it would break down at the point where we asserted (
12.21
). Here, it is critical that we are only considering
\Gamma(z)
for
\Re z\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
. Indeed, one may compute that for large positive
\gamma
, if we instead take
W(x)=e^{-x}
then
\widehat{T_{1+i\gamma}W}(\gamma)\sim\gamma^{-\frac{1}{2}}
whilst
\widehat{W}(\gamma)\sim\gamma^{-1}
, so (
12.18
) actually fails. What is going on here is that in
\widehat{T_{1+i\gamma}W}(\gamma)
we have a phase
x^{i\gamma}e(-\gamma x)=e(\gamma(\frac{1}{2\pi}\log x-x))
which is stationary at
x=\frac{1}{2\pi}
, leading to less cancellation than one might naïvely expect. The role of the factor
x^{-\frac{1}{2}}
in the definition of
W
is to reduce the relative effect of this stationary phase term by making the Fourier transform of
W
decay more slowly, so that (
12.18
) has a chance of holding.

12.4.
Proof of the main result

We are now in a position to establish the main result of the section, Proposition
12.1
.

Proof of Proposition
12.1
.

(1) We have

\sum_{n=1}^{N}n^{-\sigma}w(n)\ll\sum_{n=1}^{N}n^{-\sigma}\big(\frac{n}{Y}\big)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\ll Y^{\frac{1}{2}}N^{\frac{1}{2}-\sigma}\ll N^{1-\sigma},

with the implied constants being uniform for
\sigma\leqslant\frac{1}{4}
(say).

(2) We have

\sum_{n=1}^{N}w(n)\geqslant\sum_{n=1}^{Y}w(n)\gg\sum_{n=1}^{Y}\big(\frac{n}{Y}\big)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\gg Y.

(3) This has already been established in Proposition
12.4
.

(4) We first handle the case
|\eta|\leqslant N^{-\frac{1}{2}}
. By Lemma
12.2
,

\sum_{n=1}^{N}n^{\rho-1}w(n)e(\eta n)=Y^{\rho-1}\widehat{T_{\rho}W}(-\eta Y)+O(N^{\frac{7}{8}}).

(12.24)

Taking
\rho=1
,

\sum_{n=1}^{N}w(n)\cos(2\pi\eta n)=\Re\widehat{W}(-\eta Y)+O(N^{\frac{7}{8}}).

(12.25)

However, by (
12.18
) we have

\displaystyle|Y^{\rho-1}\widehat{T_{\rho}W}(-\eta Y)|

\displaystyle\leqslant 10Y^{-\sigma}(1+|\eta Y|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}\sigma}\Re\widehat{W}(-\eta Y)

\displaystyle\leqslant 11N^{-\frac{1}{4}\sigma}\Re\widehat{W}(-\eta Y).

Combining these results gives the conclusion when
|\eta|\leqslant N^{-\frac{1}{2}}
.

In the case
N^{-\frac{1}{2}}<|\eta|\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
, Proposition
12.4
(applied to both sides of (
12.1
)) gives the result immediately.

(5) The case when
N^{-\frac{1}{2}}<\|\eta\|\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
may be handled exactly as in (4). For the case
|\eta|\leqslant N^{-\frac{1}{2}}
, we again start with (
12.24
) and (
12.25
). In fact, we need to take real parts of (
12.24
) which, since
\rho=1-\sigma
is real, gives

\sum_{n=1}^{N}n^{-\sigma}w(n)\cos(\eta n)=Y^{-\sigma}\Re\widehat{T_{1-\sigma}W}(-\eta Y)+O(N^{\frac{7}{8}}).

(12.26)

By (
12.19
) we have

\displaystyle Y^{-\sigma}\Re\widehat{T_{1-\sigma}W}(-\eta Y)

\displaystyle\leqslant(1+O(\sigma))Y^{-\sigma}(1+|\eta Y|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}\sigma}\Re\widehat{W}(-\eta Y)

\displaystyle\leqslant(1+O(\sigma))Y^{-\sigma}N^{\frac{1}{2}\sigma}\Re\widehat{W}(-\eta Y)

\displaystyle\leqslant N^{-\frac{1}{4}\sigma}\Re\widehat{W}(-\eta Y).

Here, in the second step we used
1+|\eta Y|^{2}<1+(N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\frac{N}{\log N})^{2}<N
, and in the last step we used
Y=\frac{N}{2\log N}>N^{\frac{7}{8}}
and
1+C\sigma\leqslant N^{\frac{1}{8}\sigma}
, which is valid uniformly for all real
\sigma\geqslant 0
and for
N>e^{8C}
.

The desired result follows by combining this with (
12.26
) and (
12.25
).

(6) We use the decomposition
W=W_{0}+W_{1}
from Lemma
12.3
, taking
\varepsilon:=X^{-2}
, and set
w_{0}(n):=W_{0}(\frac{n}{Y})
,
w_{1}(n):=W_{1}(\frac{n}{Y})
. From Lemma
12.3
we have
w_{1}(n)=\int\widehat{W}_{1}(\xi)e(\frac{\xi}{Y}n)d\xi
, so we take
a(\xi):=\widehat{W}_{1}(\xi)
. The stated bound (
12.3
) on
\int_{\mathbf{R}}|a(\xi)|d\xi
is then immediate from Lemma
12.3
.

Also,

\sum_{n=1}^{N}w_{0}(n)\leqslant\sum_{n\leqslant NX^{-2}}W\big(\frac{n}{Y}\big)\ll Y^{\frac{1}{2}}(NX^{-2})^{\frac{1}{2}}\ll NX^{-1},

which is the claimed bound (
12.2
).
∎

Part IV
The main construction

We turn now to the main construction of a function
\Psi
and to the proof of Theorem
2.2
(and hence, as explained shortly before the statement of that result, Theorems
1.1
and
1.2
). The reader may find it helpful to reread the broad overview of the construction, as given in Section
3.1
.

For the remainder of the paper, we fix a scale
T
satisfying the conclusions of Proposition
7.7
, with
\sigma_{0}=\frac{1}{48}
, this choice being with future applications of Proposition
12.1
in mind. From now on,
\rho_{1},\rho_{2},\dots,\rho_{J}
are the zeros in
\Xi_{T}:=\Xi_{T}(\frac{1}{48})
listed in order of decreasing real part, or equivalently in increasing order of
\sigma_{j}:=1-\Re\rho_{j}
. For each
j
, write
\chi_{j}
for the primitive character for which
\rho_{j}
is a zero of
L(s,\chi_{j})
, and set
q_{j}:=\operatorname{cond}(\chi_{j})
.

At least one of the options (1) and (2) of Proposition
7.7
holds (indeed that is the content of the proposition). We refer to the two cases as the “unexceptional” and “exceptional” cases respectively.

Remark.
As previously remarked, it is technically possible to be in case (1) yet still have an exceptional zero at scale
T
in the sense of Proposition
7.1
. However, there should be little danger of confusion since we will have no further need for the definitions in Proposition
7.1
.

13.
Construction of the damping term

To ease notation a little, in this section only we write

\varphi_{j}:=\tilde{F}_{\chi_{j},T}\quad j=1,2,\dots,J\quad\mbox{and}\quad\varphi_{0}:=\tilde{\Lambda}_{T}

for the completed approximants as defined in Section
6
. Thus, from (
6.5
), (
6.11
) we have

\varphi_{j}=\frac{q_{j}}{\phi(q_{j})}\overline{\chi}_{j}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\leqslant T\\
p\nmid q_{j}\end{subarray}}\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}},\quad j\geqslant 1\quad\mbox{and}\quad\varphi_{0}=\prod_{p\leqslant T}\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}.

(13.1)

We are now in a position to actually construct the damping term
D
. Recall the definition of
\Phi_{r,b}
(see (
8.24
)), namely
\Phi_{r,b}(x):=r^{\frac{5}{6}}1_{r|x}e(\frac{bx}{r^{3}})
. Here are the key properties our damping term
D
will have.

Proposition 13.1
.

There is a function
D:\mathbf{Z}\rightarrow\mathbf{R}
with the following properties.
D(n)
is a finite convex combination

\sum_{q}\sum_{a\in\mathbf{Z}/q^{3}\mathbf{Z}}\alpha_{q,a}\Phi_{q,a}(n),

(13.2)

where

0\leqslant\alpha_{q,a}\leqslant 1,\quad\sum_{q,a}\alpha_{q,a}=1\quad\mbox{and}\quad\alpha_{1,0}\geqslant\frac{3}{4},

(13.3)

and such that

N^{-\frac{1}{8}\sigma_{j}}\varphi_{j}^{+}\varphi_{0}^{-}\tilde{H}_{T}D\prec\varphi_{0}^{+}\varphi_{0}^{-}\tilde{H}_{T}D

(13.4)

for every
j\geqslant 1

(
in the unexceptional case
)
and for every
j\geqslant 2

(
in the exceptional case
)
.

Proof.

In this proof we write for positive integer
q
,

\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/q\mathbf{Z}}:=\prod_{p|q}\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}},\quad\tau^{2}_{\mathbf{Z}/q\mathbf{Z}}:=\prod_{p|q}\tau^{2}_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}.

We first show that it is enough to construct a function
D:\mathbf{Z}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}
with the required properties, that is to say without the condition that
D
be real-valued. The key claim here is that, for every
j
,
\overline{\varphi}_{j}
also appears as one of the functions
\varphi_{j^{\prime}}
.

To prove the claim, consider the zero
\rho_{j}
and the character
\chi_{j}
associated to the index
j
. As explained in the proof of Lemma
4.5
,
\overline{\rho}_{j}
is a zero of
L(s,\overline{\chi}_{j})=0
, so for some
j^{\prime}
(possibly
j^{\prime}=j
) we have
\rho_{j^{\prime}}=\overline{\rho}_{j}
and
\chi_{j^{\prime}}=\overline{\chi}_{j}
. From (
13.1
) we see that
\varphi_{j^{\prime}}=\overline{\varphi}_{j}
, which completes the proof of the claim.

Now suppose we are given
D:\mathbf{Z}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}
satisfying Proposition
13.1
. We assert that
\Re D=\frac{1}{2}(D+\overline{D})
has the same properties (and of course it
is
real-valued). First note it is a finite convex combination of the form (
13.2
) because
\overline{\Phi_{q,a}}=\Phi_{q,-a}
.

Now, by the claim, if we have (
13.4
) for all
j
then we also have

N^{-\frac{1}{8}\sigma_{j}}\overline{\varphi}_{j}^{+}\varphi_{0}^{-}\tilde{H}_{T}D\prec\varphi_{0}^{+}\varphi_{0}^{-}\tilde{H}_{T}D

for all
j
. Taking complex conjugates and using the fact that both
\varphi_{0}=\tilde{\Lambda}_{T}=\prod_{p\leqslant T}\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}
and
\tilde{H}_{T}=\prod_{p\leqslant T}\tau^{2}_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}
are real-valued, and using Proposition
8.1
(3), we have

N^{-\frac{1}{8}\sigma_{j}}\varphi_{j}^{+}\varphi_{0}^{-}\tilde{H}_{T}\overline{D}\prec\varphi_{0}^{+}\varphi_{0}^{-}\tilde{H}_{T}\overline{D}.

Adding this to (
13.4
) and dividing by
2
(and using Proposition
8.1
(4)) confirms our assertion about
\Re D
.

Now we turn to the construction of a
D:\mathbf{Z}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}
satisfying the properties claimed. Recall (Definition
11.1
) the definitions of the classes
\mathscr{F}
and
\mathscr{F}_{\mathcal{P}}
, where
\mathcal{P}
is a set of primes. For each
j
, write
\mathcal{P}(j)
for the set of primes dividing
q_{j}
.

For the moment suppose that we are in the unexceptional case in which option (1) of Proposition
7.7
holds. For every finite (possibly empty) sequence
(j_{1},j_{2},j_{3},\dots,j_{m})
of integers from
\{1,\dots,J\}
we define
\Psi_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m}}\in\mathscr{F}
as follows. Set
\Psi_{\emptyset}:=\Phi_{1,0}
, that is to say the constant function
1
. Assuming that
\Psi_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m-1}}
has already been defined, take
\Psi_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m}}\in\mathscr{F}_{\mathcal{P}(j_{m})}
to be such that

\chi^{+}_{j_{m}}1_{(q_{m},n-1)=1}\Psi_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m-1}}\prec M\Psi_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m}}.

(13.5)

The existence of
\Psi_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m}}
is guaranteed by Proposition
11.2
.

Since
\Psi_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m}}
lies in
\mathscr{F}_{\mathcal{P}(j_{m})}
, it is supported on
n
for which
p|n
for all
p\in\mathcal{P}(j_{m})
. For such
n
and for
p\in\mathcal{P}(j_{m})
, we have
\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}^{+}(n)=\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}^{-}(n)=\frac{p}{p-1}
, and so
\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/q_{m}\mathbf{Z}}^{+}(n)=\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/q_{m}\mathbf{Z}}^{-}(n)=\frac{q_{m}}{\phi(q_{m})}
. Therefore
\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/q_{m}\mathbf{Z}}^{+}\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/q_{m}\mathbf{Z}}^{-}\Psi_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m}}=(\frac{q_{m}}{\phi(q_{m})})^{2}\Psi_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m}}
. Since also
\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/q_{m}\mathbf{Z}}^{-}(n)=\frac{q_{m}}{\phi(q_{m})}1_{(q_{m},n-1)=1}
, it follows from (
13.5
) that

\frac{q_{m}}{\phi(q_{m})}\chi^{+}_{j_{m}}\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/q_{m}\mathbf{Z}}^{-}\Psi_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m-1}}\prec M\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/q_{m}\mathbf{Z}}^{+}\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/q_{m}\mathbf{Z}}^{-}\Psi_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m}}.

We now multiply both sides of this by
\tau^{2}_{\mathbf{Z}/q_{m}\mathbf{Z}}=\prod_{p|q_{m}}\tau^{2}_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}
and then by
\prod_{p\leqslant T:p\nmid q_{m}}\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}^{+}\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}^{-}\tau^{2}_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}
. By (
8.16
) and Lemma
8.7
(and Proposition
8.1
(1)) both of these are Fourier-positive, so by Proposition
8.1
(5) we have

\displaystyle\frac{q_{m}}{\phi(q_{m})}

\displaystyle\chi^{+}_{j_{m}}\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/q_{m}\mathbf{Z}}^{-}\tau^{2}_{\mathbf{Z}/q_{m}\mathbf{Z}}\big(\prod_{p\leqslant T:p\nmid q_{m}}\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}^{+}\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}^{-}\tau^{2}_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}\big)\Psi_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m-1}}

\displaystyle\prec M\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/q_{m}\mathbf{Z}}^{+}\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/q_{m}\mathbf{Z}}^{-}\tau^{2}_{\mathbf{Z}/q_{m}\mathbf{Z}}\big(\prod_{p\leqslant T:p\nmid q_{m}}\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}^{+}\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}^{-}\tau^{2}_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}\big)\Psi_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m}}.

By (
13.1
) and the fact (Definition
8.8
) that
\tilde{H}_{T}=\prod_{p\leqslant T}\tau^{2}_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}
, this may equivalently be written as

\varphi_{j_{m}}^{+}\varphi_{0}^{-}\tilde{H}_{T}\Psi_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m-1}}\prec M\varphi_{0}^{+}\varphi_{0}^{-}\tilde{H}_{T}\Psi_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m}}.

(13.6)

Set

\mu:=\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\sum_{(j_{1},j_{2},\dots,j_{m})}N^{-\frac{1}{16}(\sigma_{j_{1}}+\dots+\sigma_{j_{m}})}.

Here, the tuples
(j_{1},\dots,j_{m})
range over all
J^{m}
tuples with
1\leqslant j_{i}\leqslant J
for each
i
. We claim that this is a convergent sum and that in fact
\mu\leqslant\frac{1}{3}
. For this, note that the
m
th term in the sum for
\mu
is
\big(\sum_{j=1}^{J}N^{-\frac{1}{16}\sigma_{j}}\big)^{m}
, so by summing the geometric series it is enough to show that
\sum_{j=1}^{J}N^{-\frac{1}{16}\sigma_{j}}\leqslant\frac{1}{4}
, which is (massively) true by (
7.6
).

Now we define

D(n):=(1+\mu)^{-1}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{(j_{1},\dots,j_{m})}N^{-\frac{1}{16}(\sigma_{j_{1}}+\dots+\sigma_{j_{m}})}\Psi_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m}}(n),

(13.7)

where again the tuples
(j_{1},\dots,j_{m})
range over all
J^{m}
tuples with
1\leqslant j_{i}\leqslant J
for each
i
.
The functions
\Psi_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m}}
all lie in
\mathscr{F}
(as defined in Definition
11.1
), and
D
is a convex combination of them. Therefore
D
also lies in
\mathscr{F}
; the key point here is that
\mathscr{F}
is the convex span of a
finite
set of functions
\Phi_{q,a}
, so the fact that
D
is defined by an infinite sum rather than a finite one does not matter.

D
satisfies the first two conditions of (
13.3
). It also satisfies the third condition of (
13.3
): note that the term with
m=0
in
D(n)
is
(1+\mu)^{-1}\Psi_{\emptyset}=(1+\mu)^{-1}\Phi_{1,0}
, so this follows from the fact that
\mu\leqslant\frac{1}{3}
.

Turning now to property (
13.4
), we have the following deduction of this statement, where we will explain the steps below:

\displaystyle(1+\mu)

\displaystyle N^{-\frac{1}{8}\sigma_{j}}\varphi_{j}^{+}\varphi_{0}^{-}\tilde{H}_{T}D

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{(j_{1},\dots,j_{m})}N^{-\frac{1}{16}(\sigma_{j_{1}}+\dots+\sigma_{j_{m}})}N^{-\frac{1}{8}\sigma_{j}}\varphi_{j}^{+}\varphi_{0}^{-}\tilde{H}_{T}\Psi_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m}}

\displaystyle\prec\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{(j_{1},\dots,j_{m})}N^{-\frac{1}{16}(\sigma_{j_{1}}+\dots+\sigma_{j_{m}})}N^{-\frac{1}{8}\sigma_{j}}M\varphi^{+}_{0}\varphi^{-}_{0}\tilde{H}_{T}\Psi_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m},j}

\displaystyle\prec\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{(j_{1},\dots,j_{m})}N^{-\frac{1}{16}(\sigma_{j_{1}}+\dots+\sigma_{j_{m}}+\sigma_{j})}\varphi_{0}^{+}\varphi_{0}^{-}\tilde{H}_{T}\Psi_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m},j}

\displaystyle\prec(1+\mu)\varphi_{0}^{+}\varphi_{0}^{-}\tilde{H}_{T}D.

Here, the second step uses (
13.6
). In the third, penultimate, step we used the fact that for
j\in\{1,\dots,J\}
we have

N^{-\frac{1}{16}\sigma_{j}}\leqslant\frac{1}{M},

(13.8)

by (
7.6
).

Finally, in the last step we added in the terms

N^{-\frac{1}{16}(\sigma_{j_{1}}+\dots+\sigma_{j_{m}}+\sigma_{j_{m+1}})}\varphi_{0}^{+}\varphi_{0}^{-}\tilde{H}_{T}\Psi_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m},j_{m+1}},\quad j_{m+1}\neq j,

and an extra copy of
\varphi_{0}^{+}\varphi_{0}^{-}\tilde{H}_{T}
, noting that these are all Fourier-positive as we have previously seen (specifically,
\varphi_{0}^{+}\varphi_{0}^{-}\tilde{H}_{T}
is Fourier-positive by Lemma
8.7
and Proposition
8.1
(1), whilst
\Psi_{j_{1},\dots,j_{m},j_{m+1}}
is Fourier-positive because every function in
\mathscr{F}
is, as remarked after Definition
11.1
.)

This completes the proof of property (
13.4
), and hence of Proposition
13.1
, in the unexceptional case, that is to say when option (1) of Proposition
7.7
holds.

The argument in the exceptional case (option (2) of Proposition
7.7
) is essentially identical, except now the indices
j_{i}
in the above construction only range over
\{2,\dots,J\}
. Instead of (
13.8
), we need the analogous result in which
j
ranges over
\{2,\dots,J\}
. This is supplied by (
7.7
) (ignoring the contribution of
N^{-\frac{1}{16}\sigma_{1}}
in that inequality).

This completes the proof of Proposition
13.1
.∎

14.
Further bounds on correlations

The following section, which is a little technical, further enhances the main result of Section
10
(Proposition
10.1
), with an additional twist by a damping function such as the
D
constructed in Proposition
13.1
. For the purposes of this section, the precise construction of
D
is relatively unimportant and we consider general linear combinations

D(n)=\sum_{r}\sum_{b\in(\mathbf{Z}/r^{3}\mathbf{Z})^{*}}\alpha_{r,b}\Phi_{r,b}(n),

where
\sum_{r,b}|\alpha_{r,b}|\leqslant 1
. The example we really care about, of course, is the one in Proposition
13.1
.
Given such a
D
, define also the truncated sum

D_{X}(n):=\sum_{r\leqslant X}\sum_{b\in(\mathbf{Z}/r^{3}\mathbf{Z})^{*}}\alpha_{r,b}\Phi_{r,b}(n).

(14.1)

We pause to record a simple lemma which will be used a couple of times later on.

Lemma 14.1
.

Let
D
and its truncation
D_{X}
be as above. Then

\|D_{X}\|^{\wedge}_{1}\leqslant X^{\frac{5}{6}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\operatorname{s}(D_{X})\leqslant X^{3}.

(14.2)

Proof.

These follow from (
8.26
), (
8.27
) and the definition of
D_{X}
.
∎

Now we turn to the main result of the section. For the notation, see the comments after the statement of Proposition
10.1
.

Proposition 14.2
.

Let
1\leqslant Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3},Q_{4}\leqslant R
be parameters with
Q:=\min(Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3})
. Then, for any
q
,

\big\|F_{\chi,Q_{1}}^{+}\Lambda_{Q_{2}}^{-}H_{Q_{3}}D_{Q_{4}}1_{q|n}-\tilde{F}_{\chi,R}^{+}\tilde{\Lambda}_{R}^{-}\tilde{H}_{R}D1_{q|n}\big\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}\ll R^{o(1)}\big(Q_{4}^{-\frac{1}{6}}+Q_{4}^{\frac{5}{6}}Q^{-\frac{1}{4}}\big).

We isolate a lemma from the proof.

Lemma 14.3
.

We have
\operatorname{Av}|\tilde{H}_{R}\Phi_{r,b}|\ll r^{-\frac{1}{6}+o(1)}\log^{3}R
.

Proof.

Recall from the definition, Definition
8.8
, that
\tilde{H}_{R}=\prod_{p\leqslant R}\tau^{2}_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}
. From the definition of
\tau^{2}_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}
(see (
8.14
)) we have the pointwise bound

\tilde{H}_{R}(n)\leqslant 4^{\omega_{R}(n)},

(14.3)

where
\omega_{R}(n)
denotes the number of distinct prime factors
p
of
n
with
p\leqslant R
. This upper bound can occasionally be rather large, so we must be careful. So that we can quote existing number-theoretic estimates, it is convenient to compute the average of
\tilde{H}_{R}(n)\Phi_{r,b}(n)
by averaging over
n\leqslant X
then letting
X
tend to infinity.

By (
14.3
) This average is bounded above by

r^{\frac{5}{6}}X^{-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\leqslant X\\
r|n\end{subarray}}4^{\omega_{R}(n)}\leqslant r^{\frac{5}{6}}X^{-1}4^{\omega_{R}(r)}\sum_{k\leqslant\frac{X}{r}}4^{\omega_{R}(k)},

where in the second step we used the inequality
\omega_{R}(rk)\leqslant\omega_{R}(k)+\omega_{R}(r)
. We have
4^{\omega_{R}(r)}\leqslant\tau(r)^{2}\ll r^{o(1)}
by the divisor bound. Applying Lemma
B.4
(and letting
X\rightarrow\infty
) completes the proof.
∎

Proof of Proposition
14.2
.

First we claim that

\big\|\tilde{F}_{\chi,R}^{+}\tilde{\Lambda}_{R}^{-}\tilde{H}_{R}(D-D_{Q_{4}})\big\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}\ll Q_{4}^{-\frac{1}{6}}R^{o(1)}.

(14.4)

We have

D(n)-D_{Q_{4}}(n)=\sum_{r>Q_{4}}\sum_{b(\mbox{\scriptsize mod}\,r^{3})}\alpha_{b,r}\Phi_{r,b}(n),

where
\sum|\alpha_{b,r}|\leqslant 1
, and therefore by Lemma
14.3

\operatorname{Av}|\tilde{H}_{R}(D-D_{Q_{4}})|\leqslant\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}r>Q_{4}\\
b\in\mathbf{Z}/r^{3}\mathbf{Z}\end{subarray}}\operatorname{Av}|\tilde{H}_{R}\Phi_{r,b}|\ll Q_{4}^{-\frac{1}{6}}R^{o(1)}.

(14.5)

Therefore by (
8.3
)

\displaystyle\|\tilde{F}_{\chi,R}^{+}

\displaystyle\tilde{\Lambda}_{R}^{-}\tilde{H}_{R}(D-D_{Q_{4}})\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}\leqslant\operatorname{Av}|\tilde{F}_{\chi,R}^{+}\tilde{\Lambda}_{R}^{-}\tilde{H}_{R}(D-D_{Q_{4}})|

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\tilde{F}_{\chi,R}\|_{\infty}\|\tilde{\Lambda}_{R}\|_{\infty}\operatorname{Av}|\tilde{H}_{R}(D-D_{Q_{4}})|\ll Q_{4}^{-\frac{1}{6}}R^{o(1)},

where in the final step here we used (
14.5
) and the pointwise bounds
\|\tilde{F}_{\chi_{R}}\|_{\infty},\|\tilde{\Lambda}_{R}\|_{\infty}\ll\log R
, which follow from (
10.1
) and the fact that
\prod_{p\leqslant R}\frac{p}{p-1}\ll\log R
. This confirms (
14.4
).

Now by Lemma
14.1
and (
8.8
) we have

\|fD_{Q_{4}}\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}\leqslant Q^{\frac{5}{6}}_{4}\|f\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}.

(14.6)

for any periodic function
f
.

Now we are ready to prove Proposition
14.2
itself. From Proposition
10.1
and (
14.6
) we have

\big\|F^{+}_{\chi,Q_{1}}\Lambda_{Q_{2}}^{-}H_{Q_{3}}D_{Q_{4}}-\tilde{F}^{+}_{\chi,R}\tilde{\Lambda}_{R}^{-}\tilde{H}_{R}D_{Q_{4}}\big\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}\ll R^{o(1)}Q_{4}^{\frac{5}{6}}\min(Q_{1},Q_{2},Q_{3})^{-\frac{1}{4}}.

From this, (
14.4
) and the triangle inequality, we obtain Proposition
14.2
, but without the
1_{q|n}
terms.

The same bound holds
with
the
1_{q|n}
terms included, using (
8.8
) and the fact (see (
8.4
)) that
\|(1_{q|n})^{\wedge}\|_{1}=1
.
∎

15.
The main construction and completion of the proof

15.1.
Introduction

We turn now to the proof of Theorem
2.2
itself. A brief overview of the construction of a suitable function
\Psi
was given in Section
3
of the paper, and the reader might find it helpful to review that section.

In this section, we write
c_{i}:=(\frac{1}{100})^{i}
. Here,
\frac{1}{100}
is a convenient (but by no means optimised) small exponent which works for our argument. Any smaller exponent would also work but would give even weaker quantitative results. As elsewhere, we will use the
\lessapprox
,
\gtrapprox
and
\tilde{O}
notations to mean up to a factor of
N^{o(1)}
.

Let us now give an actual definition of the function
\Psi
. As it has been throughout the paper, let
N
be some large integer parameter. Let
T
be a scale satisfying the conclusions of Proposition
7.7
.

As in Proposition
7.7
, we write
\rho_{1},\rho_{2},\dots,\rho_{J}
for the zeros in
\Xi_{T}=\Xi_{T}(\frac{1}{48})
in decreasing order of their real parts
\sigma_{j}
. Recall from (
7.8
) that we have the crude bound
J\ll T
.

Definition 15.1
.

With parameters as above, define

\Psi(n):=\Lambda^{\prime}(n+1)\Lambda_{T^{c_{1}}}(n-1)H_{T^{c_{1}}}(n)D_{T^{c_{2}}}(n)E(n)w(n)1_{n\leqslant N},

(15.1)

for
n\geqslant 0
, where

•

\Lambda^{\prime}
is the von Mangoldt function restricted to primes, that is to say
\Lambda^{\prime}(p)=\log p
for
p
a prime;

•

\Lambda_{Q}
is the classical approximant to the von Mangoldt function, defined in (
3.3
);

•

H_{Q}
is a certain weight function which behaves like a kind of truncated version of
\tau^{2}
, and whose definition is given in Definition
8.9
;

•

D_{Q}
is the truncated version (as described in (
14.1
)) of
D
, the damping term whose existence was shown in Proposition
13.1
;

•

E
is the constant function
1
in the unexceptional case, and is
1_{q_{1}|n}
in the exceptional case, where
q_{1}
is the conductor of the exceptional character
q_{1}
;

•

w
is the function in Proposition
12.1
.

Recall that the terms “unexceptional” and “exceptional” here refer to options (1) and (2) respectively in the conclusion of Proposition
7.7
.

Henceforth, it is convenient to adopt the convention that
q_{1}:=1
in the unexceptional case, thus
E(n)=1_{q_{1}|n}
in all cases. This avoids a certain amount of rather tedious division into cases.

Our claim is that this function
\Psi
satisfies the conclusions of Theorem
2.2
, with
\kappa_{1}=\frac{1}{6}c_{2}\tau_{0}
and
\kappa_{2}=2c_{3}\tau_{0}
, where
T=N^{\tau_{0}}
. Recall that, by Proposition
7.7
,
c\leqslant\tau_{0}\leqslant\frac{1}{120}
(where the constant
c
comes from Proposition
7.7
). Let us first note that
\Psi
is a real-valued function because all seven of the functions making up the product in (
15.1
) are. It is also clear that
\Psi
satisfies the support property (1) of Theorem
2.2
, because
\Lambda^{\prime}
does. It therefore remains to confirm items (2) and (3) of Theorem
2.2
with the values we have just stated, that is to say the estimates

\sum_{n=1}^{N}\Psi(n)\cos(2\pi\theta n)\gtrapprox-NT^{-\frac{1}{6}c_{2}}

(15.2)

for all
\theta\in\mathbf{R}/\mathbf{Z}
and

\sum_{n=1}^{N}\Psi(n)\gtrapprox NT^{-2c_{3}}.

(15.3)

15.2.
Passing to a sharp approximant for
\Lambda

The first step in establishing (
15.2
), (
15.3
) is to reduce matters to the corresponding statements where
\Psi
is replaced by an auxilliary function
\Psi^{\prime}
in which
\Lambda^{\prime}
is replaced by
\Lambda_{\sharp}
, where

\Lambda_{\sharp}:=\Lambda_{\sharp,T,\frac{1}{48}},

(15.4)

the approximant to the primes constructed in Part
II
. Thus, we define

\Psi^{\prime}(n):=\Lambda_{\sharp}(n+1)\Lambda_{T^{c_{1}}}(n-1)H_{T^{c_{1}}}(n)D_{T^{c_{2}}}(n)E(n)w(n)1_{n\leqslant N}.

(15.5)

for
n\geqslant 0
. Note that
\Psi^{\prime}
is also real-valued since, in addition to the functions mentioned above,
\Lambda_{\sharp}
is real-valued by Lemma
4.5
.

Lemma 15.2
.

We have

\sup_{\theta}\big|\sum_{n=1}^{N}(\Psi-\Psi^{\prime})(n)\cos(2\pi\theta n)\big|\ll NT^{-\frac{1}{20}}.

Proof.

Consider the function
F:=\Lambda_{T^{c_{1}}}^{-}H_{T^{c_{1}}}D_{T^{c_{2}}}E
. By (
8.7
) we have

\|F\|^{\wedge}_{1}\leqslant\|\Lambda_{T^{c_{1}}}\|^{\wedge}_{1}\|H_{T^{c_{1}}}\|^{\wedge}_{1}\|D_{T^{c_{2}}}\|^{\wedge}_{1}\|E\|^{\wedge}_{1}\lessapprox T^{2c_{1}+c_{2}},

(15.6)

where here we have used (
8.12
), (
8.22
), Lemma
14.1
and (
8.4
) respectively for the four terms in the product.

Let us write
w=w_{0}+w_{1}
as in Proposition
12.1
(6), with
X:=T^{\frac{1}{12}}
. Then

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{N}(\Psi-\Psi^{\prime})(n)\cos(2\pi\theta n)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=0,1}\Re\sum_{n=1}^{N}(\Lambda^{\prime}(n)-\Lambda_{\sharp}(n))F(n)w_{i}(n)e(n\theta)

\displaystyle=E_{0}+E_{1},

(15.7)

say.
For the term
E_{0}
we use the crude pointwise bound
|F(n)|\lessapprox T^{2c_{1}+c_{2}}
, which follows from (
15.6
) and the rational Fourier expansion
F(n)=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbf{Q}/\mathbf{Z}}F^{\wedge}(\lambda)e(\lambda n)
, obtaining a bound of

\lessapprox T^{2c_{1}+c_{2}}\big(\sum_{n=1}^{N}\Lambda^{\prime}(n)w_{0}(n)+\sum_{n=1}^{N}|\Lambda_{\sharp}(n)|w_{0}(n)\big).

The first sum, over
\Lambda^{\prime}
, is
\ll(\log N)\sum_{n=1}^{N}w_{0}(n)\lessapprox NT^{-\frac{1}{12}}
by (
12.2
) and the choice of
X
.

The second sum, over
\Lambda_{\sharp}
is a little more delicate since this function is somewhat large for small
n
. To handle this we split the sum over
n
into the ranges
1\leqslant n\leqslant N^{\frac{1}{2}}
and
N^{\frac{1}{2}}<n\leqslant N
. On both ranges we use the pointwise estimate (
6.15
) which, in the notation introduced at the start of the section, implies that

|\Lambda_{\sharp}(n)|\lessapprox\sum_{j=1}^{J}n^{-\sigma_{j}}.

(15.8)

For
n\leqslant N^{\frac{1}{2}}
we just bound this crudely by
\lessapprox J\lessapprox T
(this being (
7.8
)).

Therefore

\sum_{n\leqslant N^{\frac{1}{2}}}|\Lambda_{\sharp}(n)|w_{0}(n)\lessapprox T\sum_{n\leqslant N^{\frac{1}{2}}}w(n)\lessapprox T\sum_{n\leqslant N^{\frac{1}{2}}}\big(\frac{n}{Y}\big)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\lessapprox N^{\frac{3}{4}}T.

(Recall here that
Y:=\frac{N}{2\log N}
, this quantity being involved in the definition (
12.5
) of
w
.)
When
n>N^{\frac{1}{2}}
, (
15.8
) gives the stronger bound
|\Lambda_{\sharp}(n)|\lessapprox 1
, since we have the estimate
\sum_{j=1}^{J}N^{-\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{j}}\ll 1
by (
7.6
) (in the unexceptional case) or (
7.7
) (in the exceptional case). Therefore, by another application of (
12.2
),

\sum_{N^{\frac{1}{2}}<n\leqslant N}|\Lambda_{\sharp}(n)|w_{0}(n)\lessapprox\sum_{n\leqslant N}w_{0}(n)\lessapprox NT^{-\frac{1}{12}}.

Putting all this together, we obtain

E_{0}\lessapprox NT^{2c_{1}+c_{2}-\frac{1}{12}}.

(15.9)

Turning now to the term
E_{1}
in (
15.7
) we use the expansion
w_{1}(n)=\int_{\mathbf{R}}a(\xi)e(\frac{\xi n}{Y})d\xi
from Proposition
12.1
(6) and write the rational Fourier expansion of
F
as
F(n)=\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbf{Q}/\mathbf{Z}}F^{\wedge}(\lambda)e(\lambda n)
, obtaining

\displaystyle E_{1}=\Re\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbf{Q}/\mathbf{Z}}F^{\wedge}(\lambda)\int_{\mathbf{R}}a(\xi)\sum_{n=1}^{N}(\Lambda^{\prime}(n)-\Lambda_{\sharp}(n))e\big((\lambda+\theta+\frac{\xi}{Y})n\big)d\xi

\displaystyle\leqslant\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbf{Q}/\mathbf{Z}}|F^{\wedge}(\lambda)|\int_{\mathbf{R}}|a(\xi)|\sup_{\lambda,\xi}\bigg|\sum_{n=1}^{N}(\Lambda^{\prime}(n)-\Lambda_{\sharp}(n))e\big((\lambda+\theta+\frac{\xi}{Y})n\big)\bigg|d\xi.

By Proposition
4.6
with
Q=T
, (
12.3
) and (
15.6
),

E_{1}\lessapprox NT^{-\frac{1}{6}}\cdot T^{2c_{1}+c_{2}}\cdot T^{\frac{1}{12}}.

Adding this to (
15.9
) concludes the proof of Lemma
15.2
, using the fact that
2c_{1}+c_{2}-\frac{1}{12}<-\frac{1}{20}
.
∎

Let us now formally state the remaining task, which is to establish the analogues of (
15.2
), (
15.3
) with
\Psi^{\prime}
in place of
\Psi
.

Proposition 15.3
.

The function
\Psi^{\prime}:\mathbf{Z}\rightarrow\mathbf{R}
satisfies

\sum_{n=1}^{N}\Psi^{\prime}(n)\cos(2\pi\theta n)\gtrapprox-NT^{-\frac{1}{6}c_{2}}

(15.10)

and

\sum_{n=1}^{N}\Psi^{\prime}(n)\gtrapprox NT^{-2c_{3}}.

(15.11)

Note that (
15.2
) and (
15.3
) follow from these two statements by Lemma
15.2
(with
\theta=0
in the case of (
15.3
)) and the fact that
\frac{1}{6}c_{2},2c_{3}<\frac{1}{20}
. As explained at the end of Subsection
15.1
, this completes the proof of Theorem
2.2
and hence of all our main results, conditional upon Proposition
15.3
.

15.3.
Fourier coefficients
\beta_{j},\beta^{\operatorname{trunc}}_{j}
and their properties

For the remainder of the section, for each
j\in\{0,\dots,J\}
write

\beta_{j}:=(\tilde{F}_{\chi_{j},T}^{+}\tilde{\Lambda}_{T}^{-}\tilde{H}_{T}DE)^{\wedge}

and

\beta_{j}^{\operatorname{trunc}}:=(F_{\chi_{j},T}^{+}\Lambda_{T^{c_{1}}}^{-}H_{T^{c_{1}}}D_{T^{c_{2}}}E)^{\wedge}.

To be clear, the
\wedge
here indicates rational Fourier coefficients in the sense of Section
8
, thus both
\beta_{j}
and
\beta_{j}^{\operatorname{trunc}}
are defined on
\mathbf{Q}/\mathbf{Z}
.

Let us collect some basic observations about these quantities.

Lemma 15.4
.

We have the following:

(1)

\|\beta_{0}\|_{\infty}\lessapprox 1
;

(2)

\beta_{0}
is real, non-negative and symmetric, i.e.
\beta_{0}(\lambda)=\beta_{0}(-\lambda)
;

(3)

\beta_{j}^{\operatorname{trunc}}(\lambda)
is supported where
\operatorname{denom}(\lambda)\leqslant T^{3}
;

(4)

\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbf{Q}/\mathbf{Z}}|\beta^{\operatorname{trunc}}_{j}(\lambda)|\ll T^{2}
;

(5)

\|\beta^{\operatorname{trunc}}_{j}-\beta_{j}\|_{\infty}\lessapprox T^{-\frac{1}{6}c_{2}}
uniformly for all
j
;

(6)

|\beta_{j}(\lambda)|\leqslant N^{\frac{1}{8}\sigma_{j}}\beta_{0}(\lambda)
for all
j\geqslant 1

(
in the unexceptional case
)
and all
j\geqslant 2

(
in the exceptional case
)
;

(7)

in the exceptional case,
\beta_{0}=\beta_{1}
;

(8)

we have
\beta_{0}(0)\gg q_{1}^{-1}
.

Proof.

Recall throughout that
\tilde{F}_{\chi_{0},T}=\tilde{\Lambda}_{T}
and
F_{\chi_{0},T}=\Lambda_{T}
. In particular,
\beta_{0}=(\tilde{\Lambda}_{T}^{+}\tilde{\Lambda}_{T}^{-}\tilde{H}_{T}DE)^{\wedge}
.

(1) By (
8.3
) we have

\displaystyle\|\beta_{0}\|_{\infty}=\|\tilde{\Lambda}_{T}^{+}\tilde{\Lambda}_{T}^{-}\tilde{H}_{T}DE\|^{\wedge}_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leqslant\operatorname{Av}|\tilde{\Lambda}_{T}^{+}\tilde{\Lambda}_{T}^{-}\tilde{H}_{T}DE|

\displaystyle\leqslant\|\tilde{\Lambda}_{T}\|_{\infty}^{2}\|E\|_{\infty}\operatorname{Av}|\tilde{H}_{T}D|.

(15.12)

By Lemma
14.3
we have
\operatorname{Av}|\tilde{H}_{T}\Phi_{r,b}|\lessapprox 1
uniformly for all
r,b
and so, since
D
is a convex combination of functions
\Phi_{r,b}
,
\operatorname{Av}|\tilde{H}_{T}D|\lessapprox 1
. We also have
\|E\|_{\infty}=1
(clear from the definition of
E
) and
\|\tilde{\Lambda}_{T}\|_{\infty}=\prod_{p\leqslant T}(1-\frac{1}{p})^{-1}\ll\log T
. Substituting these bounds into (
15.12
) gives the result.

(2) This is already implicit in the statement and proof of Proposition
13.1
, but to avoid potential confusion we give a self-contained explanation. The statement is equivalent to the assertion that
\tilde{\Lambda}_{T}^{+}\tilde{\Lambda}_{T}^{-}\tilde{H}_{T}DE
is Fourier-positive as defined in Section
8
. By Proposition
8.1
(1), it is enough to show that each of the three functions
\tilde{\Lambda}_{T}^{+}\tilde{\Lambda}_{T}^{-}\tilde{H}_{T}
,
D
and
E
are Fourier-positive. For
E
, this follows from the Fourier expansion
1_{q_{1}|n}=\frac{1}{q_{1}}\sum_{a\in(\mathbf{Z}/q_{1}\mathbf{Z})^{*}}e(\frac{an}{q_{1}})
. For
D
, this follows from the fact that
D
is a convex combination of functions
\Phi_{r,b}
, all of which are Fourier-positive by (
8.25
). Finally, we have (see (
6.11
))
\tilde{\Lambda}_{T}=\prod_{p\leqslant T}\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}
and
\tilde{H}_{T}=\prod_{p\leqslant T}\tau^{2}_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}
(Definition
8.8
), so the Fourier positivity of
\tilde{\Lambda}_{T}^{+}\tilde{\Lambda}_{T}^{-}\tilde{H}_{T}
is a consequence of Lemma
8.7
, which asserts that each
\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}^{+}\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}^{-}\tau^{2}_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}
is Fourier-positive.

Finally, the symmetry property
\beta_{0}(\lambda)=\beta_{0}(-\lambda)
is a consequence of the fact that
\tilde{\Lambda}_{T}^{+}\tilde{\Lambda}_{T}^{-}\tilde{H}_{T}DE
is real-valued and the uniqueness of rational Fourier expansion.

(3) By (
8.10
) we have

\operatorname{s}(F_{\chi_{j},T}^{+}\Lambda_{T^{c_{1}}}^{-}H_{T^{c_{1}}}D_{T^{c_{2}}}E)\leqslant\operatorname{s}(F_{\chi_{j},T})\operatorname{s}(\Lambda_{T^{c_{1}}})\operatorname{s}(H_{T^{c_{1}}})\operatorname{s}(D_{T^{c_{2}}})\operatorname{s}(E).

(15.13)

Now from the definition (
4.2
) we see that
\operatorname{s}(F_{\chi_{j},T})\leqslant T\operatorname{cond}(\chi_{j})\leqslant T^{2}
. From the definition (
3.3
) we have
\operatorname{s}(\Lambda_{T^{c_{1}}})\leqslant T^{c_{1}}
, and from Definition
8.9
we have
\operatorname{s}(H_{T^{c_{1}}})\leqslant T^{c_{1}}
. From (
14.2
) we have
\operatorname{s}(D_{T^{c_{2}}})\leqslant T^{3c_{2}}
, and finally from (
8.4
) we have
\operatorname{s}(E)\leqslant q_{1}\leqslant T^{c_{3}}
(where the bound
q_{1}\leqslant T^{c_{3}}
comes from Proposition
7.7
(2)). Substituting all this into (
15.13
) (and using the crude bound
2c_{1}+3c_{2}+c_{3}<1
), we obtain the required statement.

(4) By (
8.7
) and the definition of
\beta_{j}^{\operatorname{trunc}}
, we have

\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbf{Q}/\mathbf{Z}}|\beta_{j}^{\operatorname{trunc}}(\lambda)|\leqslant\|F_{\chi_{j},T}^{\wedge}\|_{1}\|\Lambda^{\wedge}_{T^{c_{1}}}\|_{1}\|H^{\wedge}_{T^{c_{1}}}\|_{1}\|D^{\wedge}_{T^{c_{2}}}\|_{1}\|E^{\wedge}\|_{1}.

We have the bounds
\|F_{\chi_{j},T}^{\wedge}\|_{1}\leqslant T^{\frac{3}{2}}
(
8.11
),
\|\Lambda_{T^{c_{1}}}\|^{\wedge}_{1}\ll T^{c_{1}}
(
8.12
),
\|H_{T^{c_{1}}}\|^{\wedge}_{1}\ll T^{(1+o(1))c_{1}}
(
8.22
),
\|D_{T^{c_{2}}}\|^{\wedge}_{1}\ll T^{\frac{5}{6}c_{2}}
(
14.2
), and
\|E\|^{\wedge}_{1}=1
(
8.4
), and so the result follows, again using a crude bound
2c_{1}+\frac{5}{6}c_{2}<\frac{1}{2}
.

(5) This is little more than a restatement of an instance of Proposition
14.2
in the language of this section. In that proposition, we have taken
Q_{1}=R=T
,
Q_{2}=Q_{3}=T^{c_{1}}
,
Q_{4}=T^{c_{2}}
and
q=q_{1}
. Since
c_{2}
is tiny compared to
\frac{1}{4}c_{1}
, the term
Q_{4}^{-\frac{1}{6}}
in Proposition
14.2
is dominant.

(6) This is (
13.4
) in which both sides have been multiplied by
E
(which only makes a difference in the exceptional case). This preserves the
\prec
relation, by Proposition
8.1
(5).

(7) This is a consequence of the fact that
\tilde{F}^{+}_{\chi_{1},T}E=\tilde{\Lambda}^{+}_{T}E
, which is clear from the expressions (see (
13.1
))

\tilde{\Lambda}_{T}=\prod_{p\leqslant T}\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}},\quad\tilde{F}_{\chi_{1},T}=\frac{q_{1}}{\phi(q_{1})}\overline{\chi}_{1}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\leqslant T\\
p\nmid q_{1}\end{subarray}}\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}},

since
\chi_{1}(n+1)=1
when
q_{1}\mid n
. Here,
\chi_{1}
is the (real) exceptional character to modulus
q_{1}
.

(8) From Proposition
13.1
and the fact that all the
\Phi_{q,a}
are Fourier-positive, we have
\frac{3}{4}\Phi_{1,0}\prec D
. Note that
\Phi_{1,0}
is just the constant function
1
. Since
E
is Fourier-positive, we have
\frac{3}{4}E\prec DE
. Since
E(n)=\frac{1}{q_{1}}\sum_{a\in\mathbf{Z}/q_{1}\mathbf{Z}}e(\frac{an}{q_{1}})
, we also have
\frac{1}{q_{1}}\prec E
, and therefore
\frac{3}{4q_{1}}\prec DE
.

Using this and the fact that
\tilde{\Lambda}_{T}^{+}\tilde{\Lambda}_{T}^{-}\tilde{H}_{T}
is Fourier-positive (as remarked in the proof of (2) above) it follows from Proposition
8.1
that

\tilde{\Lambda}_{T}^{+}\tilde{\Lambda}_{T}^{-}\tilde{H}_{T}DE\succ\frac{3}{4q_{1}}\tilde{\Lambda}_{T}^{+}\tilde{\Lambda}_{T}^{-}\tilde{H}_{T}.

(15.14)

Now it follows from the proof of Lemma
8.7
that the constant term in the rational Fourier expansion of
\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}^{+}\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}^{-}\tau^{2}_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}
is
(1+\frac{1}{p})(1-\frac{1}{p})^{-2}(1+\frac{3}{p})^{-1}
for
p\geqslant 3
, and
\frac{16}{5}
for
p=2
. Multiplying over all
p\leqslant T
(and using the fact that all frequencies of
\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}^{+}\Lambda_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}^{-}\tau^{2}_{\mathbf{Z}/p\mathbf{Z}}
have denominator
p
) it follows that the constant term in the rational Fourier expansion of
\tilde{\Lambda}_{T}^{+}\tilde{\Lambda}_{T}^{-}\tilde{H}_{T}
is

\frac{16}{5}\prod_{3\leqslant p\leqslant T}\big(1-\frac{1}{p}\big)^{-2}\big(1+\frac{3}{p}\big)^{-1}\big(1+\frac{1}{p}\big)\gg 1.

Combining this with (
15.14
) gives the required statement.
∎

15.4.
Preliminary analysis

We now begin the proof of Proposition
15.3
. The initial steps will be the same in the unexceptional and exceptional cases.

Recall the definitions of
\Psi^{\prime}
(see (
15.5
)) and of
\Lambda_{\sharp}=\Lambda_{\sharp,T,\frac{1}{48}}
(Definition
4.4
, (
4.5
), (
15.4
)). Substituting the latter definition into the former and expanding, and writing (just for the next few lines)
\varphi_{j}:=F_{\chi_{j},T}^{+}\Lambda_{T^{c_{1}}}^{-}H_{T^{c_{1}}}D_{T^{c_{2}}}E
for brevity, we have

\sum_{n=1}^{N}\Psi^{\prime}(n)e(n\theta)=\sum_{j=0}^{J}\varepsilon_{j}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\varphi_{j}(n)(n+1)^{\rho_{j}-1}w(n)e(n\theta),

(15.15)

where
\varepsilon_{0}=1
and
\varepsilon_{j}=-1
for
j\geqslant 1
.

The first, rather minor, task is to replace
(n+1)^{\rho_{j}-1}
here with
n^{\rho_{j}-1}
, so that we can access Proposition
12.1
. First of all, we claim that if
\rho=\beta+i\gamma
with
0\leqslant\beta\leqslant 1
and
|\gamma|\leqslant T
then

\big|(n+1)^{\rho-1}-n^{\rho-1}\big|=|n^{\rho-1}|\big|(1+\frac{1}{n})^{\rho-1}-1\big]|\ll\frac{T}{n},

uniformly for positive integers
n
.
To prove this claim, we first use the trivial bound
|n^{\rho-1}|\leqslant 1
. Then, to estimate
|(1+\frac{1}{n})^{\rho-1}-1|
, we split into the cases
n\geqslant T
and
n<T
. In the case
n\geqslant T
, we use the inequality
|e^{z}-1|\ll|z|
, valid for
|z|\leqslant 10
(say), with
z=(\rho-1)\log(1+\frac{1}{n})
, which has
|z|\ll(1+|\gamma|)\frac{1}{n}\ll\frac{T}{n}
. In the case
n<T
, we use the bound
|(1+\frac{1}{n})^{\rho-1}|=(1+\frac{1}{n})^{\beta-1}\ll 1
.

Using this claim, we see that the error in replacing
(n+1)^{\rho_{j}-1}
by
n^{\rho_{j}-1}
in (
15.15
) is bounded above by

\ll JT\sup_{j}\sum_{n=1}^{N}|\varphi_{j}(n)|\frac{w(n)}{n}\ll T^{2+2c_{1}+c_{2}}N^{o(1)}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\frac{w(n)}{n}.

(15.16)

Here, the second bound follows from the first by (
7.8
) and crude pointwise estimates for the constituent functions of
\varphi_{j}
: for
F_{\chi_{j},T}
and
\Lambda_{T^{c_{1}}}
we have the pointwise bound of
N^{o(1)}
, by Corollary
6.4
; for
H_{T^{c_{1}}}
we have the (very) crude pointwise bound
|H_{T^{c_{1}}}(n)|\ll T^{2c_{1}}
, which follows from Definition
8.9
and the trivial bound
|\eta(q)|\leqslant 1
; for
D_{T^{c_{2}}}
we have the pointwise bound
|D_{T^{c_{2}}}(n)|\ll T^{\frac{5}{6}c_{2}}<T^{c_{2}}
, which is immediate from (
13.2
) and the definition (
8.24
) of
\Phi_{q,a}
; and finally for
E
we have the trivial bound
E(n)\leqslant 1
.

Now by the definition (
12.4
), (
12.5
) of
w(n)
we have

\sum_{n=1}^{N}\frac{w(n)}{n}\leqslant\sum_{n=1}^{N}\frac{1}{n}\big(\frac{n}{Y}\big)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\ll Y^{\frac{1}{2}}\ll N^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Substituting into (
15.16
) shows that the error in replacing
(n+1)^{\rho_{j}-1}
by
n^{\rho_{j}-1}
in (
15.15
) is
O(N^{\frac{2}{3}})
, that is to say

\sum_{n=1}^{N}\Psi^{\prime}(n)e(n\theta)=\sum_{j=0}^{J}\varepsilon_{j}\sum_{n=1}^{N}\varphi_{j}(n)n^{\rho_{j}-1}w(n)e(n\theta)+O(N^{\frac{2}{3}}).

Now we expand
\varphi_{j}=F_{\chi_{j},T}^{+}\Lambda_{T^{c_{1}}}^{-}H_{T^{c_{1}}}D_{T^{c_{2}}}E
as its rational Fourier series, with (by definition) coefficients
\beta^{\operatorname{trunc}}_{j}(\lambda)
. This gives

\sum_{n=1}^{N}\Psi^{\prime}(n)e(n\theta)=\sum_{j=0}^{J}\varepsilon_{j}\!\!\!\sum_{\lambda\in\mathbf{Q}/\mathbf{Z}}\!\!\!\beta_{j}^{\operatorname{trunc}}(\lambda)\sum_{n=1}^{N}n^{\rho_{j}-1}w(n)e\big((\lambda+\theta)n\big)+O(N^{\frac{2}{3}}).

(15.17)

From now on we think of
\theta\in\mathbf{R}/\mathbf{Z}
as fixed; some choices will depend on it, but all bounds will be uniform in
\theta
. Note that, by Lemma
15.4
(3), all the
\lambda
which are relevant in the above sum have
\operatorname{denom}(\lambda)\leqslant T^{3}
. Write
\lambda_{0}
for the rational closest to
-\theta
(mod 1) with
\operatorname{denom}(\lambda_{0})\leqslant T^{3}
. We claim that the contribution to (
15.17
) from all
\lambda\neq\lambda_{0}
is small. To see this, note that for such
\lambda
we have both
\|\lambda+\theta\|\geqslant\|\lambda_{0}+\theta\|
(by the assumption that
\lambda_{0}
is closest to
-\theta
) and
\|\lambda-\lambda_{0}\|\geqslant T^{-6}
(since
\operatorname{denom}(\lambda),\operatorname{denom}(\lambda_{0})\leqslant T^{3}
). By the triangle inequality, we see that
\|\lambda+\theta\|\geqslant\frac{1}{2}T^{-6}>N^{-\frac{1}{2}}
, recalling here that we chose
T<N^{\frac{1}{120}}
in Proposition
7.7
. Therefore the contribution to the sum in (
15.17
) from
\lambda\neq\lambda_{0}
is bounded above by

\displaystyle\ll JT^{6}\sup_{j}\|\beta^{\operatorname{trunc}}_{j}\|_{\infty}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\|\eta\|\geqslant\frac{1}{2}N^{-\frac{1}{2}}\\
\rho\in\Xi^{*}_{T}(\frac{1}{48})\end{subarray}}\big|\sum_{n=1}^{N}n^{\rho-1}w(n)e(\eta n)\big|

\displaystyle\lessapprox JT^{6}\cdot T^{2}\cdot N^{1-\frac{1}{12}}\ll N^{1-\frac{1}{24}}.

In the last step, we used
J\ll T
(by (
7.8
)), Lemma
15.4
(4), Proposition
12.1
(3) and the fact that
T<N^{\frac{1}{120}}
(Proposition
7.7
).

It follows from this analysis and (
15.17
) that
\sum_{n=1}^{N}\Psi^{\prime}(n)e(n\theta)
is

\sum_{j=0}^{J}\varepsilon_{j}\beta_{j}^{\operatorname{trunc}}(\lambda_{0})\sum_{n=1}^{N}w(n)n^{\rho_{j}-1}e\big((\lambda_{0}+\theta)n\big)+O(N^{1-\frac{1}{24}}).

(15.18)

Next, we wish to replace the
\beta^{\operatorname{trunc}}
terms by their untruncated variants
\beta
. The main tool here is Lemma
15.4
(5) (which, recall, is a substantial result, requiring all of Sections
9
,
10
and
14
for its proof). By that result, the error introduced by replacing
\beta_{j}^{\operatorname{trunc}}
by
\beta_{j}
in (
15.18
) is bounded above by

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{J}|\beta^{\operatorname{trunc}}_{j}(\lambda_{0})-\beta_{j}(\lambda_{0})|

\displaystyle\big|\sum_{n=1}^{N}n^{\rho_{j}-1}w(n)e\big((\lambda_{0}+\theta)n\big)\big|

\displaystyle\lessapprox T^{-\frac{1}{6}c_{2}}\sum_{j=0}^{J}\big|\sum_{n=1}^{N}n^{\rho_{j}-1}w(n)e(\eta n)\big|.

Here, we have written
\eta:=\lambda_{0}+\theta
. By Proposition
12.1
(4), this in turn is bounded by

\lessapprox T^{-\frac{1}{6}c_{2}}\big(\sum_{n=1}^{N}w(n)\cos(2\pi\eta n)\big)\sum_{j=0}^{J}N^{-\frac{1}{4}\sigma_{j}}+O(JN^{1-\frac{1}{12}}).

(15.19)

By (
7.6
) or (
7.7
) (depending on whether we are in the unexceptional case or the exceptional case) and the bound
\sum_{n=1}^{N}w(n)\ll N
(which is Proposition
12.1
in the case
\sigma=0
), (
15.19
) is
\lessapprox NT^{-\frac{1}{6}c_{2}}
. Note that the error term of
O(JN^{1-\frac{1}{12}})
is negligible since
J\ll T\leqslant N^{\frac{1}{120}}
by Proposition
7.7
.

It follows from this analysis and (
15.18
) that

\sum_{n=1}^{N}\Psi^{\prime}(n)e(n\theta)=\sum_{j=0}^{J}\varepsilon_{j}\beta_{j}(\lambda_{0})\sum_{n=1}^{N}w(n)n^{\rho_{j}-1}e(\eta n)+\tilde{O}(NT^{-\frac{1}{6}c_{2}}).

Recall here that
\eta:=\lambda_{0}+\theta
; observe also that the error term of
O(N^{1-\frac{1}{24}})
in (
15.18
) is much smaller than the error term here, so was absorbed into it.

We may run an essentially identical analysis with
-\theta
in place of
\theta
. Since
-\lambda_{0}
is the closest rational to
-\theta
(mod 1) with
\operatorname{denom}(\lambda_{0})\leqslant T^{3}
, we obtain

\sum_{n=1}^{N}\Psi^{\prime}(n)e(-n\theta)=\sum_{j=0}^{J}\varepsilon_{j}\beta_{j}(-\lambda_{0})\sum_{n=1}^{N}w(n)n^{\rho_{j}-1}e(-\eta n)+\tilde{O}(NT^{-\frac{1}{6}c_{2}}).

Adding these together, and using the symmetry of
\beta_{0}
(Lemma
15.4
(2)) we have

\displaystyle 2\sum_{n=1}^{N}

\displaystyle\Psi^{\prime}(n)\cos(2\pi\theta n)=2\beta_{0}(\lambda_{0})\sum_{n=1}^{N}w(n)\cos(2\pi\eta n)

\displaystyle-\sum_{\pm}\sum_{j\geqslant 1}\beta_{j}(\pm\lambda_{0})\sum_{n=1}^{N}w(n)n^{\rho_{j}-1}e(\pm\eta n)+\tilde{O}(NT^{-\frac{1}{6}c_{2}}).

(15.20)

(The
\sum_{\pm}
notation means that we take the sum of two terms, one for each choice of sign.)

From this point on, the treatments in the unexceptional and exceptional cases are slightly different.

15.5.
The unexceptional case

We start with (
15.20
). Taking absolute values of the terms with
j\geqslant 1
and using Lemma
15.4
(6) gives

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{N}

\displaystyle\Psi^{\prime}(n)\cos(2\pi\theta n)\geqslant\beta_{0}(\lambda_{0})\bigg(2\sum_{n}w(n)\cos(2\pi\eta n)-

\displaystyle-\sum_{\pm}\sum_{j=1}^{J}N^{\frac{1}{8}\sigma_{j}}\big|\sum_{n=1}^{N}n^{\rho_{j}-1}w(n)e(\pm\eta n)\big|\bigg)+\tilde{O}(NT^{-\frac{1}{6}c_{2}}).

(15.21)

Proposition
12.1
(4) gives that

\big|\sum_{n=1}^{N}n^{\rho_{j}-1}w(n)e(\pm\eta n)\big|\leqslant 11N^{-\frac{1}{4}\sigma_{j}}\sum_{n=1}^{N}w(n)\cos(2\pi\eta n)+O(N^{1-\frac{1}{12}}).

(15.22)

When substituted into (
15.21
), the contribution of the error terms
O(N^{1-\frac{1}{12}})
is
\ll|\beta_{0}(\lambda_{0})|J\sup_{j}N^{\frac{1}{8}\sigma_{j}}N^{1-\frac{1}{12}}\lessapprox NT^{-\frac{1}{6}c_{2}}
. Here, we used the fact that
\sigma_{j}\leqslant\frac{1}{48}
for all
j
; the bound
|\beta_{0}(\lambda_{0})|\lessapprox 1
(Lemma
15.4
(1)); the bound
J\ll T
(
7.8
); and finally the fact that
T\leqslant N^{\frac{1}{120}}
(Proposition
7.7
).

Therefore, substituting (
15.22
) into (
15.21
) gives

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{N}\Psi^{\prime}(n)\cos(2\pi\theta n)

\displaystyle\geqslant 2\beta_{0}(\lambda_{0})\big(\sum_{n}w(n)\cos(2\pi\eta n)\big)\big(1-11\sum_{j=1}^{J}N^{-\frac{1}{8}\sigma_{j}}\big)-\tilde{O}(NT^{-\frac{1}{6}c_{2}}).

(15.23)

Now by (
7.6
) (and since
M
is vastly bigger than
11
) we have
1-11\sum_{j=1}^{J}N^{-\frac{1}{8}\sigma_{j}}\geqslant\frac{1}{2}
. Also, taking
\rho=1
in Proposition
12.1
(4) gives

\sum_{n=1}^{N}w(n)\cos(2\pi\eta n)\geqslant-O(N^{1-\frac{1}{12}}).

(15.24)

Since
|\beta_{0}(\lambda_{0})|\lessapprox 1
(Lemma
15.4
(1)) it follows from these observations and (
15.23
) that

\sum_{n=1}^{N}\Psi^{\prime}(n)\cos(2\pi\theta n)\geqslant-\tilde{O}(NT^{-\frac{1}{6}c_{2}}).

This completes the proof of (
15.10
) (in the unexceptional case).

We turn now to (
15.11
). We set
\theta=0
in the above analysis, thus
\lambda_{0}=\eta=0
and (
15.23
) gives

\sum_{n=1}^{N}\Psi^{\prime}(n)\geqslant 2\beta_{0}(0)\big(\sum_{n=1}^{N}w(n)\big)\big(1-11\sum_{j=1}^{J}N^{-\frac{1}{8}\sigma_{j}}\big)-\tilde{O}(NT^{-\frac{1}{6}c_{2}}).

By (
7.6
), Proposition
12.1
(2) and Lemma
15.4
(8), it follows that

\sum_{n=1}^{N}\Psi^{\prime}(n)\geqslant c\frac{N}{\log N}-\tilde{O}(NT^{-\frac{1}{6}c_{2}}).

for some
c>0
. This is considerably stronger than (
15.11
), and so this completes the proof of Proposition
15.3
in the unexceptional case.

15.6.
The exceptional case

We now look at what happens in the exceptional case. Thus
E(n)=1_{q_{1}|n}
, where
q_{1}=\operatorname{cond}(\chi_{1})
is the conductor of the exceptional character.

As in the unexceptional case, our point of departure is (
15.20
). Now, however, we separate off the term
j=1
and use Lemma
15.4
(7), obtaining (recalling here that
\rho_{1}=1-\sigma_{1}
is real)

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{N}\Psi^{\prime}(n)

\displaystyle\cos(2\pi\theta n)=2\beta_{0}(\lambda_{0})\sum_{n=1}^{N}w(n)(1-n^{-\sigma_{1}})\cos(2\pi\eta n)-

\displaystyle-\sum_{\pm}\sum_{j\geqslant 2}\beta_{j}(\pm\lambda_{0})\sum_{n=1}^{N}w(n)n^{\rho_{j}-1}e(\pm\eta n)-\tilde{O}(NT^{-\frac{1}{6}c_{2}}).

Taking absolute values and using Lemma
15.4
(6) gives

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{N}\Psi^{\prime}(n)

\displaystyle\cos(2\pi\theta n)\geqslant\beta_{0}(\lambda_{0})\bigg(2\sum_{n=1}^{N}w(n)(1-n^{-\sigma_{1}})\cos(2\pi\eta n)-

\displaystyle-\sum_{\pm}\sum_{j\geqslant 2}N^{\frac{1}{8}\sigma_{j}}\big|\sum_{n=1}^{N}w(n)n^{\rho_{j}-1}e(\pm\eta n)\big|\bigg)-\tilde{O}(NT^{-\frac{1}{6}c_{2}}).

Applying Proposition
12.1
(4) and handling the error term exactly as in the unexceptional case, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{N}

\displaystyle\Psi^{\prime}(n)\cos(2\pi\theta n)\geqslant\beta_{0}(\lambda_{0})\bigg(2\sum_{n=1}^{N}w(n)(1-n^{-\sigma_{1}})\cos(2\pi\eta n)-

\displaystyle-2M\big(\sum_{n=1}^{N}w(n)\cos(2\pi\eta n)\big)\sum_{j\geqslant 1}N^{-\frac{1}{8}\sigma_{j}}\bigg)-\tilde{O}(NT^{-\frac{1}{6}c_{2}}).

(15.25)

Now we additionally apply Proposition
12.1
(5), which tells us that

\sum_{n=1}^{N}n^{-\sigma_{1}}w(n)\cos(2\pi\eta n)\leqslant N^{-\frac{1}{4}\sigma_{1}}\sum_{n=1}^{N}w(n)\cos(2\pi\eta n)+O(N^{1-\frac{1}{12}}).

Substituting into (
15.25
) and using
|\beta_{0}(\lambda_{0})|\lessapprox 1
(that is, Lemma
15.4
(1)) to control the error term, we obtain

\sum_{n=1}^{N}\Psi^{\prime}(n)\cos(2\pi\theta n)\geqslant 2\tau\beta_{0}(\lambda_{0})\sum_{n=1}^{N}w(n)\cos(2\pi\eta n)-\tilde{O}(NT^{-\frac{1}{6}c_{2}}).

(15.26)

where

\tau:=1-N^{-\frac{1}{4}\sigma_{1}}-M\sum_{j\geqslant 2}N^{-\frac{1}{8}\sigma_{j}}.

Now (
7.7
) implies that
\tau\geqslant 0
; in fact, it implies

\tau\geqslant\frac{1}{2}(1-N^{-\frac{1}{8}\sigma_{1}}),

(15.27)

a fact we shall need shortly.
We again have (
15.24
) and
|\beta_{0}(\lambda_{0})|\lessapprox 1
, and therefore (
15.26
) implies that

\sum_{n=1}^{N}\Psi^{\prime}(n)\cos(2\pi\theta n)\geqslant-O(NT^{-\frac{1}{6}c_{2}}).

This confirms (
15.10
) in the exceptional case.

Finally, we turn to the proof of (
15.11
) in the exceptional case. Setting
\theta=0
in (
15.26
), we obtain

\sum_{n=1}^{N}\Psi^{\prime}(n)\geqslant 2\tau\beta_{0}(0)\sum_{n=1}^{N}w(n)-\tilde{O}(NT^{-\frac{1}{6}c_{2}}).

(15.28)

To estimate this, we use the inequalities
\beta_{0}(0)\gg q_{1}^{-1}
(Lemma
15.4
(8)),
\sum_{n=1}^{N}w(n)\gg\frac{N}{\log N}
(Proposition
12.1
(2)) and
\tau\geqslant\frac{1}{2}(1-N^{-\frac{1}{8}\sigma_{1}})
(which is (
15.27
)).
Substituting into (
15.28
) gives

\sum_{n=1}^{N}\Psi^{\prime}(n)\gg q_{1}^{-1}\frac{N}{\log N}\big(1-N^{-\frac{1}{8}\sigma_{1}})-\tilde{O}(NT^{-\frac{1}{6}c_{2}}).

(15.29)

Now we have
1-N^{-\frac{1}{8}\sigma_{1}}\geqslant 1-e^{-\frac{1}{8}\sigma_{1}}\gg\sigma_{1}
, and by Page’s theorem (Lemma
7.8
)
\sigma_{1}\gg q_{1}^{-1}
. Therefore (
15.29
) implies that

\sum_{n=1}^{N}\Psi^{\prime}(n)\gg q_{1}^{-2}\frac{N}{\log N}-\tilde{O}(NT^{-\frac{1}{6}c_{2}}).

Finally, recall from Proposition
7.7
that
q_{1}\leqslant T^{c_{3}}
. Therefore

\sum_{n=1}^{N}\Psi^{\prime}(n)\gtrapprox NT^{-2c_{3}}-\tilde{O}(NT^{-\frac{1}{6}c_{2}})\gtrapprox NT^{-2c_{3}}

since
c_{3}<\frac{1}{12}c_{2}
.

This concludes the proof of (
15.11
) in the exceptional case, and hence the proof of Proposition
15.3
itself.

As remarked at the end of Subsection
15.2
, this completes the proof of all the main results in the paper.

Part V
Appendices

Appendix A
Postnikov character formula

In this section we assemble material that is well-known to experts but for which I could not locate a convenient reference covering all that is needed for this paper in a quotable form. In preparing this we used
[
7
]
,
[
12
, Section 12.6]
and
[
18
, Lemma 13]
.

Define
L_{N}\in\mathbf{Q}[x]
by

L_{N}(x):=-\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{(-x)^{i}}{i}=x-\frac{x^{2}}{2}+\frac{x^{3}}{3}+\dots\pm\frac{x^{N}}{N}.

(A.1)

Lemma A.1
.

L_{N}(x+y+xy)=L_{N}(x)+L_{N}(y)+\sum_{N<i+j\leqslant 2N}c_{ij}x^{i}y^{j},

with the
(c_{ij})_{i,j\geqslant 0}
being rationals with denominator
\leqslant N
.

Proof.

This is
[
7
, Lemma 1]
.∎

Remarks.
For the purposes of this paper, we only need the case
N=2
, which is an easy direct check, but this sits most naturally within the more general theory. Of course,
L_{N}
is a truncated version of the series for
\log(1+x)
, and then Lemma
A.1
becomes a truncated version of
\log\big((1+x)(1+y)\big)=\log(1+x)+\log(1+y)
.

Denote by
\mathbf{Z}_{(p)}\subseteq\mathbf{Q}
the ring of rationals whose denominator is coprime to
p
(which, abstractly, is the localisation of
\mathbf{Z}
at
p
). Since
v_{p}(i)\leqslant i
for all
i,p
, one sees that

L_{N}(px)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{(-px)^{i}}{i}\in\mathbf{Z}_{(p)}[x].

(A.2)

Let
\pi_{n}:\mathbf{Z}_{(p)}\rightarrow\mathbf{Z}/p^{n}\mathbf{Z}
be the natural ring homomorphism. Let
\eta:\mathbf{Z}/p^{n}\mathbf{Z}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}^{\times}
be the additive character induced by the character
e(\frac{x}{p^{n}})
on
\mathbf{Z}
, and let
\psi_{n}:=\eta\circ\pi_{n}
, thus
\psi_{n}
is an additive character on
\mathbf{Z}_{(p)}
. Note that
\ker\psi_{n}=\{x\in\mathbf{Z}_{(p)}:v_{p}(x)\geqslant n\}
.

Lemma A.2
.

Let
p
be a prime. Let
m,n
be positive integers with
m\leqslant n
, and suppose that
p^{m}\neq 2
. Let
N
be any integer such that

m(N+1)-\lfloor\log_{p}N\rfloor\geqslant n.

(A.3)

For each
a\in\mathbf{Z}
, consider the map
\phi_{a}:1+p^{m}\mathbf{Z}\rightarrow\mathbf{C}^{\times}
defined by

\phi_{a}(1+p^{m}t):=\psi_{n}(aL_{N}(p^{m}t)).

(A.4)

Then

\phi_{a}((1+p^{m}t)(1+p^{m}t^{\prime}))=\phi_{a}(1+p^{m}t)\phi_{a}(1+p^{m}t^{\prime})

(A.5)

and

\phi_{a}(1+p^{m}t)=\phi_{a}(1+p^{m}t^{\prime})\quad\mbox{if}\quad t\equiv t^{\prime}(\operatorname{mod}\,p^{n-m}).

(A.6)

Finally, the maps
\phi_{a}
and
\phi_{a^{\prime}}
coincide if and only if
a\equiv a^{\prime}(\operatorname{mod}\,p^{n-m})
.

Proof.

We start with (
A.5
). Substituting in the definitions and using the homomorphism property of
\psi_{n}
, we must show that

\psi_{n}\big(a(L_{N}(p^{m}t+p^{m}t^{\prime}+p^{2m}tt^{\prime})-L_{N}(p^{m}t)-L_{N}(p^{m}t^{\prime}))\big)=1,

to which end it is enough (and in fact, if we want a result valid for all
a
, necessary and sufficient) to show that

v_{p}(L_{N}(p^{m}t+p^{m}t^{\prime}+p^{2m}tt^{\prime})-L_{N}(p^{m}t)-L_{N}(p^{m}t^{\prime}))\geqslant n.

By Lemma
A.1
with
x=p^{m}t
,
y=p^{m}t^{\prime}
, it suffices to show that
v_{p}(c_{ij}(p^{m}t)^{i}(p^{m}t^{\prime})^{j})\geqslant n
for every pair
i,j
featuring in that lemma. Note that
i+j\geqslant N+1
for all these pairs, and also that
v_{p}(c_{ij})\geqslant-\lfloor\log_{p}N\rfloor
since
c_{ij}
has denominator at most
N
. The required statement therefore follows from the assumption (
A.3
).

We turn now to (
A.6
). Here, it is enough to show that

v_{p}\big(L_{N}(p^{m}t)-L_{N}(p^{m}t^{\prime})\big)\geqslant n

when
t\equiv t^{\prime}(\operatorname{mod}\,p^{n-m})
.
Recalling the definition (
A.1
) of
L_{N}
, it is enough to proceed termwise and show that

v_{p}\bigg(\frac{(p^{m}t)^{i}}{i}-\frac{(p^{m}t^{\prime})^{i}}{i}\bigg)\geqslant n

(A.7)

for
i=1,2,\dots
.
By factoring
t^{i}-(t^{\prime})^{i}=(t-t^{\prime})(t^{i-1}+\dots)
we have
v_{p}(t^{i}-(t^{\prime})^{i})\geqslant v_{p}(t-t^{\prime})\geqslant n-m
, and so to establish (
A.7
) it is sufficient to show
im+n-m-v_{p}(i)\geqslant n
, that is to say
im\geqslant m+v_{p}(i)
. This is a true statement: it follows from
v_{p}(i)\leqslant i-1
and
(i-1)(m-1)\geqslant 0
. This completes the proof of (
A.6
).

Finally, we turn to the last statement. Suppose that
\phi_{a}
and
\phi_{a^{\prime}}
are the same; then
\phi_{a-a^{\prime}}
is trivial. In particular,
\phi_{a-a^{\prime}}(1+p^{m})=1
, which means that
v_{p}((a-a^{\prime})L_{N}(p^{m}))\geqslant n
. It is therefore enough to show that
v_{p}(L_{N}(p^{m}))=m
. From the definition (
A.1
) we have
L_{N}(p^{m})=p^{m}-\sum_{i=2}^{N}(-1)^{i}\frac{p^{im}}{i}
. Since the
p
-adic valuation of the first term is
m
, it is enough to show that all subsequent terms have strictly greater valuation, that is to say

im-v_{p}(i)\geqslant m+1

(A.8)

for all
i\geqslant 2
. If
p
is odd, we have
v_{p}(i)\leqslant i-2
and the inequality (
A.8
) follows from
(i-1)(m-1)\geqslant 0
, which is of course true for all
i\geqslant 2
and
m\geqslant 1
. If
p=2
, the same applies unless
i=2
. In that case, it may be checked directly that (
A.8
) holds unless
m=1
.
∎

Corollary A.3
.

Let
p
be a prime. Let
m,n
be positive integers with
m\leqslant n
, and suppose that
p^{m}\neq 2
. Let
N
be any integer such that (
A.3
) is satisfied. Let
\chi
be a Dirichlet character
(\mbox{{mod}}\,p^{n})
. Then there is some
a
such that
\chi=\phi_{a}
on
1+p^{m}\mathbf{Z}
.

Proof.

\chi
restricts to give a character on
\Gamma_{m,n}(p):=(1+p^{m}\mathbf{Z})/p^{n}\mathbf{Z}
, which is a group of size
p^{n-m}
. By (
A.5
), (
A.6
), every
\phi_{a}
also restricts to such a character. Since the number of distinct
\phi_{a}
is
p^{n-m}
, every character on
\Gamma_{m,n}(p)
arises in this way. The result follows.
∎

Now consider (
A.2
) again. Since
v_{p}(i)\leqslant i-1
for all primes
p
and all
i\geqslant 1
, we have
L_{N}(px)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}a_{i}x^{i}
with
v_{p}(a_{i})\geqslant 1
, and so
L_{N}(p^{m}x)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}a^{\prime}_{i}x^{i}
with
v_{p}(a^{\prime}_{i})\geqslant m
. From the definition (
A.4
) and the fact that
\psi_{n}
is an additive character, we see that
\phi_{a}(1+p^{m}t)=\psi_{n}(\sum_{i=1}^{N}aa^{\prime}_{i}t^{i})
. Let
b_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,N
, be integers such that
\pi_{n}(aa^{\prime}_{i})=\pi_{n}(b_{i}p^{m})
. Then we see that
\phi_{a}(1+p^{m}t)=e(\frac{f_{a}(t)}{p^{n-m}})
, where
f_{a}(t):=\sum_{i=1}^{n}b_{i}t^{i}
.

Combining these observations with Corollary
A.3
, which is the result we call the
Postnikov character formula
in this paper.

Proposition A.4
.

Let
p
be a prime. Let
m,n
be positive integers with
m\leqslant n
, and suppose that
p^{m}\neq 2
. Let
\chi
be a Dirichlet character with conductor
p^{n}
. Then there is a polynomial
f\in\mathbf{Z}[x]
such that
\chi(1+p^{m}x)=e(\frac{f(x)}{p^{n-m}})
, and such that
f
has no constant term and has degree at most
N
, where
N
is the smallest positive integer satisfying
m(N+1)-\lfloor\log_{p}N\rfloor\geqslant n
.

Finally, we remark on the particular regime of interest in the main body of the paper, specifically in Section
11
, which is where
m>\frac{1}{3}n
. Thus
3m\geqslant n+1
, and we see that
N
can be taken to be
2
. We must also ensure that
p^{m}\neq 2
, which will always be the case unless
\operatorname{cond}(\chi)=p^{n}\in\{2,4\}
.

Appendix B
Various estimates for arithmetic functions

In this appendix we collect various estimates, mainly for sums involving divisor functions, which are of fairly standard type. Throughout,
B\geqslant 0
is some real parameter.

Lemma B.1
.

We have

\sum_{n\leqslant X}\tau(n)^{B}\ll_{B}X(\log X)^{O_{B}(1)}.

Proof.

This is standard; see for instance
[
12
, equation (1.80)]
.∎

Lemma B.2
.

Let
h\in\mathbf{N}
. We have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\leqslant X\\
\mu^{2}(n)=1\end{subarray}}\frac{\tau(n)^{B}(n,h)}{n}\ll_{B}\tau(h)^{B+1}\log^{2^{B}}X

Proof.

We use “Rankin’s trick”, noting that

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\leqslant X\\
\mu^{2}(n)=1\end{subarray}}\frac{\tau(n)^{B}(n,h)}{n}

\displaystyle\ll\sum_{n}\mu^{2}(n)\tau(n)^{B}(n,h)n^{-1-1/\log X}

\displaystyle=\prod_{p|h}\big(1+2^{B}p^{-1/\log X}\big)\prod_{p\nmid h}\big(1+2^{B}p^{-1-1/\log X}\big)

\displaystyle\ll\tau(h)^{B+1}\prod_{p}\big(1+2^{B}p^{-1-1/\log X}\big).

Here, for the first term we used the crude inequality
1+2^{B}p^{-1/\log X}<2^{B+1}
. By Taylor expansion we have, for some
C=C(B)
,
(1+2^{B}x)(1-x)^{2^{B}}\leqslant 1+Cx^{2}
for all
x\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
. Applying this to the product over primes above, and noting that

\prod_{p}(1-p^{-1-1/\log X})^{-1}=\zeta(1+\frac{1}{\log X})=(1+o(1))\log X

and
\prod_{p}(1+Cp^{-2})=O_{B}(1)
, the result follows.
∎

Lemma B.3
.

Let
h\in\mathbf{N}
. We have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\geqslant X\\
\mu^{2}(n)=1\end{subarray}}\frac{(n,h)\tau(n)^{B}\log^{B}n}{n^{2}}\ll_{\varepsilon,B}h^{\varepsilon}X^{\varepsilon-1}.

Proof.

By Lemma
B.2
we have

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}X\leqslant n<2X\\
\mu^{2}(n)=1\end{subarray}}\frac{(n,h)\tau(n)^{B}\log^{B}n}{n^{2}}\ll_{B,\varepsilon}\tau(h)^{B+1}X^{\varepsilon-1}.

Summing dyadically over
X
and applying the divisor bound to
\tau(h)
gives the result.
∎

Lemma B.4
.

Write
\omega_{R}(n)
for the number of distinct primes
p\leqslant R
for which
p|n
. Then

X^{-1}\sum_{n\leqslant X}4^{\omega_{R}(n)}\ll\log^{3}R.

Proof.

We have

2^{\omega_{R}(n)}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d|n\\
d|P_{R}\end{subarray}}1,

where
P_{R}
is the product of the primes less than or equal to
R
. Squaring, summing over
X
and interchanging the order of summation gives

\sum_{n\leqslant X}4^{\omega_{R}(n)}\leqslant\sum_{n\leqslant X}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d,e|n\\
d,e|P_{R}\end{subarray}}1=\sum_{d,e|P_{R}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\leqslant X\\
d,e|n\end{subarray}}1\leqslant X\sum_{d,e|P_{R}}\frac{1}{[d,e]}\leqslant X\!\!\!\sum_{d^{\prime},e^{\prime},u|P_{R}}\frac{1}{d^{\prime}e^{\prime}u},

where in the last step we wrote
d=ud^{\prime}
,
e=ue^{\prime}
with
u:=(d,e)
.
The expression on the right is

X\big(\sum_{d|P_{R}}\frac{1}{d}\big)^{3}=X\prod_{p\leqslant R}\big(1+\frac{1}{p}\big)^{3}\ll X\log^{3}R.

This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma B.5
.

We have

1_{(m,n)=\Delta}=\sum_{d:\Delta|d|n}\mu\big(\frac{d}{\Delta}\big)1_{m\equiv 0(\mbox{\scriptsize{mod}}\,d)}.

Proof.

Suppose first that the sum on the right is empty, that is to say there is no
d
with
\Delta\mid d\mid n
and
d\mid m
. Then we cannot have
(m,n)=\Delta
, since in that case
\Delta
itself is an example of such a
d
. This means that both the LHS and the RHS are zero.

Now suppose that the sum on the right is non-empty, that is to say there is some
d
with
\Delta\mid d\mid n
and
d\mid m
. In particular,
\Delta\mid(m,n)
. Write
m=\Delta m^{\prime}
,
n=\Delta n^{\prime}
, and substitute
d=\Delta d^{\prime}
in the sum. Then the RHS is

\sum_{d^{\prime}|n^{\prime}}\mu(d^{\prime})1_{d^{\prime}|m^{\prime}}=\sum_{d^{\prime}|(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})}\mu(d^{\prime})=1_{(m^{\prime},n^{\prime})=1}=1_{(m,n)=\Delta},

by the usual version of the Möbius inversion formula.
∎

Appendix C
Dirichlet characters

We need the following result about decomposing Dirichlet characters as products of local characters, which is well-known but not always covered in analytic number theory texts.

Lemma C.1
.

Suppose that
\chi
is a Dirichlet character to modulus
q=p_{1}^{e_{1}}\cdots p_{m}^{e_{m}}
. Then there are unique Dirichlet characters
\chi_{p_{i}}
to moduli
p_{i}^{e_{i}}
such that
\chi=\prod_{i=1}^{m}\chi_{p_{i}}
. Moreover
\chi
is primitive if and only if all of the
\chi_{p_{i}}
are, that is to say if and only if none of the
\chi_{p_{i}}
is the principal character
1_{(n,p_{i})=1}
.

Proof.

Consider the isomorphism
\pi:(\mathbf{Z}/q\mathbf{Z})^{\times}\rightarrow\prod_{i=1}^{m}(\mathbf{Z}/p_{i}^{e_{i}}\mathbf{Z})^{\times}
given by the Chinese remainder theorem. This induces a natural homomorphism
\pi_{*}:\prod_{i=1}^{m}\widehat{(\mathbf{Z}/p_{i}^{e_{i}}\mathbf{Z})^{\times}}\rightarrow\widehat{(\mathbf{Z}/q\mathbf{Z})^{\times}}
defined by

(\pi_{*}(\chi_{1},\dots,\chi_{m}))(x):=\chi_{1}((\pi(x))_{1})\cdots\chi_{m}((\pi(x)_{m}),

where
(\pi(x))_{i}
denotes the
i
th coordinate of
\pi(x)
or, more colloquially,
(\pi(x))_{i}=x(\operatorname{mod}\,p_{i}^{e_{i}})
. It is easy to see that
\pi_{*}
is injective. Therefore, since the cardinality of the domain and range of
\pi_{*}
are the same,
\pi_{*}
is an isomorphism. Lifting characters on
(\mathbf{Z}/q\mathbf{Z})^{\times}
and on the
(\mathbf{Z}/p_{i}^{e_{i}}\mathbf{Z})^{\times}
to Dirichlet characters on
\mathbf{Z}
, the first statement of the lemma follows.

Turning to the statement about primitivity, denote by
\widehat{(\mathbf{Z}/r\mathbf{Z})^{\times}_{\operatorname{prim}}}
the set of primitive characters mod
r
. Now observe that if for some
j
we have
\chi_{j}\notin\widehat{(\mathbf{Z}/p_{j}^{e_{j}}\mathbf{Z})^{\times}_{\operatorname{prim}}}
(i.e. it is principal) then
\pi_{*}(\chi_{1},\dots,\chi_{m})\notin\widehat{(\mathbf{Z}/q\mathbf{Z})^{\times}_{\operatorname{prim}}}
, since the latter is induced from a character modulo
\prod_{i\neq j}p_{i}^{e_{i}}
. It follows that
\pi_{*}\big(\prod_{i=1}^{m}\widehat{(\mathbf{Z}/p_{j}^{e_{j}}\mathbf{Z})^{\times}_{\operatorname{prim}}}\big)\supseteq\widehat{(\mathbf{Z}/q\mathbf{Z})^{\times}_{\operatorname{prim}}}
.
To show that the containment is in fact an equality, it is enough to show that
\phi^{*}(r):=|\widehat{(\mathbf{Z}/r\mathbf{Z})^{\times}_{\operatorname{prim}}}|
is a multiplicative function of
r
. This follows from the fact that
\phi=1\star\phi^{*}
and hence
\phi^{*}=\mu\star\phi
, as explained just after
[
12
, equation (3.7)]
(where a formula for
\phi^{*}(r)
is given).
∎

Appendix D
An exponential sum estimate

The following lemma is of a well-known type and (apart from constants) is special case of a classical and much more general result of Hua. For references and a detailed bibliography of results of this type, see
[
3
]
.

Lemma D.1
.

Suppose that
p
is a prime and that
\min_{i=1,2}v_{p}(a_{i})=n-r
,
r\geqslant 1
. Then if
p
is odd we have

\bigg|\sum_{x\in\mathbf{Z}/p^{n}\mathbf{Z}}e\big(\frac{a_{1}x+a_{2}x^{2}}{p^{n}}\big)\bigg|\leqslant p^{n-r/2}.

A similar bound holds when
p=2
, but with an extra factor of
2^{1/2}
.

Proof.

Suppose first that
p
is odd. The claim is trivial for
r=0
, so suppose
r\geqslant 1
. Write
\Sigma
for the sum. Then
|\Sigma|^{2}=\sum_{x,y\in\mathbf{Z}/p^{n}\mathbf{Z}}e(\frac{1}{p^{n}}(a_{1}(x-y)+a_{2}(x^{2}-y^{2})))
. Making the substitution
h:=x-y
,
k:=x+y
gives that this is
\sum_{h,k\in\mathbf{Z}/p^{n}\mathbf{Z}}e(\frac{1}{p^{r}}h(a^{\prime}_{1}+ka^{\prime}_{2}))
, where we have written
a_{1}=p^{n-r}a^{\prime}_{1}
,
a_{2}=p^{n-r}a^{\prime}_{2}
with at least one of
a^{\prime}_{1},a^{\prime}_{2}
coprime to
p
. Performing the sum over
h
, we see that this is
p^{2n-r}\#\{k\in\mathbf{Z}/p^{r}\mathbf{Z}:a^{\prime}_{1}+ka^{\prime}_{2}\equiv 0(\operatorname{mod}\,p^{r})\}
. If
(a^{\prime}_{2},p)=1
then there is a unique value of
k
here, so
|\Sigma|^{2}=p^{2n-r}
, which concludes the proof in this case. If
p|a^{\prime}_{2}
then
(a^{\prime}_{1},p)=1
, and there are no values of
k
, so
\Sigma=0
. This concludes the proof in the case
p
odd.

Now suppose
p=2
. The claim is trivial when
r=0
or
1
, so suppose
r\geqslant 2
. The preceding argument needs a small modification, because on making the substitution
h
and
k
do not range over all of
(\mathbf{Z}/2^{n}\mathbf{Z})^{2}
but rather over the set of pairs with
h+k\equiv 0(\operatorname{mod}\,2)
(twice each). That is,

|\Sigma|^{2}=2\sum_{h,k\in\mathbf{Z}/2^{n}\mathbf{Z}}1_{h+k\equiv 0(\mbox{\scriptsize mod}\,2)}e\big(\frac{1}{2^{r}}h(a^{\prime}_{1}+ka^{\prime}_{2})\big),

with the notation the same as before. Suppose first that
r\neq 1
. Expanding
2\cdot 1_{m\equiv 0(\mbox{\scriptsize mod}\,2)}
as
1+e(\frac{m}{2})
, we obtain

|\Sigma|^{2}=\sum_{h,k\in\mathbf{Z}/2^{n}\mathbf{Z}}e\big(\frac{1}{2^{r}}h(a^{\prime}_{1}+ka^{\prime}_{2})\big)+\sum_{h,k\in\mathbf{Z}/2^{n}\mathbf{Z}}e\big(\frac{1}{2^{r}}h(a^{\prime}_{1}+ka^{\prime}_{2}+2^{r-1})+\frac{1}{2}k\big).

The first term is
\leqslant 2^{2n-r}
, exactly as before. For the second term we again perform the sum over
h
, obtaining that this is

2^{2n-r}\sum_{k\in\mathbf{Z}/2^{r}\mathbf{Z}}1_{a^{\prime}_{1}+ka^{\prime}_{2}\equiv 2^{r-1}(\mbox{\scriptsize mod}\,2^{r})}e\big(\frac{k}{2}\big).

If
a^{\prime}_{2}
is odd then there is a unique value of
k
here, giving a contribution of
2^{2n-r}
. If
a^{\prime}_{2}
is even then
a^{\prime}_{1}
is odd, and there are no values of
k
.

Putting all this together gives an upper bound
|\Sigma|^{2}\leqslant 2^{2n-r+1}
, which is what we wanted to prove. ∎

Appendix E
An explicit estimate for the
\Gamma
-function

In this section we record an explicit estimate for the size of the
\Gamma
-function in the strip
\frac{1}{4}\leqslant\Re z\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
. An estimate such as this is necessary if one wishes to compute an explicit exponent in our main theorem.

Lemma E.1
.

Suppose that
\frac{1}{4}\leqslant x\leqslant\frac{1}{2}
. Then
|\Gamma(x+iy)|\leqslant 7e^{-\pi|y|/2}
.

Proof.

We may assume
y>0
; the case
y<0
follows from this by conjugation. We use the explicit version of Stirling’s formula from
[
22
, p.294]
, which is valid for
|\arg z|\leqslant\frac{\pi}{2}
:

\log\Gamma(z)=\big(z-\frac{1}{2}\big)\log z-z+\frac{1}{2}\log 2\pi+\frac{\theta(z)}{6|z|},

(E.1)

where
|\theta(z)|\leqslant 1
. Substituting
z=x+iy
, we have

\displaystyle\log|\Gamma(x+iy)|

\displaystyle=\Re\log\Gamma(x+iy)

\displaystyle=\big(x-\frac{1}{2}\big)\log\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}}-y\arg(x+iy)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-x+\frac{1}{2}\log 2\pi+\Re\frac{\theta(z)}{6|z|}

\displaystyle\leqslant\frac{1}{4}\log 4-y\arg(x+iy)-x+\frac{1}{2}\log 2\pi+\frac{2}{3}.

(E.2)

Here, we used the fact that
|z|\geqslant x\geqslant\frac{1}{4}
to bound the last term, and also the inequality
(x-\frac{1}{2})\log\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}}\leqslant\frac{1}{4}\log 4
, which follows from the fact that
\log\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}}\geqslant-\log 4
for
x\geqslant\frac{1}{4}
.

Now for
t\geqslant 0
we have the inequality

\tan^{-1}t\geqslant\frac{\pi}{2}-\frac{1}{t}.

(E.3)

To prove this, observe that
f(t):=\tan^{-1}t-\frac{\pi}{2}+\frac{1}{t}
tends to zero as
t\rightarrow\infty
, but
f^{\prime}(t)=\frac{1}{1+t^{2}}-\frac{1}{t^{2}}<0
for all
t
.

The inequality (
E.3
) implies that

\arg(x+iy)=\tan^{-1}\big(\frac{y}{x}\big)\geqslant\frac{\pi}{2}-\frac{x}{y}

for
x,y\geqslant 0
. Substituting into (
E.2
) gives

\log|\Gamma(x+iy)|\leqslant\frac{1}{4}\log 4-\frac{\pi}{2}y+\frac{1}{2}\log 2\pi+\frac{2}{3}<-\frac{\pi}{2}y+\log 7,

from which the lemma follows.
∎
